#2980. Office Properties (OPI) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Asset sales and a $425 million note deal are reshaping the trust's portfolio.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Office Properties Income Trust (OPI +20.18%) reported its third quarter results, highlighting a strategic transition toward a reduced, higher-quality asset base through a multi-phase property disposition program. Management reported the company is prioritizing cash flow maximization and debt reduction by divesting noncore properties and utilizing proceeds to address near-term maturities. The trust successfully refinanced its credit facility and term loan with $425 million in senior secured notes, extending its weighted average debt maturity. The company stated that the execution of its asset sale plan will lead to a portfolio with higher occupancy and increased concentration in Class A office buildings.

Operator: Good day, and welcome to the Office Properties Income Trust third quarter 2026 earnings conference call. All participants will be in listen-only mode. Should you need assistance, please signal a conference specialist by pressing the star key followed by zero. Please note, this event is being recorded. I would now like to hand the call over to Matt Murphy, Manager of Investor Relations. Please go ahead.

Matt Murphy: Good morning. Thank you for joining us today. With me on the call are OPI's President and Chief Executive Officer, Yael Duffy, and Chief Financial Officer and Treasurer, Brian Donley. In just a moment, they will provide details about OPI's business and performance for the third quarter of 2026. I would like to note that the recording and retransmission of today's conference call is prohibited without the prior written consent of the company. Also note that today's conference call contains forward-looking statements within the meaning of the Private Securities Litigation Reform Act of 1995 and other securities laws.

These forward-looking statements are based on OPI's beliefs and expectations as of today, Tuesday, November 10th, 2026, and actual results may differ materially from those that we project. The company undertakes no obligation to revise or publicly release the results of any revision to the forward-looking statements made in today's conference call. Additional information concerning factors that could cause those differences is contained in OPI's filings with the Securities and Exchange Commission, which can be accessed from the company's website. Investors are cautioned not to place undue reliance upon any forward-looking statements. In addition, as a result of OPI's recent restructuring and the application of fresh start accounting, financial statement amounts and operating metrics are not comparable to prior periods.

Please refer to our earnings materials and SEC filings for additional information regarding the impact of fresh start accounting on our reported results. We will also be discussing non-GAAP financial measures during this call, including normalized FFO and cash basis net operating income, or Cash Basis NOI. Reconciliations of these non-GAAP figures to net income are available in OPI's earnings presentation that we issued last night, which can be found on our website. I will now turn the call over to Yael.

Yael Duffy: Thank you, Matt, and good morning. For the third quarter, OPI reported normalized FFO of $20.5 million, or $0.93 per share. Same property Cash Basis NOI increased 14.8% year over-year to $51.5 million, which Brian will discuss in further detail later on this call. Turning to our portfolio leasing and disposition activity. As of September 30th, 2026, OPI's portfolio consisted of 109 wholly owned properties across 27 states and the District of Columbia, totaling approximately 15.6 million square feet. The portfolio was 80.7% leased, or 88.8% on a same property basis, with a weighted average remaining lease term of 6.2 years. Over 60% of OPI's annualized revenue comes from investment grade rated tenants and government leases.

During our earnings call last quarter, we outlined OPI's priorities, which included maximizing cash flow through leasing, occupancy growth, and proactive asset management, executing on asset sales and applying those proceeds towards liquidity and addressing debt maturities, and driving long-term value by maintaining strong tenant relationships and efficient property operations. OPI signed 13 leases totaling 400,000 sq ft with a weighted average remaining lease term of 8.7 years, its best leasing quarter since Q2 2025. Renewals made up 97% of that volume, which showed leasing costs and concessions down to just $2.22 per square foot per lease year, more than 40% below our prior four-quarter average.

Renewals avoid downtime and lost rent, which reflects continued progress on our third priority, fostering strong tenant relationships. Looking ahead, roughly 1.3 million square feet of leases, representing 11% of annualized revenue, is scheduled to expire between now and the end of 2027. We plan to address these expirations through leasing and asset sales. Of the 1.3 million square feet scheduled to roll, 290,000 sq ft is currently scheduled to vacate or downsize. After adjusting for planned property disposition, that figure declines to just 52,000 sq ft, representing less than 1% of annualized revenue. Today, our leasing pipeline sits at 1.6 million square feet, with 1.1 million square feet, or 70%, related to renewals.

Turning to our second priority, asset sales. In June, we identified 32 properties for sale, totaling approximately 3.7 million square feet and contributing $43 million of annualized revenue. Since then, OPI has sold 14 properties for approximately $156 million. Additionally, 15 properties are currently under purchase and sale agreements for an aggregate sales price of $134 million. The remaining three properties are under letter of intent with estimated proceeds of $22.5 million. Pricing has met or exceeded our expectations, and total gross proceeds for the 32 properties should exceed $300 million, up from our initial expectations of $275 million. The buyer profile for these assets has spanned owner-users, value-add investors, and redevelopment buyers.

As we continue to evaluate OPI's portfolio, upcoming debt repayments, and liquidity needs, we have identified an additional 26 properties for sale, totaling 3.5 million square feet, which contribute annualized revenue of approximately $87 million. 18 of these properties are collateral for our senior secured notes due December 2029, six are collateral for our senior secured notes due June 2031, and two are collateral for our senior secured notes due March 2029. We are in the early stages of bringing these properties to market and expect proceeds of approximately $360 million in 2027.

Once we sell the 18 properties securing the senior secured notes due December 2029, along with the four already held for sale, only four will remain in that collateral pool. In effect, we are selling down these assets to fully repay the December 2029 notes, which now have approximately $307 million of principal outstanding. Once we complete the sale of all 58 properties, OPI will own 64 properties totaling approximately 10 million square feet across 23 states and Washington, D.C. Based on our current projections, the retained portfolio will be 93% occupied, with more than 70% of its square footage in Class A buildings, compared to approximately 63% before we began selling assets.

OPI will also reduce its concentration in Washington, D.C. Pro forma for the planned asset sales, the D.C. Metro will represent approximately 17% of annualized revenue across 10 buildings, compared to 24% of annualized revenue across 24 buildings prior to the asset sales. As a result, revenues will be more evenly balanced across our three largest markets: Washington, D.C., Atlanta, and Chicago. Government lease tenants will remain an important part of the tenant base at approximately 30% of annualized revenue, but a larger share of that revenue will come from agencies outside the D.C. area. Once complete, OPI will own a smaller, higher-quality portfolio that will be better positioned to maximize cash flow.

In summary, we are delivering on the priorities we set last quarter. Renewals made up 97% of our leasing, asset sales are on track to exceed $300 million, and we are using those proceeds to repay debt and enhance liquidity. I will now turn the call over to Brian to discuss our financial results.

Brian Donley: Thank you, Yael, and good morning. For the third quarter, we reported Normalized FFO of $20.5 million, or $0.93 per share, and Adjusted EBITDAre of $59 million. Same property Cash Basis NOI increased 14.8% over the prior year to $51.5 million. This performance was driven by an increase in cash rents as a result of the company's leasing activity and the rent abatement periods ending for certain leases. OPI's annualized rental income as of September 30th was $390 million, which includes straight line rent adjustments as well as estimated expense reimbursements under OPI's leases.

The components of annualized rental income as of September 30th are broken down as follows: cash rents of $291 million, straight line rent adjustments of $20 million, and recurring expense reimbursements due under OPI's leases of $79 million. Property operating expenses for the quarter were $50.7 million, a decline of 2.7% compared to the prior year, largely due to our dispositions. G&A expenses were $4.9 million for the quarter. For the full year 2026, G&A is projected to be $19 million-$21 million. Our current estimated quarterly interest expense run rate, before any further debt repayments, is approximately $38 million, consisting of $36 million of cash interest expense and $2 million of non-cash amortization of financing costs and discounts.

During the quarter, recurring CapEx totaled $10.4 million, comprised of $8.2 million in lease-related costs and $2.2 million in building improvements. For the full year 2026, we expect CapEx to be approximately $50 million, comprised of $10 million of building capital and $40 million of leasing capital. Turning to the balance sheet, we ended the third quarter with approximately $61 million in unrestricted cash and $25 million in restricted cash, which represents operating reserves under our loan agreements. In September, we successfully raised $425 million of senior secured notes and used the proceeds to fully repay and terminate our $325 million credit facility and $100 million term loan due in January 2027.

The new notes are secured by the same 19-property collateral pool that previously secured the credit facility. This transaction addressed our near-term refinancing priorities and extended our weighted average maturities. As a result, we currently have no meaningful debt maturities until 2029. Additionally, since the end of the quarter, we repaid approximately $78 million of our 8.375% senior secured notes due 2029, which included $58 million in connection with collateral asset sales and $20 million of mandatory principal payments. The next scheduled principal payment is due on February 1st, 2027, for $30 million. We expect to make this payment using cash on hand and proceeds from asset sales.

As Yael Duffy discussed, we are moving forward with the sale of 18 of the 22 properties serving as collateral for these notes and intend to fully redeem the outstanding principal upon completion of these sales. Earlier this month, we also redeemed $32 million of our 10% senior secured notes due June 2031, using a portion of proceeds from collateral asset sales. We look forward to providing updates about our progress on future calls. Operator, that concludes our call.

Operator: The conference has now concluded. Thank you for attending today's presentation, and you may now disconnect.

#2977. Tech Sell-Off: 3 Stocks Most Vulnerable and 3 Built to Hold Up

The line between the stocks in trouble and the ones holding up may come down to one question: Who is paying for the data centers?

The Nasdaq Composite index has taken a beating over the past month, falling roughly 8.5% from its Oct. 6 record even after Monday's rebound. And some of the biggest names in artificial intelligence (AI) have suffered considerably larger losses.

Investors have plenty to worry about. Questions about AI spending, higher interest rates, rising oil prices tied to the Iran war, and the Democratic sweep in the midterm elections have all contributed to the sell-off.

But not every beaten-down technology stock faces the same risks. Some companies have serious financial challenges that could become more difficult in this environment. Others have the earnings, competitive advantages, and financial resources to weather the downturn.

Here are three stocks that look particularly vulnerable, followed by three that appear better equipped to hold up.

Nebius Group (NBIS -5.27%) has been one of the biggest casualties of the tech sell-off, with its shares falling 35.6% over the past 30 days.

The company is building AI infrastructure and renting out computing capacity to customers, putting it at the center of the enormous spending boom. But building data centers requires substantial up-front investments, and Nebius doesn't have the established earnings base of larger technology companies.

That creates a problem for investors concerned about returns on AI infrastructure investments. Nebius needs to keep expanding capacity to meet demand, but financing that expansion becomes more difficult when borrowing costs rise, and investors become less willing to fund aggressive growth.

Its business also faces potential regulatory complications following the midterm elections, particularly if governments impose new restrictions on data center development.

Nebius has substantial growth opportunities, but its capital requirements make it particularly exposed to a prolonged downturn.

Intel (INTC -2.09%) is in even worse shape than Nebius right now, as the stock is down 44.6% over the past month.

Intel has been spending heavily to rebuild its manufacturing capabilities while competing with Advanced Micro Devices in processors and trying to establish itself as a major contract chip manufacturer. That's an expensive strategy, particularly when its core business is struggling.

The company's disappointing fourth-quarter outlook sent shares plunging 28.6% on Oct. 30 -- the stock's worst one-day performance since 1974.

Intel needs more demand and improved profitability to justify its investment plans. Higher interest rates and investors' growing reluctance to fund expensive technology projects only complicate that effort.

Oracle (ORCL -1.51%) is down 11.8% over the past month, a relatively modest loss compared with Nebius and Intel. But its exposure to the AI infrastructure spending boom deserves attention.

Oracle is spending heavily to expand its cloud infrastructure business, including data centers designed to support AI workloads. That could generate significant revenue over time, but the up-front investment weighs on Oracle stock.

The company's growing reliance on debt financing adds another complication. Higher interest rates make financing more expensive, while concerns about the profitability of AI infrastructure projects could pressure Oracle's valuation.

Nvidia (NVDA -5.47%) has fallen 22.5% over the past month, but the sell-off hasn't changed its position as the dominant supplier of AI accelerators.

The distinction matters because Nvidia sells the chips that power the data centers companies such as Oracle and Nebius are building. It doesn't have to finance those facilities itself.

Nvidia also benefits from its CUDA software platform, which makes it difficult for customers to switch to competing hardware without additional development work.

But there's a more immediate problem. The company's board announced on Sunday that it has opened an investigation into reports that CEO Jensen Huang had a relationship with a business executive allegedly connected to Chinese intelligence. Huang has acknowledged the relationship and apologized to his wife and family, but the issue raises questions about the company and sent the stock price down on Monday.

I think the issue is unfortunate and a distraction, but I don't see it changing the long-term narrative around Nvidia and its role as the leading supplier of AI processors.

Palantir Technologies (PLTR -0.23%) stock dipped 14.8% over the last month, despite the company reporting outstanding third-quarter results. Revenue was up 96% from a year ago, and U.S. commercial revenue jumped 148%. The company's rapid growth in bringing on new commercial customers suggests that businesses are finding practical uses for AI rather than merely experimenting with the technology.

Here's the other thing I really like about Palantir: Its software business doesn't require building enormous data centers to generate additional revenue.

The biggest concern is valuation. Palantir has commanded a premium stock price, and its forward price-to-sales ratio remains close to 50. Even outstanding earnings growth doesn't guarantee investors will continue to pay such a high multiple.

Micron Technology (MU -1.10%) stock has fallen 29.3% over the past month, making it one of the hardest-hit semiconductor stocks.

Micron makes high bandwidth memory (HBM) essential for AI accelerators, as well as DRAM and NAND memory that is used throughout the technology industry. Its latest quarterly results demonstrated how dramatically AI demand has improved its business.

For its fiscal fourth quarter, ended Sept. 3, Micron's revenue was $54.23 billion, a huge increase from $11.32 billion a year ago. Its operating cash flow was $43.97 billion.

Today's AI infrastructure requires increasing amounts of memory, particularly HBM. Micron is selling a critical component rather than betting primarily on the future profitability of data centers. That gives it a stronger position than companies whose expansion depends heavily on outside financing.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 852%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 208% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

#2972. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2969. Federal Reserve Board requests public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call (202) 452-2955.

For release at 4:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation and the Federal Reserve Board today published feedback letters for resolution plans submitted in October 2025. The agencies did not identify any shortcomings or deficiencies in these resolution plan submissions.

Resolution plans, also known as living wills, describe a banking organization's strategy for orderly resolution in the event of material financial distress or failure. The agencies conducted a joint review of the 2025 resolution plan submissions from 15 banking organizations with over $250 billion in assets.

In addition, the agencies determined that the shortcoming previously identified in the 2021 BNP Paribas resolution plan has been satisfactorily addressed.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

#2952. Coinbase net income surges 66% in third quarter, benefiting from bitcoin investment gains

Coinbase Global earned third-quarter adjusted net income of $719 million, up 66% from $433 million a year ago, largely due to a recovery in the cryptocurrency market.

Coinbase said it earned $2.51 a share in the latest quarter, although it wasn't immediately clear if that compared with the 6-cent-per-share profit that Wall Street analysts had estimated. Revenue of $1.20 billion trailed the $1.233 billion expected by analysts polled by LSEG.

Net income at the San Francisco-based platform surged thanks to the value of its volatile cryptocurrency investment portfolio, which can swing wildly even when the composition of the underlying holdings are little changed.

The third quarter value of Coinbase's crypto investment portfolio climbed due to a resurgence in the digital asset market. The price of bitcoin — often used as a gauge for all crypto assets — rose 39% in the three-month period ended Sept. 30 and the flagship cryptocurrency recently changed hands at roughly $80,809.

In the third quarter, Coinbase deepened its push into non-crypto products and services, including artificial intelligence integrations for traders, tokenized real-world assets and prediction markets.

In July, VP of product Max Branzburg told CNBC that Coinbase would debut an "everything exchange" spotlighting tokenized stocks and other real-world assets, derivatives trading, prediction markets and early-stage token sales. The trading platform also introduced Coinbase for Agents in early October, supporting users that want to use ChatGPT, Muse or other AI agents to manage their portfolios or research and trade various assets.

Coinbase also expanded its prediction market offerings, making a wider range of event contracts available to users.

In the third quarter, prediction markets revenue grew to more than $200 million on an annual basis, while the number of traders on those markets increased 60% compared with the second quarter.

#2950. Anthropic's trillion-dollar IPO is coming, but retail investors could have owned AI stock much earlier

Anthropic is set to go public next week, with a potential date of Nov. 19 for an initial public offering that could value the AI giant on a fully diluted basis at over $1.4 trillion. For many retail investors, the IPO may be the first chance they have to own Anthropic and even that ownership event will be out of reach with limited shares allocated to retail brokerage firms. But there were ways for retail investors to own Anthropic shares at a much earlier date. Investors may also be able to get in on the next hot IPO well before listing day.

There are several ways to do this, though it can depend on whether you're an accredited investor under SEC guidelines when investing directly in a private company versus accessing a privately held stock through a diversified fund. Generally, this means being able to prove you have $200,000 in individual income, or $300,000 jointly, or $1 million in net worth, not counting an investor's home. The SEC has proposed expanding accredited investor eligibility beyond traditional income and net worth thresholds, through an exam-based route, but that's not currently an option.

Here's what investors looking for some pre-IPO action need to know:

Some mutual funds and ETFs invest in private companies before they are public. "It's not like buying the IPO directly, so you have to be comfortable with the portfolio," said Chester Spatt, professor of finance at Carnegie Mellon University's Tepper School of Business.

That means reviewing all of the portfolio holdings of a fund and its investment approach, not just the fact that it has a stake in the one company that has yet to go public. Even so, mutual funds and ETFs can be a way to gain some exposure to the pre-IPO world, with SpaceX, Anthropic and its rival OpenAI all recent examples.

For example, Fidelity Contrafund, a large-cap growth fund, says in its prospectus that the adviser may invest the fund's assets in securities of private or newly public companies. It was an early investor in SpaceX, for example, buying shares in 2015 when the company was valued at about $10 billion. SpaceX represented about 4.4% of the fund's holdings at the end of September.

Billionaire mutual fund manager Ron Baron's Partners Fund, which seeks long-term capital appreciation, also invested in SpaceX. At the end of October, SpaceX was the fund's No. 1 holding, accounting for 28% of its assets.

Before buying a fund that invests in private companies, be sure to consider redemption and fees, Spatt said. For example, The Private Shares Fund, which seeks capital appreciation by focusing on investments in late-stage, venture-backed private companies, allows quarterly tenders for up to 5% of the fund's outstanding shares at NAV. The management fee is 1.9%, higher than many index funds.

Several ETFs list Anthropic among their holdings, including the Tema Photonics & Optical ETF (LAZR), Tema Electrification ETF (VOLT),  Alger Concentrated Equity ETF (CNEQ), Alger AI Enablers & Adopters ETF (ALAI), Alger 35 ETF (ATFV), KraneShares Public-Private AI & Technology ETF (AGIX), and iShares A.I. Innovation and Tech Active ETF (BAI).

Several big mutual fund and ETF companies, similar to the SpaceX shares, have reported ownership in Anthropic in the past, including T. Rowe Price, Capital Group and Fidelity Investments. Fifty Fidelity funds have stakes in Anthropic, according to Morningstar, at roughly $5 billion. Capital Group has an even greater stake across its funds, at $10 billion, according to Morningstar.

But there is a catch: not all the funds own shares outright, with some ETFs gaining exposure through what are called special purpose vehicles.

Fund managers and accredited investors can access late-stage start-ups through special purpose vehicles.

Tema's LAZR ETF, for example, lists Anthropic as its second-biggest holding, but it is through an SPV. Its VOLT fund has a much smaller holding in Anthropic.

Accredited investors can also access SPV-related investment opportunities directly, through an online platform like Caplight, Sydecar and Augment, said Emily Zheng, senior venture capital analyst at private investment research firm Pitchbook, or through their wealth manager. Keep in mind, however, you could be buying an SPV that owns the shares outright, or you could be invested in an SPV that has shares of an SPV that owns the shares, she said. There can also be multiple layers of fees.

"This structure may allow investors to participate at lower minimums than would typically be required for a direct share purchase, while the fund manager handles the complexity of acquisition, ongoing administration, and tax reporting on behalf of investors," according to private wealth and alternative investments manager TSG Invest, which invests in pre-IPO shares. It says SPVs are becoming the dominant structure in pre-IPO investing, now accounting for roughly 40% to 50% of all secondary market transactions.

Accredited investors who want access to shares of private companies that may not be imminently ready to IPO can buy through online private stock trading platforms such as Schwab's Forge Global, Morgan Stanley's EquityZen, Notice, Caplight Technologies and Clarity, previously known as Hiive.

Platforms have different private holdings available, and investment requirements can vary. There can also be multiple fees. For instance, at Forge, opening an account and using the marketplace to trade shares are free. However, buyers pay a one-time fee for both direct shares and fund offerings. Investors should also be aware that some companies may charge their own transfer or legal fees, according to a Forge spokesperson.

Several platforms require investment minimums. The standard minimum for direct secondary transactions at Forge, for example, is $100,000, though minimums may vary by investment type and offering. Certain Forge fund offerings may accept indications of interest starting at $5,000, but availability is limited and subject to the terms of the specific offering, the company says on its website.

Investors need to consider their time horizon and their liquidity needs before investing in private companies. Additionally, minimums at online platforms can be high, even for accredited investors, said Zheng. "But it is a lot lower than traditional venture investment," Zheng said.

Individual investors can get access to private companies through about two dozen publicly available closed-end funds focused on private equity or venture capital, according to Morningstar Direct.

Funds vary in their holdings between early-stage and late-stage companies. OpenAI, for instance, represents about 9.4% of the holdings of Robinhood Ventures Fund I. The fund's holdings also include 4.2% of Ramp, which could IPO at some point in the next year, and its largest holding is enterprise tech company Databricks.

Sofi, meanwhile, offers access to certain funds that may not be available through other brokerages. Private and venture funds on Sofi's platform include the ARK Venture Fund, which owns both OpenAI and Anthropic; and the Destiny Tech100 Closed End Fund, which also owns both AI companies.

Closed-end funds also offer exposure to other closely watched private names, such as the Cashmere Fund, which owns Kalshi. The Fundrise Innovation Fund has reported holdings in Anthropic, OpenAI, Databricks, Anduril and Ramp as of its year-end 2025 statement, but not more recently.

These funds can carry significantly higher fees than index funds or ETFs consisting of public company stocks. Robinhood Ventures Fund I, for example, has an expense ratio of 3.13%.

If a retail investor does want to get in on the IPO, some brokerage firms are able to offer Anthropic IPO access to clients, and you generally don't have to be an accredited investor to be eligible.

Anthropic, for example, said in a Nov. 2 regulatory filing that it was allocating some IPO shares to brokerage customers of Charles Schwab, Fidelity, Robinhood Financial (including its TradePMR division), SoFi, and Morgan Stanley's E-Trade.

SpaceX offered about 20% of its IPO shares to individual investors through these brokerages.

Individual brokerages determine the requirements for participation. SoFi, for example, requires investors to have a particular account type, but there are no account minimums. Fidelity allows access to brokerage customers with at least $25,000 in retail assets at the firm.

To request shares, eligible investors generally enter an indication of interest for the offering they are interested in. However, there's no guarantee investors will receive shares or how many. In many cases, investors may be disappointed with their allocation.

"We only get a limited number of shares for each IPO," Robinhood says on its website. "If customer demand is higher than our supply, we can't fulfill every request in full. How shares are distributed depends on the allocation method used for the offering."

#2943. House Oversight calls on DOJ to interview key Epstein witnesses, says govt. ‘failed’ survivors

The House Oversight and Government Reform Committee on Tuesday released a sharply critical report on the federal government's handling of investigations into Jeffrey Epstein and his accomplice, Ghislaine Maxwell.

"The federal government has failed the survivors of Jeffrey Epstein and Ghislaine Maxwell's horrific crimes for decades," Oversight Chair Rep. James Comer, R-Ky., said in a statement.

The report called for the Justice Department to interview or reinterview "key witnesses" with information about Epstein's sex trafficking of young women and girls, "further investigate allegations of criminal activity involving Epstein's associates, and provide survivors with unredacted access to their records."

The report also urged Congress to pass legislation sponsored by Comer that would allow survivors of sexual abuse to speak freely about it, even if they had signed confidentiality agreements.

In addition to criticizing the federal government, the committee blasted various financial institutions for their roles in dealing with Epstein, who killed himself in August 2019 in jail after being arrested on child sex trafficking charges.

"U.S. and foreign government authorities failed to identify indicators of human trafficking and immigration fraud," the committee said. "Institutions such as American Express enabled this activity, with company agents allowing Epstein to use fraudulent decoy flights and refundable or dummy itineraries to transport foreign women across borders directly to his residences."

"Financial institutions possessed substantial information about Epstein's criminal history and repeatedly identified transactions and activities presenting heightened compliance risks," the committee said. "Yet they failed to adequately address those risks, in some cases reporting them years later after Epstein's 2019 arrest, due to Epstein's personal connections, wealth, and influence."

The committee called on the Treasury Department and the Financial Crimes Enforcement Network to review whether banks and other financial institutions are meeting their obligations to report activity that could point to money laundering and human trafficking.

The committee has been investigating Epstein, Maxwell and their connections since last year.

#2937. $6.3 billion Binance hack is largest crypto theft on record

Binance said Friday that a security incident impacted about $6.3 billion in client assets, with around $3 billion frozen on-chain, marking the largest crypto hack on record.

The company later added it began recovering assets impacted by the breach after pledging to cover losses using its Secure Asset Fund for Users, which the company created in 2018 as a protection for clients in the event of a hack.

It wasn't clear how the attack took place. The world's largest crypto exchange initially said the hack may involve "a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions." However, Co-CEO Richard Teng later said the attack involved a more complex process rather than the misuse of a single engineer's permissions.

The FBI said Friday it was aware of the incident and was in touch with "relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate ​potential impacts, ​and ⁠coordinate appropriate investigative steps."

It wasn't clear either who was responsible for the breach. But TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and AI investigations company, pointed to North Korean hackers as the potential culprits. "Several features of this attack match earlier North Korea-linked heists, including the Bybit hack and the KelpDAO hack in April 2026: small Tornado Cash withdrawals funding an operator wallet, staging that began well before the theft, and signatures obtained in advance then executed in one burst," the company said in a post.

Blockchain data platform Chainalysis also said this appears "be the work of North Korean cybercrime group TraderTraitor. This group has repeatedly targeted the cryptocurrency industry, exchanges, DeFi protocols, and investors, to raise funds for the regime. We base our intelligence assessment on on-chain activity observed in the hours following the hack."

Binance temporarily suspended withdrawals while it overhauls its withdrawal security systems and has revoked the affected address authorizations, Teng said. The exchange is also working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the stolen funds, while deposits remain unaffected.

Binance co-founder Yi He said deposits remained unaffected and continued to operate normally, while the exchange was working on enhancements to its withdrawal service and related address controls. It expects to begin resuming withdrawal services within the next 48 hours.

"We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak. We will share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and the facts are fully verified," Binance said.

The announcement followed moves by several crypto firms, including Circle Internet Group, Tether and Ripple, to freeze funds tied to the hack.

Exploits are not uncommon in the crypto space. In 2025, Bybit suffered roughly $1.5 billion in losses due to a hack. More recently, Bitget lost more than $350 million worth of digital assets in a cyber theft incident. Over the past several years, bad actors have also drained billions of dollars in crypto from decentralized exchanges.

This week's breach "proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Michael Selig, chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said Friday in a CNBC "Squawk on the Street" interview. The U.S. derivatives regulator on Oct. 5 proposed a framework that would effectively put crypto exchanges under federal government oversight — after Congress failed to approve legislation to regulate the asset.

"The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States. We want to offer a federal option to these exchanges to come and register with the CFTC, and they would be subject to the same sorts of cyber security safeguards that we have in our markets today," he said.

Bitcoin fell more than 4% to $77,187.60 on Friday as the hack weighed on crypto markets.

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said Thursday evening in a social media post that the company had frozen more than 800 million in USD Coin associated with the incident. USDT stablecoin issuer Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also said in on X that his firm had frozen more than 1.5 billion of its stablecoin that may have been related to a potential breach.

Jack McDonald, Ripple's senior vice president of stablecoins, said it was actively freezing affected funds held in RLUSD, its U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin.

#2932. Anthropic sets IPO price range of $70 to $78, looks to start trading Nov. 19, sources say

Anthropic said it will offer 825 million shares in its upcoming IPO at a price range between $70 and $78 per share, according to an updated filing on Tuesday, bringing the 5-year-old artificial intelligence lab a step closer to a potentially historic Nasdaq debut.

The company is planning to price on Nov. 18 and trade on Nov. 19, according to people familiar with the details who were granted anonymity because they were not authorized to speak publicly. Anthropic is targeting retail allocation of about 20%, the people said, a much higher percentage than the typical initial public offering, which is 5% to 10% for retail investors.

At the high end of the range, Anthropic would net up to $64.35 billion in proceeds through the IPO. The company could end up being worth up to $1.405 trillion on a fully diluted basis, rising from the $965 billion valuation it announced as part of a funding round in May.

Anthropic said it has entered into an agreement with Advanced Micro Devices, Broadcom, Google, Micron, Microsoft, Nvidia and Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co. to purchase $26 billion of the company's Class N common stock in a private placement at a price per share equal to the IPO price. The shares sold in the private placement will not be registered in the offering and will not be subject to lock-up agreements with the underwriters, the company said.

As Anthropic executives get set to pitch the offering to investors, they have to contend with a tumultuous political environment, following last week's midterm elections that included a Democratic sweep in Congress and the passage of numerous statewide ballot measures that could slow the AI buildout. They're also facing a public market that's grown increasingly skeptical of the AI trade on concern that rising interest rates will make debt unaffordable for companies that need it for costly infrastructure purchases.

CNBC reported last week that some Anthropic investors were bracing for a lower valuation than had previously been expected. While Anthropic had been looking toward a $2 trillion valuation until recently, shareholders told CNBC that even $1.5 trillion is now in doubt, with one investor calling that number a "stretch."

Should Anthropic reach the Nasdaq this month as planned, under the ticker symbol "ANT," the debut would come roughly five months after SpaceX went public in a record offering that raised $85.7 billion. Elon Musk's reusable rocket and AI company has a market cap of just under $2 trillion, around its valuation at the time of the IPO.

Institutional demand for Anthropic is even stronger than it was for SpaceX at this point in the process, the people said. The company's book is already covered at the $70 to $78 per share range on an allocable basis, they said.

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei co-founded the AI lab in 2021 after he and a small group of researchers defected from OpenAI, now its chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, and the company primarily sells its products and services to other businesses.

On Monday, Anthropic introduced two new AI models, Claude Fable 5.2 and Claude Mythos 5.2, the first time the company has released so-called frontier models since Amodei called to slow down the pace of AI development in September. Anthropic said that the models went through rigorous safety and alignment testing, and that the company developed new evaluations and post-launch monitoring tools ahead of the release.

For the nine months ended Sept. 30, Anthropic said it generated $33.24 billion in revenue up roughly 1,200% from the same period last year. It reported a $104 billion net loss during the nine-month period, though most of that was due to the change in fair value of convertible notes and derivative liabilities. Anthropic lost $25.1 billion over the same stretch in 2025, the filing said.

Anthropic said in its amended filing Tuesday that its run-rate revenue was roughly $81 billion at the end of October, up $10 billion from the previous month.

"Our aim is to help humankind chart a safe course through this technological evolution, open new frontiers of discovery, and bring forth an age of miracles," Anthropic's co-founders wrote in the prospectus. "Everyone who owns our stock, whether an institution or an individual, becomes part of that effort. We hope you will share our ambition to see it though."

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2925. Alphabet shares appear to have bottomed. How to play it for a quick rebound

Ten days after Alphabet shares experienced their worst one-day drubbing in its history, the search, media and AI giant has barely retraced a faction of its nearly $500 billion in lost market capitalization, having rallied just a paltry 1.5%. The muted recovery stands out for a company that posted its 13th straight quarter of double-digit revenue growth in the same report, with Google Cloud revenue more than doubling. Alphabet's earnings were the first major crack to appear in the AI trade and foreshadowed a series of volatile events for the complex, including the election and the growing scandal enveloping Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang.

Still, a bullish case for Alphabet rests not in its post-earnings concerns being unfounded, but rather on its growth being strong enough to withstand them. On Monday, Google launched two new systems built on its custom AI chip, the TPU, a sign the company keeps evolving, and the debate over AI and search is far from settled. The sheer size of the drop, a record one-day loss after a quarter of 25% revenue growth, may also leave room for a reversion toward pre-earnings levels. For investors who agree, options offer a variety of ways to express that view.

Based on Monday's close, I'm buying the December 18 285/300 call spread for a debit of roughly $7.07.

Alphabet's 30-day implied volatility closed Monday around 35%, higher than about 44% of days over the past year. That is mid-range, so either buying or selling premium can be viable for this trade. A bull call spread is the higher-conviction route. It needs the stock to rally to pay off, but it offers a larger payout if the view is right. Selling a put spread is the lower-conviction route. It can carry a higher probability of profit, but with a smaller reward relative to the risk.

Due to the sheer size of the drop and the weak recovery thus far, I'm choosing the higher-conviction call spread.

Alphabet has recovered only a small part of its post-earnings loss, still down 11.3% from its pre-earnings close of $324.89. The trade expires Dec. 18 and assumes that gap at least partly closes at some point over the next 39 days, not necessarily right away.

The long 285 call sits just in the money, and the short 300 call sits about halfway back from the post-earnings low to the pre-earnings close. This gives the trade a breakeven of $292.07, 1.4% above Monday's close and still about 10% below pre-earnings levels.

The trade has a theoretical probability of profit of 47% and reaches its maximum profit of $793 per spread if Alphabet closes at or above $300 on Dec. 18. The maximum loss of $707 comes if shares close at or below $285. If Alphabet closes between $285 and $300 on Dec. 18, the long call can be exercised, leaving investors holding 100 shares per spread. Investors can close the spread before expiration to capture profits, cut losses, or avoid taking on shares.

This is a directional bet on a continued rebound, and the main risk is that the recovery stalls, even for a historically strong company. Nvidia's results on Nov. 17 could set the tone for the AI trade, and rates, energy prices, and scrutiny of data center construction remain headwinds to watch.

#2924. Poll: Do you find the Brown-Tatum drama narrative interesting?

Tonight marks the first regular season game between the Philadelphia 76ers and the Boston Celtics since the offseason trade sending Jaylen Brown to Philadelphia took place. Understandably, we're receiving a fresh wave of reports and rehashing of events surrounding the purported fallout between Brown and Celtics star Jayson Tatum. I feel compelled to ask, do you find this narrative interesting?

I'll start here: I totally understand why this is a thing. The NBA has become a 24-hour, 365-day entertainment vehicle that many of us consume obsessively. That reality is driven as much by drama between personalities, both real and manufactured, as by what actually takes place on the court. Brown and Tatum played together for nine seasons, their entire careers overlapping for all but one year, for a club perennially in contention which won one NBA title. If there was a fallout between them, and there seems like there's definitely at least something there, that's a story. If Brown had a disagreement with how he was treated by Celtics management, that's a topic worth exploring. We've hit on those points on this very website (thanks for reading, tell your friends!). If you have lamb chops baking in the oven today and popcorn ready for your viewing experience tonight, cool. I get it.

Still, I can't find myself really caring about it. Whatever happened, it all happened behind the scenes. There was no public infighting or drawn-out trade demand. The Jaylen Brown trade came completely out of left field like a flash of lightning. What Brown said the other day almost exactly aligns with my thinking on the matter:

In today's NBA, guys change teams all the time. Plenty of breakups have been a lot messier than this one, including in Philadelphia. I'll chuckle when someone makes a clever joke about everything, but beyond that, I can't find myself getting invested in the whole thing. It's Philadelphia vs. Boston. That's more than enough for me.

How about you? Are you still finding this drama interesting or is it entirely in the rear view mirror for you as well? Vote and let us know how you feel in the comments.

#2920. Amazon's Ring unveils flying drone and robot to patrol homes and businesses

Amazon's Ring is going deeper into surveillance with an indoor drone for home monitoring and an autonomous robot designed to patrol businesses.

The company on Tuesday unveiled the $250 Ring Drone Cam, a security camera that can fly from room to room, including upstairs, as well as capture footage during the day or at night. When an alarm or sensor on windows, doors and home security devices is triggered, the textbook-sized drone travels a preset path to investigate suspicious activity. Users can also deploy the drone at any time, the company said.

At a demo in New York on Monday, Ring CEO Jamie Siminoff said the drone could be used as a home monitoring tool and as a crime "deterrent" due to the device's built-in LED lights.

"If someone is going around your house at night, and your motion detector goes off on your Ring doorbell, imagine if you see spotlights and something flying across the window," Siminoff said. "You're probably not going to go into that house."

The drone, which can fly up to eight minutes at a time before autonomously returning to its charging dock, has two plastic-encased propellers that emit a loud buzz when it's airborne.

Ring devices have provided Amazon another avenue to grow its presence in the smart home beyond its Echo smart speakers and Eero routers, expanding in a market that Grand View Research estimates will top $200 billion in sales this year. It's equally fertile ground for Amazon rivals Apple and Google.

Ring first previewed its drone, then called the Always Home Cam, in 2020. Siminoff said he chose to hold off on releasing the device until Amazon could perfect the design, sensors and data processing.

The company on Tuesday also debuted the $1,000 Ring Rover Cam. The device repurposes Amazon's Astro home robot, which had a short life as a business device and has seen few upgrades since its 2021 debut, for companies to use to patrol offices, warehouses and other workspaces.

Ring said it "leveraged the best of Astro" to build the Ring Rover Cam, which it describes as a "purpose-built security robot."

Similar to the Astro, the device is a display mounted on a two-wheeled base with an extendable periscope camera. It also features a "built-in siren," two-way audio and a "light tower" that shines onto the ceiling to help users keep track of its whereabouts.

Siminoff said in an interview that the drone and the robot allow users to monitor multiple areas of their home or business with just one device.

"People like indoor cameras, but they don't necessarily want them everywhere around their home," Siminoff said.

Siminoff said the drone can't fly outside per Federal Aviation Administration regulations, which restrict operating drones unless they're within a line of sight of a pilot.

"We have told our customers not to do that because it's illegal," Siminoff said. "We've also put guardrails in there to make it so it doesn't fly outside."

The Ring Drone Cam ships in the U.S. on Jan. 27, pending approval from the Federal Communications Commission, while the Ring Rover Cam will begin shipping to users on Dec. 16, the company said.

#2917. NBA analyst suggests Warriors use Kristaps Porziņģis to trade for Magic center

NBA analyst suggests Warriors use Kristaps Porziņģis to trade for Magic center originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

The Warriors currently are on a historic win streak, but one NBA analyst believes they need to make a move to shore up their interior.

On Monday's episode of "The Kevin O'Connor Show," Yahoo Sports' Kevin O'Connor told guest Nekias Duncan why Golden State should trade for Orlando Magic center Wendell Carter Jr.

"The Warriors should trade Kristaps Porziņģis with as many picks as it takes for Trey Murphy or Wendell Carter Jr.," O'Connor said. "To me, I think Wendell Carter would make a lot of sense if the Magic continue sliding here, and we'll see if they do.

"The Magic are in the East; they've stunk so far. Maybe they'll turn things around at some point, but Carter, to me — his size, 270 pounds, the rebounding that he provides, the connective passing and playmaking that I think is a bit underutilized in his career with the Magic. I've always been a fan of his passing vision out of the elbow areas. I think it would fit that Warriors offense.

"He can shoot the ball. He's shooting the ball better than ever so far this year at over 40 percent, and we'll see if he sustains that. But ultimately, I think Carter is a really good player who's been stuck in some not-so-good situations so far in his career in Chicago and Orlando, and I think for Golden State that would be my top target."

Carter Jr. is averaging 14.8 points and 9.6 rebounds per game on 63-percent shooting from the field and 47.8-percent shooting from the 3-point line, taking 2.6 attempts per game.

At 6-foot-10 and 270 pounds, Carter Jr. would instantly become the Warriors' biggest player. Center Charles Bassey also is listed at 6-foot-10 but lacks Carter Jr.'s size, weighing just 230 pounds.

Kerr has played Bassey next to veteran forward Draymond Green for just 10 minutes this season, despite Golden State's plus-16.7 net rating in that small sample size.

Carter Jr.'s presence at the rim would allow Green to roam on defense as a "middle linebacker," and his ability to stretch the floor could further improve the spacing of the NBA's top shooting team.

With star Jimmy Butler looking to return around the NBA All-Star break, O'Connor believes that the Warriors' top priority should be bringing in a big man.

"It should be center over any other position — you want to have a big who can play next to Draymond," O'Connor said. "You want to be able to space or play in the interior, and Carter can provide you a little bit of both in ways that old man (Al) Horford can't. The way, obviously, Porziņģis can't even get on the floor, but the idea of it is a guy who can either space or play inside. So I'd be going after Wendell Carter if the Magic continue sliding."

The Magic sit at 2-7, 12th in the Eastern Conference, and are without forward Paolo Banchero, the No. 1 overall draft pick in the 2022 NBA Draft, because of an ankle injury.

As Golden State works to move Porziņģis, Carter Jr. could be an avenue that the team pursues.

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

#2914. Credit card debt climbs to a record $1.29 trillion, New York Fed research finds

Americans are increasingly turning to credit cards as financial pressures mount, new reports show.

Collectively, credit card balances climbed by $22 billion in the third quarter to a record $1.29 trillion, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of New York's latest household debt report, released Tuesday. Balances are up 4.2% from a year earlier.

Total household debt, including mortgages, auto loans and student loans, also expanded by $334 billion for the July-to-September period to a record $19.1 trillion.

"Overall debt has been growing," the New York Fed researchers said on a press call Tuesday. "This does reflect financial hardship out there."

The average credit card balance per consumer now stands at $6,678, up 2.4% year over year, a separate quarterly credit industry insights report from TransUnion found. The number of people with credit cards rose 2% to 178 million from 174.8 million.

Consumers often turn to credit cards in part because they are more accessible than other types of loans — even though the average interest rate on a new credit card offer is about 24%, according to data from LendingTree. Credit card APRs vary depending on the type of card, the issuer and the borrower's creditworthiness.

"Credit cards continue to be one of the most expensive ways to borrow," said Charlie Wise, TransUnion's senior vice president of global research and consulting.

Over time, an increasing share of new credit cards has gone to borrowers with lower incomes and credit scores, and "those accounts are now showing higher levels of distress," the New York Fed researchers said in a corresponding blog post.

Credit card issuers loosened their lending standards during the Covid pandemic, and delinquency rates rose in the years that followed, the New York Fed researchers said on the press call.

"Macroeconomic headwinds — namely high inflation and rising borrowing costs — have squeezed consumers, with inflation outpacing wage growth for low-income brackets," according to the New York Fed.

Meanwhile, the cost of living continues to climb, with Americans paying more for essentials such as groceries and gas.

Stubborn inflation has now stretched on for years, putting many households in an affordability squeeze. Costs for goods and services rose more than expected again in October, the Bureau of Labor Statistics also reported Tuesday.

"Higher prices and higher rates have pushed more people into debt," said Ted Rossman, principal consumer finance analyst at Money Management International, a nonprofit credit counseling agency.

Balances have continued to swell in the wake of the pandemic, and even though the growth rate is now slowing, "from a household finance standpoint, a lot of damage has been done," Rossman said.

The reports come on the heels of the Democrats' strong midterm results, when many voters expressed their dissatisfaction with the U.S. economy, according to Kyle Moore, chief economist at the nonpartisan think tank The Century Foundation.

"The throughline connecting so many of Tuesday's outcomes was a recognition that the American economy is failing American families," Moore said, referring to Election Day.

#2906. How Jayson Tatum and the Celtics haven't skipped a beat without Jaylen Brown

Despite having Jayson Tatum for a grand total of 522 minutes during the 2025-26 regular season, the Boston Celtics won nearly 70% of their games, finished in the top five in the NBA in both offensive and defensive efficiency, and landed the No. 2 seed in the Eastern Conference. They reached that level of success thanks, in large part, to Tatum's longtime running mate, Jaylen Brown, turning in the finest individual season of his career en route to a sixth-place finish in MVP voting and an All-NBA Second Team selection.

After that, though, the Celtics traded Brown to the Philadelphia 76ers this summer — a move that you might have heard just a teensy bit about over the past five months, and that has been driving no small amount of conversation ahead of the first meeting of the season between Boston and Philly on Tuesday night.

Since that tectonic roster shift, as they seek to chart a new path forward with a shuffled-up rotation and without a franchise cornerstone, the Celtics have … won 80% of their games, rank in the top seven in the NBA in both offensive and defensive efficiency, and sit in the No. 2 seed in the Eastern Conference.

The more things change, the more they stay the same?

This, to some degree, was the bet that Brad Stevens made when he made the decision to move Brown for Paul George, two first-round picks and two second-round picks. In the longer term, exchanging Brown — who has three years and $185 million remaining on his contract, and is also in line for an extension — for George — who makes less this season, has a player option for next season and comes off the books one year earlier — tidies up the Celtics' balance sheet moving forward, potentially putting them in better position to remain competitive in the race for the top of the East several years down the line. (Say it with me, Brad: "optionality!")

In the here-and-now, though, Stevens wagered that, as excellent as Brown was last season and as vital as he's been to the Celtics' perennial success for the past decade, the surrounding talent and head coach Joe Mazzulla's system would provide enough insulation to ensure that the team wouldn't skip too many beats without Brown. Through 10 games, at least? Boston looks pretty right about that.

The Celtics enter Tuesday's heavyweight battle with the Sixers looking broadly like the same elite squad they were last season. On offense, they sit in or near the top 10 in points scored and allowed per possession, and both team effective field goal percentage and opponents' eFG%. They're great at both avoiding turnovers and forcing them. They grab a ton of offensive rebounds and defend without fouling. They still jack a ton of 3s and make 'em at a top-10 clip, and still give up a ton of 3s in favor of protecting the rim without giving up the entire store on defense. They still play a methodical and effective brand of half-court basketball on offense; they're still great at preventing transition opportunities, despite sending multiple bodies to the offensive glass.

Even without Brown, the fundamentals of who the Celtics are haven't changed; the specifics of how they get there, though, are a little bit different. It starts with Tatum, now 18 months removed from rupturing his Achilles tendon and once again firmly entrenched as Boston's No. 1 option.

Tatum is still ironing out some of the wrinkles in his scoring game, finishing at substandard rates at the rim, from midrange and from beyond the 3-point arc. As he told reporters after blowing out the Charlotte Hornets last week, "I still got a little ways to to get back to being the best I ever felt."

Even with his bucket-getting lagging a bit, though, the 28-year-old is off to a flying start, averaging 27.1 points and 6 rebounds in 33.8 minutes per game. (He's been nails in crunch time, too, ranking eighth in the league in clutch scoring, with 21 points in 15 minutes on 8-for-13 shooting with 3 assists and 0 turnovers.) Just as Brown stepped into a higher-volume role in his absence last season, Tatum has slid into the monster void left by Brown's departure this season. He's finishing a career-high 33.3% of Boston's offensive possessions with a shot attempt, foul drawn or turnover, and leads the Celtics in usage, touches and time of possession.

As Gary Washburn of the Boston Globe put it, "Within the Celtics organization, there appears to be a sense of relief that the tension is gone" — that the team's hierarchy is clarified and established, with Tatum at the top of the pecking order, George comfortably slotting in behind him, and everybody else falling in line after that. Despite that, though, it hasn't felt like Tatum's got a stranglehold of Boston's offense — far from it, in fact.

Tatum's on-ball percentage — literally the share of his floor time that he has the ball in his hands — is down from where it was the past two seasons. His average time of possession of 5.1 minutes per game, 33rd in the NBA, between Donovan Mitchell and Ja Morant.

His willingness to get off the ball rather than pound it has helped produce a more egalitarian attack. Boston is averaging 27.2 assists per game, seventh-most in the NBA, up from 24.6 per game (27th) last season. Those dimes are leading to 70 points per game via assist, sixth-most in the league, up from 64 per game (22nd) last season.

Plenty of those helpers are coming directly off Tatum's fingertips. In the early going of the season, his playmaking has really leaped off the screen. Whether working in the pick-and-roll, operating out of the post, or surveying from the top of the floor at the nail, he's mapping the floor and delivering on-time and on-target feeds more often than ever. Tatum is averaging a career-high 7.8 assists against 2.8 turnovers per game, creating more baskets at the rim than all but five players in the NBA, according to PBP Stats, and setting up more 3-pointers than all but eight. He's one of just two players in the NBA in the top 10 in both scoring and assists. The other? The scorching-hot Luka Dončić.

The player Tatum has assisted the most thus far this season? George, who's been the recipient of 13 Tatum dimes, and who has looked like a hand-in-glove fit in his new digs.

George has picked up where he left off last season, when he came back from a 25-game suspension for violating the NBA/NBPA joint anti-drug program by taking "improper medication" for mental health issues to shine for the Sixers in their playoff push and their first-round victory over these same Celtics. He's averaging 20.4 points, 5.2 rebounds and 4.1 assists in 30.1 minutes per game, shooting 50.7% on 2-pointers, 42.5% on 3-pointers while taking more than eight per game, and 80.8% from the free-throw line.

The nine-time All-Star isn't quite the full-tilt offensive force that he was earlier in his career, but he's fit in brilliantly as a secondary scorer and creator who can demand attention as an off-ball threat on offense, and a high-level, gap-filling playmaker who can help crank up the heat on the defensive end. He's still capable of assuming a larger role when called upon, too: When Tatum got the night off against the Bucks last Wednesday, George stepped to the forefront, scoring 25 points with 5 assists, 4 steals and 3 rebounds to lead the way in a 109-102 win.

"The way we're asking him to play is maybe a little bit different than what he's been asked to do in the past," Mazzulla told reporters in Milwaukee on Sunday. "He enjoys that, he's open-minded to it and he just works at it. He's very hard on himself. Holds himself to a very high standard, constantly watching film, taking notes in his journal and just processing the game. He takes pride in learning and getting better, and you're seeing the fruits of that."

The C's are also seeing the fruits of swapping Brown for George in the possession game. According to Jared Dubin's tracking at Last Night in Basketball, the Celtics finished 15th in the nightly possession battle last season, barely generating more scoring chances than their opposition on a night-to-night basis despite top-five finishes in both offensive rebounding rate and offensive turnover rate — due in large part to bottom-10 marks in both getting to the free-throw line and forcing turnovers on defense. Seven of the 14 teams that finished ahead of Boston last season won a playoff series — including all four conference finalists, and the Sixers team that eliminated them in the first round of the playoffs.

It is perhaps notable, then, that the rate at which the Celtics forced turnovers last season increased more with Brown off the floor than it did for any other player who logged at least 1,000 minutes — and that their forced turnover rate had been significantly higher with him off the floor in each of the previous two seasons.

So: Out went Brown, and in came George, a superior off-ball defender who has consistently posted higher steal rates, and center Mitchell Robinson, who'd built his reputation in New York as one of the league's premier extra-possession creators on offense and a disruptive presence in the middle on defense.

Robinson hasn't yet begun to make a major impact in tilting the math. After a sluggish start to the season following the Knicks' championship run and a short summer, the new arrival has started to round into shape, though, averaging 7 points, 8 rebounds and 2.7 steals-plus-blocks over the last three games, with the C's outscoring opponents by 30 points in his 53 minutes of floor time. All told, though, the Celtics have ratcheted up their board-crashing another notch, pulling in 34% of their missed shots through 10 games, up from 31.8% last season.

They're taking 6.5 more free-throw attempts per game, too, due in large part to a more concerted effort to pressure the rim. Last season, Boston ranked dead last in the share of its field goal attempts that came within four feet of the basket. This season, that's up to 17th.

Tatum's played a big role there. Replacing Brown's high-volume attacking as the only Celtic to rank in the top 50 in the league in drives to the basket per game, he's been living at the free-throw line. His previous career high in trips to the charity stripe was 8.4 per game, accumulated when averaging 36.9 minutes a night during the 2022-23 season. Through his first nine appearances of this season, he's taking 9.3 freebies in 33.8 minutes a night — a notable uptick on a per-minute and per-possession basis, and on pace for far and away the highest free-throw attempt rate of his career. (In fact, Tatum's .477 FTA rate ranks in the top 15 in the league among all non-centers.)

Most notably, though, the C's have been significantly more disruptive on the ball. Last season, Boston ranked 28th in the NBA in steals, 25th in deflections and 23rd in opponent turnover rate, playing more conservatively on defense in the belief that if they just stayed at home, funneled the ball to the areas and shooters they preferred, and contested, the math would work out in their favor over the long run.

This season, though — owing partly to the arrival of George, who's been a demon in the passing lanes in the early going — the Celtics are tied for 14th in steals, fifth in deflections and sixth in opponent turnover rate. They're cashing in on those miscues, too, scoring 20.8 points per game off turnovers — seventh-most in the league, and a full five points per game more than last season. (It's also on pace to be more than any Celtics team has posted since Rick Pitino was on the sidelines at the Garden, regaling reporters about Larry Bird and doors.)

Add up the gains in creating cough-ups, in getting to the line and in even more aggressively crashing the offensive boards, and the Celtics are now generating 4.1 more possessions per game than their opponents — the third-highest total in the league, according to Dubin's tracking.

Thanks to Tatum's playmaking, George's shotmaking and a collective effort from a deep rotation, the Celtics have made those extra possessions count, producing a top-five offense despite shooting just 63.2% at the rim as a team (a bottom-five mark) and seeing multiple players (Tatum, Derrick White, Payton Pritchard, Baylor Scheierman, Jordan Walsh, Hugo González) struggle with frigid jumpers in the early going. If those perimeter players start to thaw out a little bit, this offense could hit a new level, and the Celtics could once again find themselves in the thick of the championship chase, even with Brown now plying his trade elsewhere in the division. The more things change, the more they stay the same.
