#2903. Jensen Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy further complicates Nvidia’s China problem

Following Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's admission on Sunday to an extramarital affair with alleged Chinese spy Alice Huang, the chipmaker's relationship to China is about to come under intense scrutiny in Washington, D.C.

Since October 2022, when the Biden administration announced sweeping controls to limit China's ability to obtain advanced U.S. chips, Nvidia has seen its business diminish in the world's second-largest economy. Months after an even stricter set of rules was implemented by the Trump administration, Huang said in October 2025, "We are 100% out of China."

The recent allegations against Huang, while throwing the chipmaker into crisis, have also brought attention to the Nvidia CEO's potential influence over U.S. policy on chip sales to China, where its business once flourished. Of particular interest is whether restricted Nvidia chips have reached Chinese companies through customers like Singapore-based Megaspeed, the company linked to Alice Huang.

The New York Times first reported that the Chinese businesswoman was a spy for China's Ministry of State Security, according to people briefed on the intelligence circulated in a memo among the Trump administration in the spring. China has called the allegation a smear.

Jensen Huang has been closely aligned with President Donald Trump during his second administration, helping advise the White House on artificial intelligence safety regulations, while also pushing for looser restrictions on selling AI chips to China. In December, Trump said Nvidia would be allowed to ship its H200 chips to China, provided the U.S. got a 25% cut of sales. In March, Huang told reporters that Nvidia had "received purchase orders, and we're in the process of restarting our manufacturing."

Huang was a last-minute addition to the roster of tech executives who joined Trump on a trip to China in May, after the president was briefed on intelligence concerns, according to the Times.

"In 35 years, I haven't seen a chip CEO with this much direct influence on export policy," said Patrick Moorhead, CEO at Moor Insights & Strategy, in an email.

Since last week's midterm elections that included sweeping victories across the country for Democrats, the mood in Washington has changed. Three House committees have requested closed-door briefings with the Nvidia CEO about his relationship with Megaspeed.

Democratic lawmakers, who are about to take power in the House and Senate, have said publicly that Congress will investigate and seek answers. Trump said Monday that he was "amazed" by the allegations and that Huang told him he "had no idea that she was a spy." The FBI is now looking into the issue, Trump said at a White House briefing.

It's been a long four years for Nvidia in China.

Before the initial export controls in 2022, China made up nearly a quarter of Nvidia's revenue. Nvidia responded to the Biden-era restrictions by redesigning some of its products to comply with the new regulations.

"It was a turbulent month or so as the company went upside down to re-engineer" the products, Huang told CNBC in a 2023 interview. Still, he was confident the company could continue selling chips to China. "I think we're going to be just fine in the ability to serve the customers there," he said.

After the Biden administration tightened export restrictions again, Nvidia redesigned another AI graphics processing unit for China, the H20. Following reports that Chinese model developer DeepSeek was using restricted Nvidia chips, the Trump administration limited sales of H20. Nvidia revealed it would incur $5.5 billion in costs related to canceled orders for the redesigned chip.

However, despite saying in its forecasts that "we're assuming 0% for China," the company maintained business in the region. In the last fiscal year, Nvidia reported that about 9% of its revenue came from China, including Hong Kong, although that includes revenue from segments outside of restricted data center chips, such as gaming, auto and industrial. Nvidia's China revenue was 8% in the most recent quarter.

A report in May from short seller Culper Research alleged Nvidia's China exposure was much higher. The firm said that more than 20% of Nvidia's fiscal 2026 compute revenue was driven by China, supported by "illegal GPU diversion and Southeast Asian intermediaries," according to the report.

A loophole allows Chinese companies to rent Nvidia GPUs in data centers, even when they're restricted from buying them outright. For example, a Chinese company could lease Nvidia computing power in Europe by renting server capacity from a non-Chinese neocloud or data center operator that's exempt from export controls.

Nvidia didn't respond to a request for comment for this story.

Megaspeed, an Nvidia customer based in Singapore, has been accused of diverting its own supply of Nvidia's restricted AI chips to Chinese companies. Megaspeed denies wrongdoing and said inspections found no evidence of violations.

In 2025, Nvidia changed the way it reported regional sales to categorize by customer headquarters, instead of by where the products were billed. Nvidia also stopped reporting Singapore sales during the same period.

"Whether Blackwell chips reached China is a Megaspeed question that predates this week," said Moorhead, pointing to Bloomberg reporting in December 2025 that found Megaspeed had imported 50,000 more Nvidia chips than Nvidia tallied in its data centers.

In 2025, a House Select Committee opened an investigation into whether China's DeepSeek operates on Nvidia's chips.

A separate investigation by the U.S. Commerce Department was centered on Megaspeed. That probe was abruptly transferred to the Justice Department, the Times reported, following an email from Nvidia's general counsel to the Commerce Department seeking information about the investigation.

The restrictions aren't just coming from the U.S. China has continued to limit the amount of Nvidia chips it will allow into the country, keeping Nvidia's China sales numbers down. The U.S. has licensed H200 sales to Alibaba, Tencent, ByteDance and other Chinese companies, but Beijing hasn't approved large-scale imports, Moorhead told CNBC. Nvidia said H200 shipments to China made up less than 1% of data center revenue for the quarter that ended in July.

Moorhead expects more export control legislation to pass as a result of the latest turmoil. Proposals could allow Congress oversight of chip export licenses or require chipmakers to install security mechanisms to track the location of chips to determine if they've found their way to China.

"Every China license now gets read through this story," Moorhead said. "Jensen has been Nvidia's lead voice on China, and that voice is weaker."

— CNBC's Kif Leswing and Seema Mody contributed reporting to this report.

Correction: Three House committees have requested closed-door briefings with the Nvidia CEO about his relationship with Megaspeed. An earlier version misstated the time element.

#2900. Homeowners tap equity as HELOC balances post biggest jump since 2005: New York Fed report

Homeowners tapped their home equity lines of credit in the third quarter at the highest rate in more than two decades, according to a new Federal Reserve Bank of New York report on household debt released Tuesday.

Balances on HELOCs jumped by $23 billion during the three months ending Sept. 30, the largest increase since 2005. That brings the outstanding amount owed to $481 billion, the report shows. This also marks the 18th consecutive quarterly increase.

HELOC balances peaked at roughly $714 billion in the first quarter of 2009, according to a 2015 New York Fed report.

The current rise in balances comes amid a sluggish housing market, due at least partly to the so-called mortgage rate lock-in effect. Just under half of outstanding mortgages — 49.1% — carried rates of 4% or lower in the second quarter, according to a Realtor.com analysis of federal mortgage data, leaving many owners reluctant to give up their low rates to purchase another home at current higher borrowing costs.

The median price of an existing home was $417,500 in September, according to the National Association of Realtors, a real estate trade group. While up just 1.3% from a year earlier, the amount is nearly 54% higher than in September 2019, according to NAR. The average rate on a typical 30-year fixed-rate mortgage is 7.3% as of Tuesday, according to Mortgage News Daily.

"HELOCs let them borrow against their equity without giving up the low interest rate on their home," said certified financial planner Douglas Boneparth, president and founder of Bone Fide Wealth in New York City and a member of CNBC's Financial Advisor Council.

Mortgage holders collectively had $17.5 trillion in home equity as of the third quarter, according to a new report from Intercontinental Exchange, or ICE, a financial markets technology and data company.

Tappable equity that can be accessed — meaning a homeowner's equity would remain at or above 20% of the home's value after tapping it — rose 1.2%, or $16,600, from a year ago to an average of $204,000 per borrower, the ICE report shows.

"Homeowners are sitting on substantial housing wealth, with 2.4 million mortgage holders now holding at least $1 million in equity," Andy Walden, head of mortgage and Housing Market Research at ICE, said in the report.

HELOCs let you tap a line of credit over time as you need the money, instead of getting it all at once like a home equity loan or cash-out refinance.

While HELOCs may have fewer up-front costs than a home equity loan, they generally have a variable interest rate. This means it will move up and down based on a benchmark like the prime rate, which banks use as a basis to set rates on a variety of loans. And, while the Federal Reserve doesn't control that rate, it is influenced by changes the Fed makes to the so-called federal funds rate.

"If your budget only works at today's rate, then your budget doesn't really work," Boneparth said of HELOCs. "You kind of have to be mindful of the fact that rates may go up."

The average interest rate for a $30,000 HELOC was 7.49% as of Nov. 4, according to Bankrate.

Many HELOCs also have a "draw" period when you can take money out, which often lasts five or 10 years. During that time, you typically are only required to pay the interest on any funds you've withdrawn. After that, however, you'll enter a repayment period of, say, 10 or 20 years, when you're required to pay both interest and principal. Because of that, your payments will jump if you've only been paying interest.

"The real test comes when the repayment period kicks in, and the principal starts to show up, if that's how their HELOC is structured," Boneparth said. "Payments potentially could double overnight, and you really want to know that before you sign anything."

Additionally, consider what you're planning to use the money for, Boneparth said.

"If you're borrowing against your home to improve your home, that could make a lot of sense," Boneparth said. "Borrowing against your house to pay off credit cards is a little trickier because you're turning unsecured debt into debt secured by the roof over your head."

In that case, if your spending habits don't change, he said, "you're just moving the problem somewhere a little scarier."

#2899. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

#2897. Jordan Goodwin scores 18 on 6 3-pointers, Suns ease past the Clippers 133-110

PHOENIX (AP) — Jordan Goodwin scored 18 points in his return from a calf injury, Devin Booker added 17 and the Phoenix Suns cruised past the Los Angeles Clippers 133-110 on Tuesday night.

Phoenix shot 22 of 43 (51%) from 3-point range and had seven players score in double digits, including five who came off the bench. Luke Kennard had 14 points, Jalen Green scored 13 and Collin Gillespie added 12 points and eight assists while Khaman Maluach contributed 11 points and seven rebounds.

The Suns had a 17-point halftime lead and pushed the advantage to 91-59 with four minutes left in the third quarter. They have a 3-2 record so far on their season-long, seven-game homestand.

Goodwin — who signed a $19 million, three-year deal to return to the Suns during the offseason — made 6 of 7 shots from beyond the arc in just 14 minutes. He missed the past three games.

The Clippers were coming off their first win in more than two weeks, but have now lost eight of their last nine games. Rui Hachimura, Baba Miller and Darius Garland all scored 13 points while Max Strus had 12.

Gillespie hit a 3-pointer from 39 feet just before the halftime buzzer to give the Suns a 64-47 lead. Phoenix made 11 of 22 (50%) shots from beyond the arc before the break.

Clippers: Host Oklahoma City on Friday night.

#2896. The best revenge for Jaylen Brown is to keep doing what he’s doing

Sunday wasn't the finest outing of the 2026-27 season for the Sixers.

They struggled defensively against the one-win Bulls, but came away with a 127-121 win in Chicago, moving their record to a league-best 9-2. An afternoon day game to close out a three-game road trip against a team collecting ping-pong balls is ripe for a letdown, but the Sixers took care of business.

Jaylen Brown was strong throughout, dropping a game-high 29 points to go along with seven rebounds and four assists. The five-time All-Star's numbers have been solid through 10 games with averages of 22.3 points, 6.7 rebounds and 4.0 assists. He's shooting 47.7% from the field (the exact same percentage he shot last season) with a decent uptick from three at 39.2%.

As the Sixers get set to welcome Jayson Tatum and the Boston Celtics to South Philly on Tuesday, things remain unsettled between Brown and his former organization. When it comes to Tatum, who Brown spent nine seasons with in Boston, there is no beef, but maybe some lamb chops and gravy, as the 2024 Finals MVP put it.

With all the attention and narratives, it's hard to know what we'll see Tuesday night. Will Brown be out for revenge or does he just want to get a win for his team?

There's a way to do both — keep doing exactly what he's doing.

While the numbers have been solid, closely resembling the numbers he put up in 2023-24 when he helped the Celtics win a championship, it's been the little things with Brown.

Sunday's win was a prime example. As the plucky young Bulls kept coming, Brown provided an offensive boost, nailing a pair of threes in the fourth to thwart multiple Chicago runs. With under a minute left, he made all four of his free throws and also came away with a steal on Josh Giddey while the game remained in doubt.

Earlier last week, he made similar plays against the Pistons. While the Sixers were in control throughout the game — with Brown scoring a game-high 30 — a late comeback attempt by Cade Cunningham in Detroit was enough to make any Sixers fan nervous.

A Cunningham layup cut the lead to 106-102. After a rough offensive possession that ended in a Joel Embiid missed three, the Pistons' All-Star guard came charging up the floor. He pulled up for three in transition, but was blocked by Brown. If that shot goes in, it's suddenly a one-point game with 38 seconds left. Instead, Brown's rejection allowed the Sixers to close the game at the free throw line.

While we heard so much talk about Brown's advanced analytics and on/off splits this offseason, it's hard to quantify a guy who simply makes winning plays.

"He's a really good player," Nick Nurse said last week. "He just wins. He defends. He'll rebound. Obviously, he can score from a bunch of different levels, and I'll take all that any day. It's been phenomenal to coach him.

"What does he bring? He brings an intangible of confidence to your team. Even maybe he's not pouring in points or doing anything that you're [seeing in the box score], but there's a level of fight and competitive drive and winning spirit."

Brown has flaws like any other player. Last season, he turned the ball over more than any player in the NBA. This season, he's averaging 2.1 giveaways a game, on pace for his best season in that category since 2018-19.

There were concerns Brown wanted his own team, which supposedly led to the rift with Tatum. Thus far, there have been no issues there. In fact, Brown even openly said last week this was Tyrese Maxey's team and they're all following the second-longest-tenured Sixer's lead.

When Brown is on the floor, the Sixers' net rating is +5.4, per DataBallr. It's -3.1 when he's off. So, it's cut and dry, the Sixers are a better team when Jaylen Brown is on the court.

And just like his time with the Celtics, his current team has an excellent record.

His best path to revenge against his former team is to do what he does — win.

"Listen, the dude is tough and he wins. I'll take that all day long," Nurse said. "Makes a lot of winning plays, man. He's a winner."

#2895. How much you can make in 2027 and still pay 0% capital gains tax

The IRS has released the capital gains tax brackets for 2027, which include a six-figure earnings limit for the 0% rate for married couples.

If you're eager to sell profitable brokerage account investments, higher income thresholds for the 0% rate could help reduce your tax bill, experts say.

"The 0% capital gains bracket is easily one of the most overlooked strategies for tax-free income," said Stephen Maggard, a certified financial planner with Abacus Planning Group in Columbia, South Carolina.

But it's important to know how the tax brackets work and the possible trade-offs before selling investments.

Despite recent stock market volatility, many investors have portfolio gains after years of market growth. As of market close Nov. 9, the S&P 500 was up about 10% year-to-date. The index also returned more than 16% in 2025, and over 23% in 2024.

If you're planning to leverage the 0% capital gains bracket in 2027 to sell or rebalance brokerage account assets, here are some key things to know.

The capital gains brackets — with rates of 0%, 15% or 20% — apply to assets owned for more than one year, known as long-term capital gains. By contrast, investments held for one year or less are short-term gains, subject to regular income tax rates.

Your rate depends on taxable income, which you calculate by subtracting the greater of the standard or itemized deductions from your adjusted gross income, or AGI. Taxable income also includes any gains from investments that year.

The standard deduction will rise to $16,600 for single taxpayers and $33,200 for married couples in 2027.

For example, let's say a married couple's AGI is $130,000 for 2027, including capital gains. After subtracting the $33,200 standard deduction, their taxable income would be $96,800, which falls below the 0% threshold.

However, taxable income should include mutual fund distributions, which can "dramatically alter year-end planning projections" and increase taxable income, according to CFP Jim Guarino, managing director at Baker Newman Noyes in Woburn, Massachusetts. He is also a certified public accountant.

If your federal taxable income is above the 0% threshold, you'll pay 15% on the portion of investment gains that exceed the 0% limit, up to that bracket's cap. Gains above that may be taxed at 20%.

Also, the 0% capital gains bracket applies to federal taxes, but your profits could still be subject to state-level taxes, experts say.

As income limits rise for the 0% capital gains bracket due to IRS inflation adjustments, more investors could have tax savings opportunities, experts say.

"There is tons of planning to be done here," especially for early retirees with lower income, according to CFP Dinon Hughes, a partner with Nvest Financial in Portsmouth, New Hampshire.

One strategy, known as "tax gain harvesting," is selling profitable brokerage account assets during lower-income years to leverage the 0% bracket, he said. Keep in mind, these sales stack on top of your other earnings, which could bump taxable income above the 0% threshold.

You can also sell the asset and immediately rebuy it to "reset the cost basis," or original purchase price, said CFP Charles Sachs, chief investment officer of Imperio Wealth Advisors in Coral Gables, Florida.

By resetting the cost basis to current market value, you can reduce future capital gains to trim your tax bill.

When selling assets at a loss, the "wash sale rule" blocks tax-loss harvesting when you rebuy a "substantially identical" asset within 30 days before or after the sale. But the same rule doesn't apply when harvesting gains.

Managing capital gains is only one part of yearly tax planning, according to Guarino with Baker Newman Noyes.

Before selling investments, you should know how boosting AGI could impact your broader financial plan, including income phase-outs for various tax breaks, he said.

For example, modified adjusted gross income can impact a range of tax benefits, such as Affordable Care Act health insurance premium subsidies, which can disappear once earnings exceed the federal limits by even a single dollar.

Another example is President Donald Trump's temporary $6,000 senior "bonus" deduction, which is worth up to $12,000 for married couples per year through 2028.

The Baltimore Ravens need a new kicker. Again.

The Atlanta Falcons are signing Jake Moody off of the Ravens' practice squad, per NFL insider Jordan Schultz, robbing Baltimore of another kicker less than a week after Week 1 starter Tyler Loop landed on injured reserve with a groin injury.

The move raises questions about the Ravens' approach to their kicker situation with Loop sidelined for at least four games. The team had a roster spot available, but opted to elevate Moody from the practice squad to face the Jacksonville Jaguars last Thursday. With only two elevations remaining, he would have required a promotion to the 53-man roster to see out Loop's absence, which would have also precluded his departure to Atlanta. Logic suggests that the Ravens should have just promoted Moody knowing they would need him to kick for the rest of the month, maybe longer.

Additional reporting from Jeff Zrebiec of The Athletic indicated that the Ravens were "willing" to promote Moody to their active roster on Monday. The Falcons, though, offered a better situation after releasing kicker Jason Sanders: a potential long-term job for Moody and a home field enclosed by a dome.

It still seems, though, that Baltimore could have headed off this entire issue had they promoted Moody to the active roster last week. It is possible that his agent was keeping tabs on the Falcons' kicking situation and preferred a practice squad stay to preserve his options. However, Loop has not exactly inspired much confidence that he can deliver key kicks for a team that will need to win at the margins to make the playoffs this year. There is an argument that the Ravens should have identified Moody as their kicker for the foreseeable future and adjusted their roster accordingly.

Instead, they will have to scour the NFL's practice squads and free-agent pool to find their next kicker. Signing Nick Folk, who the Falcons cut to add Moody, would be an ironic twist. After three straight years of leading the league with a field goal conversion rate of 95.5% or better, the 42-year-old's numbers have collapsed in 2026. He made just 72.2% of his field goals to start the season with two misses inside of 50 yards and a one-for-four mark from beyond.

#2888. U.S. gas prices fall below $4 for the first time since July

Americans may finally find some relief at the pump.

The national average price of regular gasoline fell below $4 a gallon for the first time since July, reaching $3.9788 on Tuesday, according to AAA. Prices are down about 17 cents from a week ago and nearly 39 cents from a month ago.

The decline comes after energy inflation slowed significantly in October. Energy prices increased 0.7% during the month, a far cry from the 5.2% surge seen in September, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. The BLS' gasoline index increased 1% over the month, after advancing 9.1% in September.

Bespoke Investment Group pointed out that gasoline prices also tend to decline at this time of the year, making part of the recent relief seasonal.

"While lower oil prices have been a tailwind for the relief at the pump, it's also perfectly normal for gas prices to be falling at this time of year, so if they weren't, there would really be something wrong," the firm said.

The drop at the pump followed a sharp retreat in crude oil. International oil benchmark Brent crude on Tuesday briefly fell below $90 a barrel for the first time since August as comments from U.S. President Donald Trump fueled hopes for an end to Washington's nearly nine-month conflict with Iran.

Recovering oil flows from the Persian Gulf have also eased supply concerns. Regional crude exports have returned to an estimated 80% to 85% of prewar levels, including pipeline shipments, according to ING.

"Energy markets remain under pressure as hopes for a de-escalation in the Middle East reduce geopolitical risk premiums. Oil extended its losses as Gulf supply flows continued to recover, while European gas also moved lower," analysts at ING wrote.

However, the Federal Reserve Governor Christopher Waller warned Tuesday that energy shocks may have more persistent effects on inflation than they did before the pandemic.

"While I am pleased to see energy prices down from their high rates over the past week or so, they are still quite elevated relative to the start of the year, and the ultimate effect on inflation will depend on what levels are sustained," he said.

#2886. Manchester United’s $10 Million Pochettino Move Could Change Everything

A $10 million release clause could bring Mauricio Pochettino to Manchester United, but the total cost rises when assistant coaches are included. The Athletic reported this specific number as the price to trigger his exit from the U.S. national team. That sum covers the head coach alone, yet the final bill would likely be much higher. Pochettino's assistant coaches also have contracts that must be bought out, adding several million more to the total. Manchester United has the cash to handle such a bill without blinking. Forbes values the club at $7.2 billion, making it the third most valuable soccer team in the world.

United is staring at a possible drop to the second division after a terrible start to the season. They have won only one league game in ten matchdays and sit in the relegation zone. Club executives met on Tuesday morning to discuss firing current manager Michael Carrick. Pochettino is a leading candidate to replace him if the decision goes through. He signed a new four-year deal with the U.S. in August that runs through the 2030 World Cup. That pact includes the $10 million release clause, which is a pittance for a club of this size. Other names on the shortlist include Eddie Howe, Julian Nagelsmann, and Antonio Conte. Currently unemployed, all three possess substantial European experience.

The U.S. team is not sitting idle while these rumors swirl. Veteran defender Antonee "Jedi" Robinson spoke to reporters before training on Tuesday morning in Atlanta. "That news kind of got sprung on all of us along with the rest of the world today," Robinson admitted. He noted that the distraction has not changed how the players work on the field. Young stars like Cavan Sullivan remain focused on the coach who has helped them grow. Sullivan said, "Mauricio has taken me under his wing in the most tremendous way possible." The players are used to fast-changing environments in soccer and know how to handle the noise. They plan to focus on the next task at hand until told otherwise.

Pochettino earns about $7 million annually in his current role, which is a very cushy job for a coach. He leads the national team with a relaxed schedule compared to the daily grind of club management. Some wonder why he would leave the U.S. for the high pressure of the Premier League. One source noted, "Why would he leave the U.S. for something like this? He's made a lot of money!" But the chance to lead a historic brand like Manchester United might be too good to pass up. If he succeeds, he could become an instant legend and build statues at Old Trafford. The first match of the two-game series against Haiti is Saturday in Miami.

The decisive second leg of the Concacaf Nations League quarterfinal series takes place in Cincinnati on Nov. 17. The winner of this series will advance to the final four in March in Inglewood, California. On Nov. 11 in Monaco, United executives plan to reconvene regarding Carrick's future. If Pochettino leaves, the U.S. will likely take its time to find a replacement before the next game on Dec. 13 against Jamaica. An interim staff will probably handle the team through the rest of the calendar year. The realistic window to hire a new coach could open sometime before March 2027.

#2883. Commanders stand pat at NFL trade deadline: What it means

The 2026 NFL trade deadline passed on Tuesday with a few more moves around the league. The Washington Commanders weren't a part of those moves. While the Commanders listened to offers, they truly believe they are still in the NFC East race and chose to stand pat for the second consecutive year.

Was this the right move? Only time will tell. The Commanders had several players who could've been moved for Day 3 picks. Players such as wide receiver Stefon Diggs, linebackers Dorance Armstrong, K'Lavon Chaisson and Frankie Luvu, offensive lineman Andrew Wylie and cornerback Mike Sainristil could have all likely fetched a pick each. Lesser players at the same positions as Diggs, Chaisson and Luvu got quality picks in return.

General manager Adam Peters needs to win games. Whether the Commanders can catch the Giants or not — they better win on Thursday — he needs to stack some wins to show owner Josh Harris he should be picking the next head coach.

Peters isn't on the hot seat, regardless of what anyone says. He'll be picking the next coach. At this point, it's not an assumption that the Commanders will move on from Dan Quinn after the season. Quinn hasn't done himself any favors, whether it's the continued losing, the end-of-game mismanagement, or more. Barring a very unexpected winning streak, Washington will move on from Quinn. Peters isn't going anywhere.

So, if you're a GM that needs to win, why would you trade some valuable pieces who can probably help you carve out a few wins for the remainder of the season? With Terry McLaurin out, keeping Diggs felt essential. Washington wants to help quarterback Jayden Daniels finish strong, and taking away another top option would not help accomplish that.

Players like Chaisson and Luvu could be a part of the future. Sure, you could re-sign them after the season if you traded them, but what happens if they go elsewhere, play well, and are offered an extension? Luvu has bounced back after a rough 2025. Chaisson looks like a player you can build around for the next few seasons.

It doesn't really make sense why Peters didn't trade Wylie and Sainristil. Wylie brings value as a sixth offensive lineman. He's started close to 100 NFL games and can play tackle or guard. You could make the case that he should be starting now on Washington's starting offensive line. But he's not. So, why not trade him if you can get a pick back?

Sainristil is the most confusing case. Is this a case of Peters just not wanting to admit a mistake? A second-round pick in 2024, Sainristil regressed over the last two years after a promising season. Does Peters think newly hired assistant Steve Wilks can help Sainristil? Otherwise, it makes no sense. He has played one defensive snap combined in the last two games. In three of the last four games, he's played fewer than 10 defensive snaps. He's essentially Washington's No. 4 cornerback behind Amik Robertson, Trey Amos and Rasul Douglas, but ahead of Isaac Yiadom and Fabian Moreau.

He had been linked to the Bears and Lions, among others. That's not to say they would've given up much for him, but there was rumored interest. Sainristil could use a fresh start. So could the Commanders.

Time will tell if Peters made the right move by not making a move. Some just assume that adding picks means landing guaranteed players. It means nothing if you could pick the wrong guys. In this case, Peters is banking on the continued growth of Daniels and a schedule that lightens up after a brutal start, allowing the Commanders to pick up more wins and maybe — just maybe — compete in the NFC East.

If you've watched this team this season, it's difficult to imagine this team winning multiple games in a row and competing. But as long as Washington has a chance, Peters is rolling the dice.

#2881. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2880. Colts keep RB Jonathan Taylor at NFL trade deadline, reportedly turned down offers of second-round picks

The Indianapolis Colts were aggressive sellers at the trade deadline.

But the most prominent potential trade piece on their roster is staying put. Per NFL Insider Tom Pelissero, the Colts declined to trade three-time Pro Bowl running back Jonathan Taylor ahead of Tuesday's NFL trade deadline.

Per the report, Indianapolis turned down multiple offers of second-round picks to keep Taylor in a Colts uniform. The Colts would have only considered a "blockbuster" return to part with Taylor, according to the report.

It's a curious decision for a team that's given up on the season and faces what looks like a long road back to relevance.

Taylor has been one of the league's most reliable and productive backs throughout his seven-season NFL career. At 27 years old — toward the end of the traditional running-back prime — he's a potential difference-maker for a team seeking to compete for a championship now. The Colts are not that and don't project to be anytime soon.

But Taylor has been the face of the Colts through most of his career and is a fan favorite. That holds value in Indianapolis, so much that the Colts reportedly turned down multiple reasonable offers that would have provided a valuable asset to build for the future.

Amid the failures of extending Daniel Jones and trading two first-round picks for Sauce Gardner, the Colts fired longtime general manager Chris Ballard in October after a 2-4 start. Indianapolis continued its roster purge Tuesday with the trade of wide receiver Keenan Allen to the 49ers as the trade deadline approached. Hours later, the Colts reportedly agreed to trade wide receiver Josh Downs to the Chiefs.

But Taylor is reportedly staying put alongside Jones on an offense that looks little like it did when the Colts started the season with hopes of competing for the playoffs.

#2878. Koa Peat’s development requires patience amid Suns’ early struggles

You could feel it last night during the loss to the San Antonio Spurs, couldn't you? When Suns' rookie wing Koa Peat entered the game, you could feel that "disease of what if" beginning to creep in. Perhaps I felt it more than most because I'd spent my morning browsing eBay, looking through the newly released 2026-27 Topps basketball cards and hunting for Peat rookie cards. Side note: I love that they're doing the 45th anniversary of the 1981-82 Topps cards. Classically beautiful.

So when he entered the game with 5:56 left and the Suns trailing by 30 points in the fourth quarter, I suddenly had a reason to remain engaged for the rest of the evening. And he didn't disappoint. The first time Peat touched the ball, he attacked the cylinder, drove left, spun right, and banked a beautiful 6-foot shot off the glass for two points. On the very next possession, he found himself in transition and tossed an alley-oop to Michael Jordan reincarnated in Jamaree Bouyea for another two points.

I felt it deep within my soul. We need to get this guy minutes!

Then reality quickly set in. There aren't necessarily minutes available for him right now, and one of the primary reasons those two possessions looked so beautiful was sitting comfortably on the San Antonio bench. Victor Wembanyama, the very villain responsible for putting Phoenix in a 30-point hole, wasn't on the floor to swat either opportunity into the fifth row.

When you're watching a team that desperately lacks size and rebounding, any opportunity to see a player who possesses those attributes immediately feels like you've stumbled upon the solution. You begin convincing yourself that the coaching staff doesn't know what it's doing, that players like Peat should be receiving consistent opportunities because they offer something the current rotation is sorely missing.

On paper, that sounds wonderful. In reality, it's not nearly that simple, and there are legitimate reasons why it shouldn't happen right now.

For starters, where are those minutes supposed to come from? Ryan Dunn has been playing out of his mind to begin the season. Are we supposed to take some of his limited 16.7 minutes away? Do we cut his playing time in half so Peat can get on the floor long enough to work up a sweat without ever establishing enough rhythm to make those minutes meaningful?

Granted, if we could carve out some minutes from Jalen Green, I'd certainly entertain that conversation. Perhaps that's where an opportunity exists. But the reality is that developing chemistry and cohesion requires consistency within your rotations. You can't expect players to become comfortable with one another if you're constantly reshuffling responsibilities and playing time every time a rookie enters a game and strings together two positive possessions.

Quite honestly, if Peat is receiving significant minutes right now, it probably means the Suns have shifted their priorities away from winning in the present and toward developing for the future. As I've stated before, losing doesn't benefit this franchise. Phoenix doesn't control its own draft destiny, so intentionally prioritizing development at the expense of winning creates a situation where you're accumulating losses without receiving the traditional reward that comes with them. You might as well go out there and try to win basketball games.

There will eventually come a time when development becomes a greater priority. If the Suns remain 12th in the Western Conference standings as the season progresses, the organization may need to reevaluate its approach and provide more meaningful opportunities to its younger players. At that point, giving Peat, Fleming, and others additional minutes could become an important part of determining what the future looks like.

But we're nine games into the season. Nine. And while I recognize there are people already pounding their fists on the table demanding more playing time for Peat, I'm here to tell you that now is neither the time nor the place.

That's the challenge of the disease of what if. It sends your mind wandering toward hypothetical solutions that seem perfectly logical when you're watching your team get dismantled. You see a young player make a couple of exciting plays, recognize that he possesses attributes your team desperately needs, and begin imagining how differently things might have unfolded had he been given an opportunity earlier.

But countless variables contribute to those moments. The opponent's personnel, the score, the defensive intensity, the matchups, and the circumstances surrounding those opportunities all matter. And because you'll never receive a definitive answer to what would have happened had that player entered the game earlier, the hypothetical begins to feel like a solution.

Adding some fuel to the debate is that Suns' head coach Jordan Ott stated just a few days ago that Peat is "right on the cusp."

"Koa is next and Koa's right there on the tip," Ott stated. "We understand that he's young and he's hasn't done doesn't have a ton of NBA game experience, but again, the way the game is moving, the ability to drive, process, and defend the drive, he appears to have it, and he's right there. He'll get his opportunity, and we have full faith that that's what's coming."

The Suns could certainly use some help on the glass, as last night's loss served as another painful reminder of how difficult it has been for this team to secure rebounds. Phoenix was outrebounded by 20 against San Antonio and currently ranks dead last in the NBA with a 44.6% overall rebounding percentage. Perhaps even more concerning is its defensive rebounding percentage, which sits at a league-worst 60.1%. That means opposing teams have an alarmingly high likelihood of extending possessions by grabbing offensive rebounds, a recurring nightmare for Phoenix.

The consequences are showing up on the scoreboard. The Suns allow a league-high 19.0 second-chance points per game, essentially handing opponents additional scoring opportunities because they cannot consistently finish defensive possessions with a rebound. It's difficult enough to stop NBA offenses the first time around. When you're giving them multiple opportunities to score on the same possession, you're making an already challenging task nearly impossible.

Where do I land on Koa Peat? I want to see him play. I want to see what he can become, and I certainly want the Suns to develop the talent they've invested in. But wanting to see more of a player and believing he deserves more minutes right now are two different things.

The Suns have to balance the future with the present, and nine games into the season, they still have a responsibility to compete. Maybe Peat eventually earns a larger role. Maybe the circumstances change and create that opportunity. Until then, we have to resist the temptation to turn two exciting possessions into an answer to problems that are considerably more complicated. The disease of what if is powerful, but it doesn't necessarily make the prescription any better.

Me? I'll just head back over to eBay and hunt more rookie autos.

#2877. Serene Jourdy, Jane Chevalier star at Section 1 swimming championships

WEST NYACK — Horace Greeley senior Serene Jourdy capped off her final Section 1 swimming championships Tuesday in a big way.

At Felix Festa Middle School, she helped the Quakers 200-yard medley relay take second and she won two more individual event titles.

Jourdy won the 100-yard backstroke (55.49 seconds) title for the third straight year and the 100 butterfly (54.04) for the fourth straight year. The later came with an added bonus.

"I'm super proud of myself," Jourdy said. "I'm so happy all of my fellow (Greeley) swimmers were here to support me. It means so much to me. It's bittersweet because I know it's my last sectionals, but I'm happy I've come this far. Both of those were the fastest I've ever gone. I'm really excited for states to see if I can do even better."

Her time in the fly was not only was an automatic All-American time and lowered her meet record she set a year ago, but Jourdy set a new Section 1 record. The record was previously held by former Fox Lane standout Kim Holden in 2008 (54.40).

"I knew I had the meet record," Jourdy said. "I was kind of hoping I could get the Section 1 record with it, but I wasn't expecting to. I was just trying to go out there and swim my race the way I wanted to swim it. I'm just happy it ended up being the result I was hoping for."

The combined squad of Mahopac/Putnam Valley/Carmel started the meet off with a Section 1 record in the 200 medley relay. The quartet of sophomore Chloe Forger and juniors Elisabeth Knuth, Kira Fufaro, and Keira Troccoli won the event in a time of 1:45.69. They tied the meet and Section 1 record set back in 2019 by a quartet from Sleepy Hollow/Hastings/Edgemont/Irvington.

After last year's title run, Ursuline junior Jane Chevalier said she felt pressure when it came to defending the team championship.

It didn't look like she felt any as she led the Koalas to two Section 1 relay titles, two more individual titles and the team championship for the second straight year.

"I'm really happy," Chevalier said of the meet. "We did it last year so I felt there was pressure on us to do it again. I just felt it was great to be able to do it again."

The Koalas won the title, scoring 357 points. Fox Lane took second (316 points). Greeley took third (227 points). Scarsdale and Pelham round out the top five.

Chevalier won the 500 freestyle (4:52.67) and the 200 individual (2:01.6) event titles.

She, along with senior Lori Jiang, and sophomores Sheena Terri and Hayden Turnbull won the 200 and 400 free relay titles, 1:37.15 and 3:32.36, respectively

Jiang won a team championship when she was a sophomore swimming for Scarsdale.

Jiang moved her junior year but because of the Section 1 transfer rules, she had to sit out. After Tuesday's win, she showed love for her former team as her current team got the win.

"Amazing," Jiang said of the win. "The team is great. I love the Scarsdale team. They're all great girls. We're still really good friends. Being able to swim this year for Ursuline and getting the team title again was an amazing experience with another great group of girls."

Swimming is a tough sport technically and diving is also a tough sport technically. So imagine competing in both.

Due to in-season qualifying with a score of 440, Dougherty qualified for the NYSPHSAA championships during the season at the White Plains 11-dive meet on Oct. 16. Dougherty became the first athlete from Section 1 in decades (according to Section 1 swim coordinator Meg Kaplan) and the first ever from Pelham to qualify for the NYSPHSAA championships in both swimming and diving.

At Tuesday's Section 1 championships, she helped the 200 medley relay take fourth overall and qualfied for the NYSPHSAA championships.

"Just having fun," Dougherty said. "I've been super lucky with my team this year. We have the most girls we've had on our squad in years. We've had our strongest performances. I honestly think just having fun and working hard has been the biggest thing for me."

At Friday's Section 1 diving meet at Purchase College, Dougherty finished fifth overall.

Scarsdale's Sophia Wu won the title scoring a 496.20. John Jay/North Salem's Samantha Mishler took second and Rye/Blind Brook/Rye Neck's Lucie Megret took third.

White Plains freshman Fiona Blaho was the third swimmer of the day to win two individual Section 1 championships. She won the 200 free in an All-American consideration time of 1:50.06. She won the 100 free in a time of 50.78 which was also in consideration for All-American.

Pelham senior Claire Reed won the 100 breaststroke in an All-American consideration time of 1:03.35.

Clarkstown South sophomore Samara Javer had the best individual swim of anyone from Rockland County at Tuesday's Section 1 championships.

She took second in the 200 freestyle behind Blaho in a time of 1:51.48. She took third in the 500 free in a time of 4:58.26.

"I was really happy," Javer said. "I was nervous going into it but I was happy I was able to finish strong and get two medals."

The Section 1 swimmers and divers will compete at the NYSPHSAA championships Friday, Nov. 21 and Saturday, Nov. 21 at the Webster Aquatic Center in Webster. This meet is the 50th anniversary of the meet. The swim preliminaries begin at 10 a.m. on Friday. The diving will follow at 1:30 p.m. with the full 11 dive program. The swimming finals will begin at 10 a.m. on Saturday.

#2876. Grading the trade: Josh Downs gives Chiefs WR help they needed, but is he the right fit?

Just prior to the trade deadline, the Kansas City Chiefs acquired wide receiver Josh Downs from the Indianapolis Colts. The Chiefs will receive Downs and a 2027 seventh-round pick, and the Colts will get back fourth- and fifth-round picks in 2027.

Downs, 25, who recently requested a trade, got his wish and now will team up with Patrick Mahomes. In nine games (seven starts) with the Colts this season, Downs caught 33 passes for 354 yards and one TD on 57 targets.

Downs also had a team-high seven catches for 72 yards on nine targets in the Colts' 33-30 loss in Kansas City back in Week 2.

Who won this trade? Let's dive into the debate.

The Chiefs were in big need of adding receiver help. Not one wideout on the roster has either 50 targets or 300 yards receiving on the season. Downs, sharing the lead role in Indy along with Keenan Allen (and with Alec Pierce hurt), has surpassed both marks.

That's not to say that he instantly will become Patrick Mahomes' go-to target. But it's hard not to imagine Downs stepping in and earning a fairly sizable role in Kansas City's offense, assuming he can develop a relationship with Mahomes and pick up the playbook quickly.

Downs has primarily been a slot receiver in his career, although his work rate playing outside has increased slightly in 2026 out of need. It would make sense to put him in the slot, although what makes him, Rashee Rice and Xavier Worthy attractive is that they can line up in multiple spots.

Rice's numbers over the past year-plus have been slightly better inside, but Worthy has been far more efficient as a perimeter receiver in his career. That likely means Downs can take those snaps and do what he does best: run efficient routes, separate quickly and generate first downs. The biggest drawback is that Downs is not a deep threat, which was probably their biggest need.

This move also can help the Chiefs' punt-return situation. Nikko Remigio has struggled in that role this season. Downs has only returned two punts this season, but he has a very respectable 9.8-yard career average on punt returns.

The Chiefs know that Downs is a free agent after the season, and they had to be comfortable with the idea of this trade potentially being a half-season rental. Thinning out their Day 3 pick allotment stings a little, but the price wasn't that steep in the big picture, especially when you compare it to what the Seahawks paid for Rashid Shaheed (fourth- and fifth-round picks) and what the Jaguars dealt for Jakobi Meyers (fourth- and sixth-rounders) before last year's deadline.

This move is about taking advantage of a championship window with Mahomes showing he's recovered from his knee injury and Travis Kelce still performing at a relatively high level in his expected swan-song season.

Downs requested a trade and got his wish. The Colts likely weren't convinced they had to extend him, and Downs probably wasn't returning to Indianapolis next season anyway, so trading him now made perfect sense instead of waiting for compensatory-pick returns more than a year from now.

It fits right in line with the other trades they've made since firing GM Chris Ballard, shipping out one-time franchise pillars Quenton Nelson and DeForest Buckner and also dealing Allen. When you consider what they got back for Downs, which was two early Day 3 picks, it was a pretty fair market price.

Not having a first-rounder next spring from the Sauce Gardner will continue to sting. But with the trades, they've managed to replenish the cupboard a bit. They already have seven Day 3 picks to work with and are projected to receive two more compensatory selections in that same range.

Having those picks can allow the Colts potentially to slide up in the draft, and quarterback could be on the menu given the uncertainty surrounding Daniel Jones, even if Jones was not traded by the deadline.

They'll also eventually need to replenish a gutted WR room, but for now the Colts can see what they have in the likes of Nick Westbrook-Ikhine, Anthony Gould, Ashton Dulin, Deion Burks, Laquon Treadwell and, of course, Pierce, when he's back healthy again. It wouldn't be stunning if the Colts only bring back a few of these players next year, but the remainder of the season will help them decide who stays and who goes.

#2874. LeBron Log No. 4: A courtside sip, 16,000 makes and Cleveland's welcome

LeBron Log No. 4: A courtside sip, 16,000 makes and Cleveland's welcome originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

LeBron James' latest week with the Sixers included a sip of a stranger's wine, yet another number no NBA player had reached and a return to the city where his career began.

It also included his worst performance with Philadelphia, followed less than 48 hours later by his highest-scoring game with the team.

Welcome to the fourth edition of the LeBron Log.

No chronicle of James' first season in Philadelphia could omit the time he helped himself to a fan's wine during a game.

James chased Tyrese Maxey's transition pass into the stands during the third quarter of the Sixers' Nov. 2 win over the Bulls. He collided with Philadelphia native Troy Clemons, spilled some of Clemons' wine and sampled what had landed on his arm.

The interaction received its own story on our site, and deservedly so. It also belongs in the Log.

Clemons did not keep the cup as a souvenir. He threw it away and got another drink.

James returned to the court and later broke into a dance after scoring over the 6-foot-1 Tre Jones. The wine clip generated millions of views across social media and became the defining image from an otherwise ordinary 19-point Sixers victory.

The following night in Detroit, James offered a broader explanation for why Philadelphia has already looked comfortable together.

"We've got a good team," James said. "Guys vibe with one another. Guys like each other. Guys enjoy being around each other. That has a lot to do with it, too. It makes the game a lot easier when you enjoy being around people."

Eleven games into his Sixers career, James appears to be doing exactly that.

James arrived in Detroit two baskets away from another unprecedented number.

His second field goal against the Pistons made him the first player in NBA history to reach 16,000 regular-season field goals.

James finished the Sixers' 112-106 victory with 13 points, 10 rebounds and eight assists. The near-triple-double helped Philadelphia improve to 8-1 and avenge its only loss of the season.

Kareem Abdul-Jabbar ranks second in league history with 15,837 made field goals. Karl Malone is a distant third at 13,528. James is the only active player with more than 12,000.

His scoring record has long been secure. James passed Abdul-Jabbar in career points in February 2023 and later overtook him in made baskets. The round number in Detroit still illustrated the distance he has created between himself and every other scorer.

James began his career by making 5,361 field goals across seven seasons with Cleveland. He added 3,510 in Miami, returned to make 3,691 more for the Cavaliers and made another 3,421 during eight years with the Lakers.

Basket No. 16,000 belonged to Philadelphia.

The Sixers' 8-1 start also carried a notable James connection. Each of his previous four teams to begin a season at least 7-1 reached the NBA Finals.

James was not ready to entertain that conversation in early November.

"It's too early to be talking about the Finals and things of that nature," he said. "We've got to continue to buy into what we want to do."

Two nights later, James returned to the place where nearly half of those 16,000 baskets were made.

His first game in Cleveland as a Sixer brought out jerseys from every stop of his career. Cavaliers colors filled Rocket Arena alongside Heat black, Lakers purple and the Sixers' throwback look.

James received a warm ovation when he was introduced last among Philadelphia's starters. When he checked out late in the fourth quarter, with the result decided, the crowd stood for him again.

James has experienced both ends of Cleveland's emotional range.

He returned as a member of the Heat on Dec. 2, 2010, to one of the most hostile receptions in NBA history. Six years later, he delivered the Cavaliers their first championship and ended Cleveland's 52-year major professional sports title drought.

"I've seen the depths of hell when it comes to a reception here, all the way to the heavens above," James said. "Anything in between is easy money."

The reception fell somewhere in the middle. His performance landed much closer to the bottom.

James finished with eight points on 3-for-12 shooting, three assists and six turnovers in the Sixers' 119-95 loss. It was his first single-digit performance in Cleveland since he scored three points against the Rockets on Dec. 30, 2004.

He had scored at least 10 points in 396 consecutive regular-season games in the city. The run covered his first stint with the Cavaliers, every visit as an opponent and his entire second tenure with the organization.

It ended during his first trip back in a Sixers uniform.

"I definitely did not feel in a good rhythm offensively," James said. "A couple of them were careless. A couple of them were just trying to make the right play and not being successful with it."

It was only the 10th single-digit regular-season game of James' 1,630-game career. He still took time afterward to consider what an eventual jersey retirement in Cleveland could represent.

"The journey from being an 18-year-old rookie, to leading a franchise to a championship," James said. "And everything in between."

James did not carry the rough night into Chicago.

Less than 48 hours after struggling in Cleveland, he scored a season-high 22 points in the Sixers' 127-121 victory over the Bulls. Thirteen came during the second quarter, when James helped Philadelphia turn an early deficit into a halftime lead.

His first basket produced another milestone.

James entered Sunday trailing Abdul-Jabbar's 1,895 points for the most scored against the Bulls in NBA history. His first-quarter three-pointer moved him into the lead. He ended the afternoon with 1,916.

The most interesting play from his night was a pass.

With the Sixers ahead by four and 2:20 remaining, James drove into the paint. Three Chicago defenders collapsed toward him. Maxey stood between the left wing and corner, but even he did not believe James could see him.

James fired the ball through the defense. Maxey sidestepped Isaac Okoro and made the three-pointer, pushing Philadelphia's lead to seven.

After his postgame interview, James pulled out his phone and watched the play again.

"He didn't think I saw him," James called out from his locker as reporters asked Maxey about the possession.

Maxey acknowledged that he had positioned himself to rebound because he expected James to shoot.

"Honestly, I was like this," Maxey said, raising his hands. "I thought he was about to shoot a layup."

James has spent the opening weeks of the season teaching his new teammates to expect the unexpected pass.

"You've got to become a wide receiver or a catcher at that point," Maxey said. "You never know when you're going to get the ball.

#2873. Fort Defiance volleyball secures second-straight regional title

FORT DEFIANCE — Annalee Halterman was all smiles standing in the Don Landes Gym. As the final point played out, she looked to a teammate on the bench and said, "This is peak high school."

Peak indeed. It doesn't get much better as a high school athlete than to celebrate a championship with teammates and Fort Defiance got to do that Tuesday, Nov. 10 in the Region 2B championship. Fort beat previously unbeaten Madison County 25-12, 25-22, 25-20 for its second straight regional title.

"That was one of the greatest feelings of all time," Halterman said. "This is insane. I've never felt this happy in my life."

Fort Defiance will now host a Class 2 state quarterfinal match Nov. 14 against the loser of Amelia or Poquoson. Those two play Nov. 11 in the Region 2A title game.

This season has been just as special for senior libero Norah Anderson as last season and the season before as Fort tries to reach the state championship game for a third year in a row. For Anderson, it's all about the journey and in each of the past three seasons that journey has been with a different cast of teammates.

While there are returning players every season, there are also plenty of new faces. This season freshman Maddie Gale has made a huge impact on the team as a setter and hitter. Amelia Good transferred from Highland County and has been a big hitter for Fort Defiance. And middle blocker Harper Carter is a crucial part of this Fort team after sitting out last season.

"Just the chance to experience this with new girls, my best friends, honestly, I'm just so excited," said Anderson. "I cannot wait to keep playing. I don't want it to be over."

It's not over yet, although from here on out it's win-or-go-home games for Fort Defiance. Win three of those and they will be state champions.

Caroline Hanger, certainly not a new face for this team, continued to be a force with 12 kills in the win. Gale had 22 assists, five kills, two aces and 10 digs. Anderson finished with 13 digs to lead the team. Alexis Miller had nine assists.

The match was delayed by 50 minutes because of traffic issues that kept Madison County's bus from arriving in time for the scheduled 6 p.m. start. Once the match did start, Fort made short work of the Mountaineers. Madison was close in the second and third sets, but it never felt like Fort was in trouble.

"I feel like we played really great defense," said Fort coach Amber Pitsenbarger. "That was one thing that really kept us in it, was our defense. We were picking up absolutely everything."

Pitsenbarger said getting to host a state quarterfinal match for the second year in a row is special.

"It's huge," she said. "The girls have worked really, really hard. They definitely deserve it. Our fans were great tonight and I think that really helped us. Just hope everyone shows up for Saturday too."

#2871. Observations after Sixers lose 1st Brown vs. Tatum matchup, George scores 36

Observations after Sixers lose 1st Brown vs. Tatum matchup, George scores 36 originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Round 1 of the new-look Sixers vs. the new-look Celtics decisively went Boston's way on Tuesday night.

In the first meeting between the teams since the July trade that sent Jaylen Brown to Philadelphia and Paul George to Boston, the Celtics earned a 123-101 win at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

The Sixers dropped to 9-3 and the Celtics improved to 8-3.

George was the game's brightest star, scoring 36 points on 14-for-22 shooting. Jayson Tatum added 21 points.

Tyrese Maxey's 24 points led the Sixers. Brown was hampered by foul trouble in his heavily anticipated first outing against Tatum. He posted 13 points, five rebounds and two assists in 26 minutes.

The Sixers will host the Raptors on Thursday night. Here are observations on their blowout loss to the Celtics:

To no one's surprise, Brown and Tatum guarded each other to open the night.

VJ Edgecombe defended a former teammate in George, who scored Boston's first four points with a pair of pure jumpers. On the other end, Maxey made the Sixers' first three field goals.

Judging by volume, the home fans cared most about the much-scrutinized Brown vs. Tatum matchup. The crowd was excited early when Brown snagged a steal and then dished to Maxey for a fast-break three-pointer. The fans also dialed up the noise when Brown sized up Tatum in isolation. He wound up dribbling the ball off his foot and out of bounds. Tatum pointed to signal it was Celtics ball.

Boston's star forward had a productive first quarter, posting 11 points. He scored an easy two when he used a back screen to break free from Brown and convert a layup that put the Celtics up 11-7. Boston began 9 for 12 from the floor as a team.

As for Brown, he sat shortly after being called for his second foul with 6:48 left in the first quarter.

Brown re-entered at the start of the second quarter. The Sixers still struggled to eat into Boston's lead.

A Sam Hauser triple extended the Celtics' advantage to 12 points. There was some audible frustration in the building after Brown missed two free throws on the next possesion.

The Sixers' deficit hit 16 points on a Hugo Gonzalez jumper. Meanwhile, the Sixers missed 12 of their first 14 three-point tries. While some of those shots were open looks that simply didn't go down, the Sixers also rushed up several contested jumpers before they made Boston's defense do serious work.

In the bigger picture, it wouldn't be remotely surprising if shooting stands out as a weak area for the Sixers come the trade deadline. They entered the game ranked 27th in the NBA in three-point frequency outside of garbage time and 22nd in three-point accuracy, according to Cleaning the Glass.

Joel Embiid (16 points, six rebounds) had an injury scare late in the second quarter when he fell hard after missing a layup in transition. He rose gingerly and limped through the final 30 seconds or so of the first half.

The Sixers' star big man was fine to start the third quarter and even drained a long-overdue three on the opening possession. He'd been in a terrible outside shooting slump (3 for 32 from deep on the season) before that shot.

Brown's foul trouble persisted. He picked up his fourth personal with 10:40 to go in the third quarter.

The Sixers stormed back without him and their defense played a large role. After LeBron James rumbled in for a layup, Dean Wade stonewalled Payton Pritchard on the perimeter to force a shot-clock violation. Wade later canned a three to even the contest at 76-all.

The Celtics responded and Tatum led the charge. His third-quarter exploits included a highlight slam on James and a top-of-the-key three to build Boston's lead back up to nine points.

Pritchard buried a three on the first possession of the fourth quarter and the Celtics' advantage kept growing. George celebrated a triple that stretched his team's lead to 100-82. The Sixers' spirits were considerably lower as they headed to their bench for a timeout. As a Sixer, George only had one higher-scoring game (39 points) than his performance Tuesday.

George and Tatum were each excellent in Philadelphia. Outside of Maxey, who finished 8 for 12 from the field, the Sixers didn't receive similar games from their stars.

Brown will be back in Boston on Jan. 21 when the Sixers visit the Celtics. He'll try to even the score.
