#2870. Fernando Mendoza gets 3-word message from Kirk Cousins after Raiders trade

Fernandomania has officially begun in the desert.

With the Las Vegas Raiders trading quarterback Kirk Cousins to the Minnesota Vikings before Tuesday's NFL trade deadline, Las Vegas is expected to turn to Fernando Mendoza, the No. 1 pick in the draft earlier this year, under center.

Mendoza is slated to make his first career start against the defending Super Bowl champion Seattle Seahawks at home on Sunday, Nov. 15 (4:05 ET).

Mendoza's lone regular-season action came during his debut, at the end of a Week 4 road loss to the New England Patriots on Oct. 11. He finished 6-for-8 passing for 48 yards. General manager John Spytek confirmed that Mendoza will be the team's starter moving forward during a news conference Nov. 10.

"He passed every test," Spytek said. "I want to stop short of saying he's earned it, but he put us in a position where we had to make a really hard decision today, with his growth as a person, as a teammate, as a leader, as a quarterback."

Spytek said he never made a call shopping Cousins, and that talks with the Vikings started in earnest Tuesday morning after the Vikings' 23-6 drubbing on "Monday Night Football" at the hands of the Buffalo Bills. The organization was clear with Cousins upon signing him to what is basically a one-year deal in March that Mendoza is obviously the future of the franchise.

Cousins and Spytek met before the trade was agreed to. While it wasn't an easy conversation, Spytek said, it was productive and the Raiders felt like it was important they showed him respect as a 15-year veteran.

"We're all kind of staring the future in the face here with Fernando," said Spytek, in his second year as GM of the "Silver and Black."

Spytek added: "We had to basically decide do we want to go forward here over the last eight games, or do we want to take what Minnesota is offering right now? Ultimately, we decided to do that."

Mendoza's first two opponents boast vaunted defensive units. Seattle's defense, dubbed the "Dark Side" for a reason, did not allow a touchdown last week to the undermanned Arizona Cardinals. Looking ahead, the Raiders face the Denver Broncos on the road in Week 11 then the Cleveland Browns. Denver ranks in the top 10 in points allowed per game (20.7), sacks (26) and takeaways (12).

"Fernando has everything we've asked him to do in practice," head coach Klint Kubiak said. "I think he's exceeded those expectations, and we all know that live bullet Sunday is far different from practice. However, he showed us that he's ready, not only physically but mentally. He's up there studying right now like he would any other game. He puts a lot of work in. His teammates know how prepared he is. It's not just lip service, but he has shown it on the practice field."

Kubiak rattled off the number of ways Cousins influenced Mendoza, the reigning Heisman Trophy winner. The examples he offered included working through a progression, recognizing blitzes, taking care of the body and ways to connect with teammates, Kubiak said, "there's a lot more to playing the position than what your coaches tell you in the meeting room."

"I think Kirk embodied all the right things there, how to prepare and how to be a professional, and really, when things aren't going well, what buttons to push on your teammates and what questions to ask your coaches," Kubiak said. "It's an endless list of all positive things."

On his Instagram story, Cousins offered a goodbye to "Raider Nation" and a message to Mendoza, whom Cousins tagged in the post.

"I feel that Fernando has done a great job," Cousins told reporters Oct. 26. "He has a bright future ahead. I'm going to try and go out there and play as well as I possibly can and try and win and give him a good example to learn from."

Toughening matters for Mendoza on the field will be the lack of a rushing attack for the Raiders. First-round talent Ashton Jeanty from the 2025 draft has not worked out, although rookie Mike Washington has shown some juice in the backfield. Still, Las Vegas ranks 31st in rushing yards per game (71.3) and is last in yards per rush (2.9).

Similar to Cousins, Mendoza will likely have to shoulder much of the output in the Raiders' offense.

"The game has to be balanced," Kubiak said following the team's most recent loss to the San Francisco 49ers that dropped their record to 4-5. "You can't be a running and throwing team when you're throwing it every time. That's not easy.

"You got to start the game a lot faster. You got to go toe-to-toe in the first half so that the second half isn't a throwing fest."

The good news is that tight end Brock Bowers ranks ninth in the league in receiving yards and provides a crucial red-zone target with six touchdowns already this season. Wide receiver Tre Tucker emerged as a reliable downfield option for Cousins, which gives Mendoza more relief in the passing game.

#2869. Former Indiana quarterback Fernando Mendoza to start for Las Vegas Raiders

Fernando Mendoza, the former Indiana football quarterback, Heisman Trophy winner, Big Ten champion and national champion, will start at quarterback for the Las Vegas Raiders, per reporting from ESPN and NFL Network's Ian Rapaport.

The move comes after the Raiders traded starter Kirk Cousins to the Minnesota Vikings just before today's NFL trade deadline. Mendoza, selected by Las Vegas with the first overall pick in this past spring's NFL Draft, has sat behind Cousins for the first nine games of the season and now is set to lead the team for the remainder of the season.

Mendoza made his NFL debut in relief of Cousins in the fourth quarter of a loss against the New England Patriots, completing six of his eight passing attempts for 48 yards.

Trading Cousins says a few things about the Raiders' thinking. For one, Las Vegas has lost five of its past six games after opening the season 3-0. The focus has clearly shifted from winning in 2026 to building for the future, and that means seeing what they have in Mendoza. For another, it says they're comfortable sending Mendoza out there.

He won't have the easiest start. The Raiders return to action this Sunday at home against one of the top defenses in the NFL in the Seattle Seahawks. They follow that up with another top defense in Denver.

#2868. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#2867. Observations after well-balanced Sixers hold off Bulls

Observations after well-balanced Sixers hold off Bulls originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Still no losing streaks to speak of for the 2026-27 Sixers.

The Sixers earned a 127-121 win Sunday over the Bulls to wrap up a 2-1 road trip. They moved to 9-2 overall and Chicago fell to 1-8.

Every Sixers starter scored in double figures.

Three reached the 20-point mark in Jaylen Brown (29 points), Tyrese Maxey (27 points) and LeBron James (22). Joel Embiid had 18 points and 10 rebounds.

Josh Giddey posted 19 points, 15 assists and seven rebounds for the Bulls.

The Sixers were without Adem Bona (cervical spine sprain), Dominick Barlow (lower back stress reaction) and Ariel Hukporti (right Achilles tendon tear). The Bulls' absences included Norman Powell (right shoulder inflammation/soreness) and Zach Collins (respiratory illness).

Next on the Sixers' schedule is a home game Tuesday night vs. the Celtics. Here are observations on their win over Chicago:

Following his 0-for-9 night Friday in the Sixers' loss to the Cavaliers, Embiid did not snap right back into form.

He was well short on his first shot attempt, a wide-open three-pointer. Embiid then failed to score in the post over Jalen Smith.

Embiid got a field goal to go at the 6:42 mark of the first quarter when he tipped in his own miss. However, Smith canned a three on the ensuing possession to give Chicago a 23-16 lead.

James committed two early turnovers, although he did sink a corner three-pointer during a fast-paced first quarter. VJ Edgecombe added two long-range jumpers in the first.

Entering Sunday, the Sixers' minus-9.6 net rating in the first quarter ranked 27th in the NBA. They managed to hold a one-point lead in Chicago after the first.

The Sixers' offensive rebounding was their greatest strength in the first quarter. They poured in 17 second-chance points. Embiid had four offensive boards in the opening frame.

Jabari Walker assumed backup center duties for the second straight game without Bona. He picked up where Embiid left off on the glass, grabbing seven rebounds (three offensive) in a great first stint.

Though he's undersized in the height department for an NBA center at 6-foot-7, Walker has plenty of rebounding tools.

He's listed at a strong 237 pounds, doesn't mind contact whatsoever, hunts the ball, and has a knack for winning contested boards. Walker showed all of that Sunday. Very hard to overstate how well he rebounded.

The Sixers' first-shot offense also had many bright moments in the second quarter.

James got rolling on his way to a 16-point first half, Embiid made a couple of mid-range jumpers, and Maxey hit two free throws put the Sixers up 60-50 late in the second period.

Chicago evened the game at 68-all early in the third quarter on a Matas Buzelis three, but the Bulls couldn't sustain that run.

The Bulls' defense continued to look like one of the worst in the NBA and the Sixers' well-balanced offense kept the scoreboard moving. Brown nailed a turnaround jumper to give his team a double-digit lead. Walker knocked down a top-of-the-key triple to extend the Sixers' advantage to 12 points.

The Sixers had two apparent injury scares in the second half. Brown limped to the sideline with 6:55 to go in the third quarter, but he stayed in the game. Edgecombe headed to the locker room after subbing out at the 9:08 mark of the fourth quarter. He returned to the Sixers' bench and ultimately returned for a final stint.

On the floor, the Sixers couldn't ease to victory. Bulls rookie Caleb Wilson trimmed Chicago's deficit to 116-113 by draining a three. Sixers head coach Nick Nurse called timeout.

The Sixers were efficient enough in the half court down the stretch.

Embiid made a put-back hoop and a pair of free throws. Maxey drilled a side-step three-ball after a wraparound feed from James. The Sixers converted their clutch free throws and secured a winning road trip.

#2866. Traders bet on a pop for Anthropic's historic IPO

News of Anthropic's historic initial public offering provided a wave of relief for tech investors. The response in the perpetual futures market might provide even more relief.

As Anthropic prepares to raise money in its initial public offering this month, traders in offshore markets are making large bets that there will be a big pop on the AI giant's debut.

Trying to guess an initial first day IPO reaction is nearly impossible, but a new and fast-growing corner of the markets might offer some clues.

Pre-IPO perpetuals, contracts that aim to track the price of private companies, trade on decentralized crypto exchanges like Hyperliquid, Coinbase, Binance and OKX to name a few. The current Hyperliquid Anthropic contract implies a stock price of $98 and its market cap at roughly $1.7 trillion, more than 30% higher than the $70-$78 range announced in the company's updated S1.

While trading on the exchange is not currently available to American investors, about $250 million of volume traded across Hyperliquid's two Anthropic trackers in the past 24 hours, according to the exchange.Hyperliquid's pre-IPO Anthropic perp began trading two weeks ago at $138 before settling into a roughly ten-dollar range between $110 and $120 prior to last week's selloff in tech stocks, as the contract slipped below $94 at one point.To proponents of this new asset class, the pricing of the perps is evidence that bankers intentionally undersell equity ahead of IPOs to ensure a post-market "pop." Looking at SpaceX, the argument rings true: pre-IPO perps on SpaceX traded at a premium and converged with equity prices at the IPO, all before shares went on a three-day rally of as much as 40% in the immediate aftermath of the listing.News of Anthropic's upcoming roadshow may be weighing on competitor OpenAI. Perps tracking OpenAI's market cap dropped 7% to $1.27 trillion, according to Hyperliquid trading on a contract trading $2.8 billion in volume over the past 24 hours.Shares of Hyperliquid Strategies (PURR), the publicly-traded Treasury company investing in the HYPE tokens that power the exchange fell 4% Tuesday. The stock is up 150% on the year.

#2865. Las Vegas Raiders get their gold mined by the 49ers

The Las Vegas Raiders traveled to Levi's Stadium in Santa Clara, California, to renew the Battle of the Bay rivalry with the San Francisco 49ers. A rivalry that has meant so much to the two fan bases. Turnovers were the story of this contest. When the clock read zero, the Raiders had committed five turnovers and allowed 30+ points on defense for the sixth straight week.

Kirk Cousins had his worst game as the signal caller for the Raiders. In the first half, he only threw the ball eight times. By the end, Cousins threw three interceptions, with one pick-six after recovering his own fumble. Things simply did not go his way today.

Ashton Jeanty regained his starting position and managed 25 yards on six carries. In the passing game where he has made his largest impact, he caught five passes for -5.0 yards. Mike Washington Jr. was subjected to simply awful run blocking, finishing the game with -3.0 yards on six carries with a long of four yards.

Bowers played his first game near his hometown of Napa, California, with reportedly around 50 friends and family in attendance. He would punish the 49ers, finishing with 111 yards on seven catches, including a 21-yard score. A questionable spot robbed him of a second touchdown.

Early on, Raiders defensive playcaller Rob Leonard continued getting his lunch money stolen by an opposing offensive play caller. The 49ers drove down the field on the opening possession in 12 plays, five passes, seven runs and scored. On the second possession, the 49ers ran the ball down the Raiders' throats. Brock Purdy, the 49ers quarterback, would finish the game with 40 yards rushing on seven carries. Including a rushing touchdown which put the 49ers up 14-0.

By the end of the 49ers third drive the Raiders defense surrendered 94 yards rushing, while being on the field for every play except three with 1:52 seconds to play in the first quarter. What we heard from the television broadcasters was that it was 49ers head coach Kyle Shanahan's goal to run the ball 40 times against the Raiders' putrid run defense.

After going three-and-out on their first drive, Kirk Cousins gets settled in and hits passes to Jack Bech, Brock Bowers, and Jalen Nailor. Driving the Raiders downfield when the end of the quarter makes them switch sides. Cousins finishes the drive off with his team leading with the second rushing touchdown of the year. Drawing the Raiders to within a score.

Raiders' defense continued to allow nearly six yards per carry to the 49ers offense. On 4th and 1, the run blocking of the 49ers knocked the Raiders' defenders three yards off the line of scrimmage. Raiders followed it up by running some type of zone blitz where both defensive tackles dropped into zone coverage and surrendered a passing touchdown to McCaffrey.

After a Raiders offensive possession netting a field goal, the Raiders were down 21-10 and needed a defensive stop to close out the half. Maxx Crosby would get his fourth sack of the season and a false start penalty would end the half. At the end of the half, the 49ers had ran the ball 25 times for 125 yards and two touchdowns.

First play of the second half, Cousins fumbled the football, picked it up and threw a pick-6, 17 seconds into the second half. Exactly what the Raiders could not afford to have happen. To be fair, everything is resting on his shoulders and in order for the Raiders to have any chance of winning any game, he can't afford to make a mistake.

In the retribution drive after the turnover, Cousins came right back, completing seven of seven throws. finishing with a 21-yard touchdown pass to Bowers to level the score at 28-17. Mistakes happen, but atoning for them is critical. With just under 10 minutes remaining in the 3rd quarter, the 49ers offense would take the field for the first time in the second half.

Treydan Stukes, the Raiders' second-round pick, fooled Purdy, intercepted the pass, tried to fall numerous times with no one around to tackle him and stumbled back to the 30-yard line of the 49ers. Momentum shifts are a huge thing, giving the Raiders an opportunity to get it back to a one-score game. However, in the most self-sabotaging way ever, Cousins nearly threw an interception to a defensive lineman; the very next play, Fred Warner peanut-punches the ball out of Bowers hands and the 49ers recover the ball.

Four plays later, the Raiders get a 4th-and-short stop and a turnover on downs for the first time in a long time. Five turnovers were committed by both teams in the third quarter alone.

On the next drive, Cousins hits Michael Mayer for a nice gain and as he fought to reach the goal line, disaster struck. Mayer was tackled and fell on top of a defender, while another 49ers defender punched the ball out of his hands just before he crossed the goal line. Negating a potential score, turning the ball over yet again, giving the 49ers a touchback.

From there, the 49ers would drive past midfield with a few runs. But the lack of a Raiders pass rush gave Purdy enough time to find Jordan Watkins open for a 37-yard touchdown catch.

On the ensuing possession, the Raiders drove to the goal line again, behind a 33-yard catch-and-run from Bowers that appeared to stretch the ball over the pylon but was ruled out of bounds. Cousins' quarterback sneak was stuffed when Warner leaped over the pile after the snap. On the next play, Mayer was called for offensive pass interference, negating his own touchdown catch. Followed up with Cousins' second interception of the game, the second turnover inside the red zone, turnover number four and the third straight turnover.

But the hits just kept on coming; in junk time, Cousins would throw his third interception of the day, fourth straight turnover and fifth total. Cousins had Tre Tucker open deep, loaded up, putting his full body into the throw, only for it to fall short and into the waiting arms of a 49ers defender, mercifully allowing this game to come to an end.

#2864. Observations after Sixers suffer blowout loss to Cavs, fall in NBA Cup

Observations after Sixers suffer blowout loss to Cavs, fall in NBA Cup originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers couldn't rekindle the magic of their comeback home-opening win over the Cavaliers.

They fell Friday night in NBA Cup action, suffering a 119-95 defeat in Cleveland. The Sixers dipped to 8-2 and the Cavs improved to 5-4.

The Sixers' top scorers were Jaylen Brown (20 points), VJ Edgecombe (19 points) and Tyrese Maxey (17).

The Cavs got 23 points from Donovan Mitchell and 22 from Evan Mobley.

The Sixers' injured players included center Adem Bona (cervical spine sprain). Cleveland was down big man Jarrett Allen (right knee contusion). Sam Merrill sat out the second half with a right elbow injury.

With Friday's loss, the Sixers are 1-1 in Eastern Conference Group B of the NBA Cup. Their next Cup game is scheduled for Nov. 25 against the Heat.

The Sixers will conclude their three-road trip Sunday afternoon against the Bulls. Here are observations on their blowout loss to Cleveland:

After a missed Merrill three-pointer, the Sixers steamed ahead on the fast break and Edgecombe opened the scoring with a slam.

The Cavs replied with a 15-0 run kickstarted by six straight Mitchell points. Much like in the Sixers' Oct. 22 win over Cleveland, they were many levels below their best in the first quarter.

The Sixers began 0 for 4 from three-point range, including two LeBron James misses. The Cavs were also the better team in transition and limited the Sixers' chances to attack in the open floor by taking excellent care of the ball in the early going. Edgecombe was called for his second foul with 7:34 left in the first quarter.

Meanwhile, Joel Embiid expressed his frustration about a couple of early no-calls. Embiid's larger problem was that he couldn't hit any sort of shot besides free throws.

He went 0 for 9 from the floor in the game. The seven-time All-Star finished with six points and seven rebounds.

Embiid has had a subpar shooting start to the year, especially from three-point territory — he's now 3 for 28 (10.7 percent) — but Friday was the kind of extreme night that's bound to happen here and there over the course of a long season.

Of course, it's notable that Embiid's been playing through right foot plantar fasciitis and the Sixers have been keeping a close eye on his minutes. We'll see how the team manages his availability moving forward and whether Embiid is able to bounce back next time out.

James didn't play anywhere near his finest game either. He had eight points on 3-for-12 shooting, eight rebounds, three assists and six turnovers.

As a team, the Sixers were 36.5 percent from the field and 25 percent beyond the arc.

Jabari Walker rejoined the Sixers' rotation as the backup center with Bona sidelined. Anfernee Simons, former Cav Dean Wade and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope all remained rotation fixtures.

With Walker and Caldwell-Pope on the court next to three starters, the Sixers cut their deficit to single digits in the second quarter. Walker threw down a fast-break dunk that cut Cleveland's lead to 48-41. He gave the Sixers some good defensive possessions when switched on to James Harden and provided his usual physical, high-effort work on the glass.

Edgecombe was fantastic in the second, sinking three triples, racking up 15 points and nabbing three steals.

Many second-year players would have a tough time finding a rhythm after encountering early foul trouble, but Edgecombe was great as soon as he checked back in and delivered a ton of valuable buckets in the Sixers' comeback push.

The Sixers pulled to within 66-63 when Maxey picked a steal and threw a pretty one-handed bounce pass to Edgecombe for a dunk.

With Embiid and James offering little as scorers, Brown hit several shots that helped the Sixers stay in the game. After his 30-point performance last time out against the Pistons, Brown reached the 20-point mark for a third consecutive game.

Still, the Sixers' offense never got rolling. The Cavs maintained their lead and extended it late in the third quarter. By early in the fourth, the Sixers trailed by over 20 points.

Embiid didn't play the fourth quarter and logged 25 minutes. He's had zero made field goals on just one other occasion in his career, back in Nov. 2019 against the Raptors.

Even with their tremendous first nine games, the Sixers knew they'd hit speed bumps throughout the year. They'll try to get back on track Sunday in Chicago.

#2863. Op-ed: In Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's alleged China spy scandal, there's plenty of blame to go around

To learn more about the CNBC CFO Council, visit cnbccouncils.com/cfo

In June 2013, President Barack Obama welcomed Chinese President Xi Jinping to Sunnylands, a secluded California estate, for an informal summit. Behind the diplomatic pleasantries, Obama confronted Xi over Chinese state-directed cyber-enabled espionage and theft of American intellectual property, warning that such activities threatened American companies and the broader bilateral relationship.

Thirteen years after the Sunnylands Summit, the threat has evolved significantly as the race to dominate AI frames the U.S.-China relationship. The stakes are higher, the technology more consequential, and the vulnerabilities increasingly involve attempts to compromise or coopt the "key man" at the helm of the nation's top technology companies.

With the unfolding scandal involving Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, the blame should start with the CEO himself, but it should not end there. The situation illustrates a failure of Silicon Valley, corporate and political leadership at a much wider level.

Huang has acknowledged an extramarital affair with a woman identified in reporting as Alice Huang, a former executive at Singapore-based Megaspeed, an Nvidia customer. American intelligence officials reportedly believe she was recruited by China's Ministry of State Security to gather information and potentially influence Nvidia's efforts to loosen American semiconductor export restrictions. China denies the allegations.

Nvidia's independent directors have launched an investigation, and so far, they say no compromise of company secrets or national security information has been established. But the problem extends well beyond Huang or Nvidia. Focusing exclusively on whether proprietary technology or trade secrets were transferred fundamentally misunderstands the potential intelligence value of such an operation.

For Beijing, gaining insight into, influencing, or potentially compromising American technology policymaking could be equally valuable. Advance knowledge of export-control deliberations, regulatory intentions, negotiating positions, or divisions within the administration could provide strategic advantages without a single semiconductor design being stolen.

The Trump administration reportedly received warnings about the relationship before the scandal became public. Nevertheless, Huang remained an influential participant in semiconductor policy discussions while advocating greater access to China's market.

New reporting also raises questions about interactions between Nvidia and Commerce Department officials during an investigation into Megaspeed, subsequently transferred to the Justice Department. No improper influence has been established, but corporate access to government decision-making deserves scrutiny alongside proprietary technology.

Whether appropriate protective measures were taken remains unclear. President Donald Trump's public defense of Huang — saying that Huang told him he had no idea the woman could be a spy and that he trusted the CEO, and that if national intelligence had warned the president about this threat, as has been reported, he didn't remember — hardly conveyed the urgency warranted by allegations of a foreign intelligence operation targeting a strategically indispensable American company.

Trump has met Xi three times since returning to office, with two additional encounters anticipated in November and December. These meetings offer Trump an opportunity to follow Obama's example by confronting Xi directly over Chinese state-directed or sponsored espionage targeting American technology companies, their executives, and potentially the U.S. policymaking process.

It is also time for a more formal check from Congress on Silicon Valley, which has long celebrated the visionary founder, the indispensable executive whose genius supposedly transcends conventional corporate management and traditional corporate governance. Investors have rewarded this concentration of authority, while boards have all too frequently accommodated it.

Corporate governance professionals call this "Key Man" risk: excessive organizational dependence on an individual whose departure, financial misconduct, or moral compromise could materially damage the enterprise. In the artificial intelligence era, this risk becomes considerably more dangerous. When a handful of executives control technologies central to America's economic competitiveness, military capabilities, and intelligence advantages, their personal vulnerabilities become potential national security vulnerabilities.

When those executives also enjoy extraordinary access to the White House and senior policymakers, they become potential conduits for foreign intelligence collection and influence directed not simply at their companies, but at the American people and American policy itself.

Congress has opportunities to strengthen America's defenses and must act immediately.

The AI OVERWATCH Act would strengthen oversight of advanced semiconductor exports and associated national security licensing conditions.

The MATCH Act would address weaknesses in restrictions on advanced semiconductor manufacturing equipment, including foreign suppliers and servicing arrangements.

The Chip Security Act would strengthen mechanisms for tracking advanced chips and detecting diversion.

Importantly, the Remote Access Security Act would extend export-control authorities to certain remote access arrangements, including cloud computing, addressing the possibility that restricted American technological capabilities could be accessed without physically exporting the underlying equipment.

Together, these measures address important technological and regulatory gaps and vulnerabilities. But controlling chips, equipment, and computing access admittedly will not solve the human vulnerabilities problem — the so-called "Key Man" risks to U.S. technology supremacy and national security.

Most of these bills have a good amount of Congressional bipartisan support. However, they have encountered stiff resistance from the technology industry and their lobbyists, and some very detailed disagreements over specifics of export-control policy. There has also been uncertainty about White House support for these bills and an unwillingness by the Republican-controlled Congress to get on the wrong side of the White House. However, there is no doubt in my mind the Nvidia controversy has given supporters a new argument for action. The Democratic takeover of both chambers could further strengthen congressional oversight and an appetite for codifying much of this into law using these pieces of legislation.

There should also be a serious discussion among key U.S. government and corporate governance experts about whether strategically important technology companies need explicit board-level counterintelligence responsibilities, independent executive-security assessments, and confidential mechanisms for reporting foreign intelligence approaches to the appropriate channels.

This is not about morally policing executives' private lives. It is about recognizing that individuals controlling America's most sensitive technologies, and influencing the policies governing them, are valuable intelligence targets and assets if compromised.

Unfortunately, the Trump administration's broader philosophy appears to emphasize voluntary industry commitments and confidence in technology executives' ability to police themselves. Morality is not a governance system. Personal assurances are not counterintelligence safeguards. Importantly, shareholder interests are not always synonymous with the national interest.

Obama understood at Sunnylands that protecting American innovation required confronting China directly. That engagement helped to clearly articulate American redlines against cyber-enabled theft of intellectual property for commercial advantage.

Trump now has an opportunity, and a responsibility, to extend that principle to today's intelligence threats. At his forthcoming meetings with Xi, he should make clear that state-directed or sponsored operations targeting American technology executives and attempts to compromise U.S. technology policymaking are unacceptable and carry consequences for the bilateral relationship.

But confronting Beijing is only half the responsibility. Washington must also confront weaknesses within the body of laws governing technology access and must weigh in on America's corporate institutions and governance.

America cannot declare artificial intelligence essential to national security while leaving protection of its technology, executives, and policymaking process primarily to corporate discretion. The greatest vulnerability in Silicon Valley may not be the technology we export, but the people and governance systems entrusted to protect it, and the policies determining who gets access to it.

Neither Congress nor the White House should be comfortable leaving that responsibility to Silicon Valley's own moral code.

—By Dewardric McNeal, Managing Director and Senior Policy Analyst at Longview Global, and a CNBC Contributor. He worked in the U.S. Department of Defense as an East Asia and China security relations expert during the Obama administration.

#2862. Does 49ers trade for Keenan Allen solidify their Super Bowl hopes?

The San Francisco 49ers acquired veteran wide receiver Keenan Allen in a trade with the Indianapolis Colts for a 2027 sixth-round pick just hours before the NFL trade deadline on Nov. 10.

It's no secret around the NFL that the Niners have a shortage of available wideouts, making the addition of Allen all the more vital to San Francisco's passing game.

The 49ers have been dealt injuries throughout the season Mike Evans, Deebo Samuel Sr., De'Zhaun Stribling, Ricky Pearsall, Demarcus Robinson and Christian Kirk. Each week, there has been someone in the Niners' WR room who steps up to the mantle. Now, Allen gets his turn as San Francisco found their plug-in at receiver.

He returns to the Bay Area having played his collegiate years at Cal from 2010 to 2012. From Cal, he was selected by San Diego Chargers with the 76th overall pick in the third round of the 2013 NFL Draft. Allen was runner-up for Offensive Rookie of the Year but he developed into the Chargers' No. 1-receiver. He would become a six-time Pro Bowl selection in 10 seasons, even as the team moved to Los Angeles.

He spent a season with the Chicago Bears in 2024, then returned to LA for another season with the Chargers. He signed with Indianapolis during the offseason.

With Allen back on the West Coast, the hope is to earn the league's most prized piece of jewelry.

Does Allen have enough left in the tank to help bring a Super Bowl to the Bay Area?

Allen comes to San Francisco as a 34-year-old veteran receiver in his 14th NFL season, which bears the question of if he has enough in the tank to be a contributing piece to the 49ers offense. Allen is added to a star-studded wide receiver room, but still, looming injuries loom are a cause of concern, at least until guys start trending upward.

He'll likely come in as a starter. Allen is known for his elite route-running, high IQ and superb ball-tracking and catching ability. Nothing thrown Allen's way gets dropped.

Allen caught 40 balls for 351 yards and 1 touchdown in eight games with the Colts during the 2026 NFL season. He's shared 22% of targets and has a nearly 69% catch rating. He averages 8.8 yards per catch.

His addition provides Brock Purdy with another weapon to find trust in, especially while the San Francisco receiver room slowly gets healthy. Adding Allen means the team doesn't necessarily have to rush guys back to the field.

Plus, Jordan Watkins is coming off a week where he caught four passes for 86 yards and a touchdown. He continues to improve. Having a threat like Allen alongside Watkins will likely open things up for himself.

The 49ers are still the favorite to win the Super Bowl with +550 odds, according to BetMGM, currently tied with the Buffalo Bills.

For San Francisco, the team's hopes will ride on health and availability. Allen isn't a young buck anymore at 34. Example A, Evans is 33 years old and look how much time he's missed.

No question at what the 49ers will get from the vets on the field, it's a matter of getting them on the field at 100%. Allen has been relatively healthy in 2026 and that's good news for San Francisco.

Allen appeared in every game, except one, for the Colts having missed Week 4 with a groin injury. Since then, he's been dependable, especially production wise. Maybe not the fantasy pickup he once was, but Allen does the things that contributes to winning football games.

#2861. Back in Philadelphia with Celtics, Paul George looks forward to ‘a regular game'

Back in Philadelphia with Celtics, Paul George looks forward to 'a regular game' originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Paul George's presence back in town was one of the many swirling morning storylines at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

For good reason, former Celtics teammates Jaylen Brown and Jayson Tatum will draw immense attention Tuesday night. After all, Brown has said there "might be a little lamb chops with some gravy" between the two, if not a full-blown beef.

But George's return to Philadelphia is more than a tiny subplot. Following two seasons in Philadelphia, he was the centerpiece of the Sixers' summer trade package to acquire Brown.

George told reporters before the Celtics' shootaround that it feels like "a regular game."

"Had I been here eight, nine, 10 years, different story," he said. "I was here two years. Obviously, we didn't have the best of luck or had a great, I think, partnership in terms of my ability to stay healthy and on the floor. So that kind of played into a lot of it.

"But obviously, it's a tremendous fan base, a great sports town to play in. So that was appreciated. There's no ill will for me. It'll be fun to just compete. And outside of that, it's a normal game."

The 36-year-old George indeed had trouble staying available for sustained stretches as a Sixer. He dealt with a variety of injuries and served a 25-game suspension last season for violating the NBA's anti-drug policy.

George did play well on both ends of the floor for much of the 2026 playoffs. He helped the Sixers come back to win a seven-game first-round series against the Celtics in historic fashion. The nine-time All-Star also brought a veteran steadiness to the Sixers' locker room and was glad to mentor younger players like VJ Edgecombe.

"Just his routine … even when he got hot, he didn't try to force it," Jabari Walker said Tuesday morning. "His game was smooth and I think that's how he is as a person, just not forcing anything and having a calm energy."

George has certainly enjoyed his early days as a Celtic.

Through nine games, he's averaged 20.4 points, 5.2 rebounds, 4.1 assists and 2.3 steals while shooting 42.5 percent from three-point range. Boston will enter Tuesday night's matchup second in the Eastern Conference standings at 8-2, the Sixers first at 9-2.

"I've been kind of blown away," George said. "I think it exceeded my expectations since being in Boston. You never know how much culture is involved and how much culture changes your outlook on things and the effect it has mentally, physically, just being in an environment … and the culture that Boston presents.

"So I've been thrilled. I've been playing some of my best basketball of late, and just been enjoying grinding it out with these guys out here. It's a fun style of play and I can't talk (enough) about just the thrill, the happiness and the excitement and joy that it has brought being here."

He was asked what sort of reception he expects from Sixers fans.

"Not sure," George said. "In some ways, I helped land them a future piece for them. So with that, not sure how the reception would be."

#2860. Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."

#2859. Tulane Basketball Outlasts Nicholls for Second Win of Season

The Tulane Green Wave (2-1) picked up its strongest win of the season on the hardwood, taking down the Nicholls Colonels (1-2) by a score of 77-63 on Tuesday night at Avron B. Fogelman Arena at the Devlin Fieldhouse.

Tulane fell behind by as many as five points in the first few minutes of action, but it wouldn't take long for the team to find a way back into the contest. The Green Wave built a lead through the first half, and though the Colonels neared a comeback a few times, Tulane managed to take a 35-24 lead into the locker room.

However, Nicholls would not go down without a fight. The visiting squad roared out of the locker room and trailed by just one point with a little more than 12 minutes left in the game. Tulane would go on a hot streak to push the lead right back out, fending the Colonels off as the minutes ticked down. The team led by as many as 16 points in the frame, ultimately reigning victorious in the end.

Krystian Lewis continued to make a name for himself in the Olive Green and Sky Blue, scoring 35 points, including four three-pointers, and seven rebounds. The junior guard also went a perfect 17-for-17 at the charity stripe, capitalizing on his ability to take tough contact and draw fouls. Tyler Ringgold was the only other Green Wave player to score in double figures with 12 points and six rebounds of his own.

Carson Schwieger added eight points and three rebounds, while Joah Chappelle had a team-high eight boards and seven points. KJ Greene led the team with four assists and six points. TJ Nadeau and Josiah Moore combined for nine points to round out the scoring for the home team.

As a whole, Tulane's offense shot 43 percent from the floor, including an impressive 50 percent from beyond the arch. The team outperformed Nicholls on the boards with 34 rebounds to 29, and it kept itself out of trouble with just 14 fouls committed on the night. If there's one area that the Green Wave was dominated in, it was points in the paint: Nicholls found a way to battle through for 34 of those points, compared to just 18 from Tulane. That's an indication that the Wave needs to toughen up a bit defensively down low.

Up next, Tulane will face its first road test of the season as it travels to Ohio to face the Akron Zips. Tip-off is set for 11 a.m. on Saturday, November 14th.

#2858. Week 10 Fantasy Football Waiver Wire: Corey Kiner, Tank Dell among top pickups

Every Monday during the season, Justin Boone breaks down the recommended waiver-wire pickups.

Roster percentages are generated from Yahoo leagues. Free-Agent Budget (FAB) amounts are based on a $100 salary cap. Only players rostered in less than 50% of leagues are considered.

With Kirk Cousins heading back to Minnesota, the Raiders can finally put their first-overall pick under center. While you're not going to want to use Mendoza against the Seahawks defense this week, his outlook for the rest of the season is quite promising as a highly-drafted pick attached to Klint Kubiak.

The Heisman Trophy winner proved himself to be a good leader in college with excellent on-field awareness and the toughness needed to overcome adversity during games. He has a strong arm to make any throw, but it's his touch passing that stood out. Though Mendoza's not fast enough to be a meaningful runner in the pros, he has enough speed to scramble his way out of trouble and pick up yards on the ground. This all amounts to an intriguing fantasy QB2 with room to grow.

The quarterback position is very picked over at the moment. Malik Willis was featured in this spot last week, but he's now playing through a groin injury that's limited his abilities as a runner and neutered his fantasy potential. So, all that's left are uncomfortable streaming options.

The Steelers are coming off their bye week, which should help the 42-year-old Rodgers (turns 43 in December) feel refreshed for a date with the division rival Bengals.

Rodgers hasn't had many favorable opponents this season, but he did take advantage of his easiest matchup. That just happened to be against the Bengals in Week 3, when the veteran passed for 292 yards and three touchdowns en route to a QB8 finish.

In his last visit to Cincinnati, Rodgers threw four touchdowns back in Week 7 of the 2025 campaign. He's not a slam-dunk streamer, but he's a solid one given the options this week.

Desperation streaming options (if healthy)

Desperation streaming options (if starter is out)

The elite backup RBs who are one injury away from stardom should be rostered everywhere and I'll continue to highlight them in this space until they no longer qualify for this article.

It was clear early in the season that Johnson was one of the best backups to roster, in case anything ever happens to Kenneth Walker III. In recent weeks, Johnson's value has increased even more now that he's offering weekly flex production.

The rookie posted a fluky 128 receiving yards and a score in Week 8, but he followed it up with 34 yards and another touchdown on Sunday. Upcoming matchups with the Falcons, Cardinals and Bills are all spots where Johnson could be a viable flex play while also offering league-winning upside if Walker ever misses time.

Black is in the same situation as Johnson; you can basically just swap Walker's name with Christian McCaffrey's in the previous section.

The 49ers' rookie plays in one of the best offenses in the league and has flashed his potential with at least 60 rushing yards in three of his first eight games, while serving as CMC's direct backup. That includes the past two games, when Black turned 12 touches into 63 yards and a TD against the Falcons, as well as Sunday's outing, which resulted in 11 carries for a season-high 70 yards.

Next up is the Cowboys defense, which is a top-five matchup for fantasy running backs. The 49ers are 4-point favorites in a game with the highest projected point total in Week 10 at 55.5.

Stashing a talented No. 2 back playing behind a 30-year-old starter who handled 450 touches last year is just an obvious move. Black should be rostered everywhere as a flex option with RB1 upside if McCaffrey were ever sidelined.

Rhamondre Stevenson left Sunday's game with a knee injury and was very quickly ruled out. Mike Vrabel didn't have an update Monday morning, but did acknowledge that his back was undergoing an MRI. With TreVeyon Henderson already sidelined indefinitely, that leaves Kiner as the next man up.

Though Kiner hasn't been asked to do much as a pass-catcher, the 2025 UDFA has shown some juice in the run game by topping 50 yards in three of his last five outings in a change-of-pace role.

If Stevenson can't suit up in Week 10, which seems unlikely due to news that he'll probably miss some time, Kiner immediately becomes a high-end fantasy RB3 with the usual touchdown upside that comes with a starter's workload. We can't lock that outcome in, though until we get a proper update on Stevenson's injury.

Josh Jacobs fractured his finger and is set to undergo surgery that will keep him out a maximum of three games.

MarShawn Lloyd missed Week 9 due to a knee injury, which allowed Johnson to step up in Jacobs' absence. Johnson finished with 68 yards and a touchdown, making him the RB11 on the week with just MNF left to be played.

If you're wondering why Johnson isn't higher on this list, just take a look at Green Bay's upcoming schedule. The Packers face the Vikings this week, then have a bye before taking on the Rams.

So, even with Jacobs sidelined, this isn't the ideal setup for a spot starter. On top of that, we aren't sure if/when Lloyd will be available. If Lloyd is active in Week 10, it only further muddies the projections.

If Lloyd remains out, Johnson would be a risky RB3/flex versus the Vikings. If Lloyd plays, then both players would be risky flex options.

With the Saints cutting Kendre Miller, Estimé stepped into a significant role in his first game since joining the Saints. The result was a 47% snap share, which led to 15 carries for 72 yards and a touchdown, as well as another five yards on a catch.

Estimé's presence helped kickstart New Orleans' rushing attack, but his fantasy outlook is directly tied to Travis Etienne Jr.'s health. Etienne, who can be activated off IR at any time, wasn't ready to practice last week. Once we see him back on the field, it will severely diminish Estimé's fantasy value.

However, if Etienne misses another week, fantasy managers will be able to start Estimé as a fringe low-end RB2.

Chris Rodriguez Jr. at TEN (33% rostered)

Sione Vaki vs. NE (Germany) (2% rostered)

Dart-throw flex options (if starter is out)

DeMario Douglas has always been a risky receiver to put in your fantasy lineup due to his low floor. We witnessed that occur in Week 7, when he only delivered three catches for 23 yards.

However, Douglas has posted at least 12 fantasy points in his other four outings over the last five weeks, finishing as a top-10 WR three times. After hauling in five of his nine targets for 102 yards in Week 9, he currently sits as the WR23 with just Monday Night Football remaining.

Four touchdowns during this stretch have helped his cause, but he's come away with very respectable yardage totals in these games with at least 65 in each.

A.J. Brown's recovery timeline remains uncertain and with a bye looming, it seems likely we won't see him until Week 12 at the earliest. Fantasy managers can continue to take advantage by starting Douglas as a WR3/flex option with temporary upside.

Dell's return from a near career-ending injury is one of the best stories in the NFL this season. The fact that he's been productive since getting back on the field only makes it more incredible.

While his 43-yard catch on one target in Week 7 was a bit fluky, it's hard to ignore a nine-target outing this past week. Dell finished the day with four grabs, 105 yards and a score and looked good in the process. However, he still only ran 50% of the routes in a Houston passing attack that's known for rotating receivers behind Nico Collins. We'll need to see more from Dell before we can trust him as an every-week fantasy starter, but he's done enough to warrant being added in most formats as a potential boom-or-bust WR3/flex.

After back-to-back games with at least 50 yards, it was discouraging to see Williams underwhelm with just three catches for 25 yards this week. I'm still holding out hope though.

The rookie continues to play the most snaps and run the most routes in the Commanders' receiver room. This was the third straight outing where Williams accomplished that feat, following Terry McLaurin's season-ending injury.

It's possible Williams levels off as a low-ceiling WR4/flex option for fantasy, but I'm holding for at least another week. The talent is evident and Washington has one of the top-10 easiest schedules for fantasy receivers the rest of the way.

Douglas has played four games this year that weren't impacted by injury. In those contests, the rookie has posted stat lines of 5-94-0, 2-70-2, 4-43-0 and 6-70-0.

With Malik Willis heading to IR, Douglas's fantasy ceiling takes a hit. However, he's still an intriguing rookie who is emerging as the No. 1 target in his offense.

When a first-year player is showing this kind of production early in his career, it's worth taking notice. Even with Kyle McCord under center, Douglas can be started as a WR4/flex given his intriguing matchups against the Colts and Bills the next two weeks.

It's easy to forget, but Bryant had a growing role in the Broncos' passing attack during the opening month before being placed on injured reserve with an ankle injury. In those first four games, Bryant topped 40 yards three times.

So, it was great to see him post a season-high 56 yards and a touchdown on four catches in his first game back since the injury. Jaylen Waddle also suffered a concussion, but he should be cleared after their Week 10 bye.

Regardless, Bryant was a borderline flex option early in the season and has returned with an even better performance. Given Waddle's struggles and Courtland Sutton's age, there's a non-zero chance Bryant becomes the most valuable fantasy receiver in Denver for the rest of the season.

Watkins' fantasy value is difficult to assess because it's entirely dependent on the health of the other receivers in San Francisco.

Keenan Allen was acquired at the trade deadline, but most of the other wideouts on the roster are injured. Deebo Samuel Sr. is nearing a return from a hamstring injury that kept him out for the last month. Mike Evans' Achilles tendinitis is an ongoing problem with no clear recovery timeline. I'll be surprised if he suits up in Week 10. Demarcus Robinson is apparently playing at less than 100%, while De'Zhaun Stribling and Christian Kirk are on IR.

That opened the door for Watkins, who put up four receptions, 86 yards and a touchdown versus the Raiders. That's his second consecutive quality stat line, after a 56-yard performance versus the Falcons prior to the Niners' Week 8 bye.

If everything remains the same with Evans and Deebo sitting out, Watkins will be a boom-or-bust WR4/flex option in a good matchup with the Cowboys this week. However, once Allen is up to speed and the other receivers are healthy, Watkins won't be worth rostering in redraft leagues.

#2857. Jim Cramer says Anthropic's explosive growth could justify a record-breaking IPO

Anthropic's stunning revenue growth helps justify its $1 trillion-plus valuation ahead of what could become the largest initial public offering in history, CNBC's Jim Cramer said Tuesday.

The Claude developer filed updated IPO paperwork Tuesday outlining plans to offer 825 million shares at $70 to $78 apiece. At the high end, the offering would raise $64.35 billion and value Anthropic at up to $1.405 trillion on a fully diluted basis. The company plans to price its shares Nov. 18 and begin trading the following day. Throw in a separate $26 billion private placement purchased by leading AI companies including Nvidia and Alphabet, and Anthropic's entire haul could exceed SpaceX's raise from its June IPO.

"The fact that this deal is on is bullish for tech and the AI trade," the "Mad Money" host said.

Anthropic reported $33.2 billion in revenue during the first nine months of 2026, up 1,206% from a year earlier, according to its updated prospectus. Much of that growth has been driven by enterprise customers, particularly surging adoption of Claude Code, its AI coding agent. "Anthropic's tantalizing growth rate is the key reason why this should be the largest IPO in history," Cramer said.

The company now has more than 11,900 customers spending over $100,000 annually, up from roughly 1,500 at the end of 2025. Existing customers are also spending dramatically more, with Anthropic reporting a net revenue retention rate of approximately 900% at the end of September, meaning customers are spending roughly nine times as much as a year earlier.

While Anthropic is seeing accelerating revenue growth through the first nine months of the year, the company's quarter-over-quarter growth rate seemed to peak in the second quarter, slowing to 47% in the third quarter from 144% in Q2. "To some extent, it's the law of large numbers at work, and let's be clear that 47% sequential growth is still very impressive," Cramer said.

While some deceleration is inevitable as the company gets larger, he said that increased competition from OpenAI and the possibility that customers are beginning to scrutinize their AI spending could also be factors. One thing is for sure, Anthropic won't have to jockey for position with OpenAI, which pushed its IPO plans into next year.

Additionally, Anthropic's annualized revenue run rate, which projects yearly revenue based on the most recent month's sales, suggests demand remains strong. The frontier AI lab reported an annualized revenue run rate of $81 billion at the end of October, putting the company within striking distance of $100 billion by year-end if its current momentum continues.

"It's good to see that it's still growing nicely," Cramer said. "As for Anthropic's revenue and growth rate, the main subject of tonight's review, it's nothing less extraordinary, even if we are slightly past the peak, growth-wise."

#2856. ‘Let's check the ball up': Jaylen Brown ready for first game against Celtics

'Let's check the ball up': Jaylen Brown ready for first game against Celtics originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Jaylen Brown was asked Sunday whether he and Jayson Tatum had spoken since the former teammates addressed their relationship during the preseason.

Does he plan to approach Tatum before Tuesday night's game at Xfinity Mobile Arena?

"I mean, he's on his side, I'm on my side," Brown said. "We're playing basketball. Both of us are trying to win. So it's nothing to talk about. Let's check the ball up."

After months of public comments, documentaries and questions about how their partnership ended, the basketball will finally take over.

Brown will face Boston for the first time since the Celtics traded him to Philadelphia for Paul George and four future draft assets in July. He spent his first 10 NBA seasons in Boston, played nine alongside Tatum and helped the Celtics win the 2024 championship, earning Finals MVP honors.

Tuesday's 8 p.m. game on NBC will also bring together the top two teams in the Eastern Conference. The Sixers are 9-2. Boston, riding a three-game winning streak, is 8-2 and sits a half-game behind Philadelphia.

"Another good opportunity for our team to be able to show what we're made of," Brown said. "I'm looking forward to it. Big-time matchup, two heavyweights in the East. Let's get it."

Brown has not hidden his appreciation for Boston, even as more has emerged about the deterioration of his relationship with Tatum and the organization.

"I love Boston," he said Sunday. "Boston's a great city. A lot of people I developed relationships with, you know what I mean? But I'm just focused on what's in front of me right now. And that's the most important thing."

He also acknowledged everything that accompanies an abrupt end to a decade with one organization.

"Decisions were made," Brown said. "It's life. It's business. Move on. It's OK. … It's emotions. You've got to pack up and move your stuff. Your family's got to pack up and move. But it's life. It's happened to numerous amounts of people, myself included.

"So I just look forward to being able to build with my team over here, trying to make the best of my situation."

Brown faced the Celtics during the preseason, but Tuesday will be the first meeting that counts. He said he did not know whether that would introduce emotions that were absent during the exhibition schedule.

"We'll see how I feel when we get out there," Brown said. "As of now, I've got no emotion toward nothing. So I'm just ready to go."

Tatum was similarly restrained after Boston's 117-97 victory over Milwaukee on Sunday.

"It's another game on the schedule," Tatum said. "It's the Celtics versus the Sixers. In the words of Brad Stevens, they all count as one."

Tatum called the Sixers "an incredible group" with a "great coaching staff," but declined to add to the discussion surrounding his relationship with Brown.

"Drama gets clicks and views," he said. "That's not my thing." CBS Sports reported Tatum's full comments Sunday night.

Celtics head coach Joe Mazzulla was even more concise.

"It'll be fun," Mazzulla said. "It'll be a good atmosphere."

Brown is not the only centerpiece of the July deal who will face his former team.

George has settled quickly into Boston's lineup, averaging 20.4 points and supplying the high-volume outside shooting the Celtics have long prioritized. He scored 27 points and made five three-pointers Sunday as Boston beat Milwaukee for its third consecutive victory.

The Celtics have not needed George to duplicate everything Brown provided. He has fit alongside Tatum, Derrick White and Boston's returning core as another willing shooter and versatile defender.

Tatum is averaging 27.1 points, six rebounds and 7.8 assists. His playmaking has been especially important for a team that replaced Brown's downhill pressure with more shooting and ball movement.

Boston enters Tuesday ranked third in offensive rating and fourth in defensive rating. It is one of two NBA teams currently inside the top five on both ends of the floor.

The Celtics have also made 15.6 three-pointers per game, the second-highest average in the league. They set an NBA Cup record by hitting 24 against Charlotte on Friday, then made 18 more Sunday in Milwaukee.

That presents a clear challenge for a Sixers defense that has generally performed well but has occasionally surrendered clean perimeter looks after committing extra help toward the ball. Tatum, George, White, Payton Pritchard and Sam Hauser can all punish late rotations.

The Sixers have reached 9-2 without depending upon the same formula every night.

Tyrese Maxey leads them at 26.9 points per game. Brown is averaging 22.3 points, 6.7 rebounds and four assists while shooting 47.7 percent from the field and 39.2 percent from three. Joel Embiid and LeBron James have supplied scoring, passing and defensive flexibility around them.

Brown has repeatedly described the team as unfinished despite its record.

"From our perspective, it's been some growing pains for sure, even though we've been able to win," he said. "The result, we're not so much fixated on the result. It's more so about the process, and we've got to improve our process and our mentality to creating a winning environment."

Boston should provide the clearest early measure of that process.

The Celtics play considerably slower than the Sixers, but their half-court execution has been more efficient. Philadelphia will want to create turnovers and run before Boston can set its defense. The Celtics will attempt to control the pace, generate three-pointers and make the Sixers execute against a defense that has yet to allow an opposing player to score 30 points this season.

There is also the unavoidable Brown matchup. Boston could use Tatum, George and White against him at different points, giving the Celtics several defenders capable of absorbing his drives without immediately sending help.

Nick Nurse expects Brown to bring something extra, even if Brown has publicly downplayed the emotional component.

"It's going to be very different for him," Nurse said. "I think probably a little bit of emotion in there, too, but also I think he's going to be very motivated to play."

Boston's rotation is healthy entering the matchup. Two-way guard Devin Carter is out because of an illness.

Dominick Barlow and Ariel Hukporti remain out for the Sixers. Adem Bona could return after missing Sunday's contest.

The betting market views the matchup as essentially a toss-up. Monday afternoon listings had the spread moving between Boston minus-1.5 and Philadelphia minus-1.5, depending on the sportsbook, with the total at 215.5.

That fits the larger picture. Philadelphia owns the East's best record. Boston has the conference's strongest point differential and has outscored opponents by 10.7 points per game.

The surrounding conversation will center on Brown and Tatum until the opening tip. Brown does not intend to contribute any ceremonial small talk.

"He's on his side," Brown said. "I'm on my side."

#2855. Former Duck QB Austin Novosad throws 3 TDs in 1st career start

In three seasons with the Oregon Ducks, quarterback Austin Novosad stuck around, awaiting his turn to start. The four-star passer from the 2023 class competed with Dante Moore for the starting nod in 2025, but was beat out and ultimately entered the transfer portal after the season having completed 12-of-15 passes for 99 yards as a Duck.

Novosad soon committed to the Bowling Green Falcons, getting a fresh start and looking to compete for the starting job during his junior campaign. Once again, he was beat out, this time by Division-II Assumption College transfer Jay Kastantin.

But with Kastantin out with an injury, Novosad finally got his first career college start on Tuesday night in Bowling Green's MAC matchup with Kent State. He didn't disappoint, completing 15-of-25 passes for 229 yards and three touchdowns, leading the Falcons to a 33-28 victory.

The former Ducks' first career touchdown pass was as impressive as it gets, converting a third-and-six for a 70-yard touchdown to his running back with a free rusher in his face.

After Bowling Green built a two-score lead, Kent State rallied back and took a 28-27 lead with 2:00 minutes remaining in the fourth quarter. Novosad completed a pass for 16 yards before Bowling Green running back Nakai Amachree ran down the left sideline for the 48-yard game-winning score. The Falcons stopped the Golden Flashes on the ensuing drive, giving Novosad a win in his first game as a starter.

With just a 2-7 record with Kastantin at the helm, Bowling Green would be smart to give Novosad a longer look as their starting quarterback.
