#2853. Payton Pritchard, Paul George preview highly anticipated Celtics-76ers matchup

The Celtics will face an old friend when they take on the 76ers on Tuesday night: Jaylen Brown, who spent a decade in Boston before being traded to Philadelphia on Tuesday.

And Payton Pritchard, one of Brown's longest-tenured teammates, looks forward to the match-up.

"I definitely had a lot of good memories with JB," he said in front of an unusually large media corp at the Xfinity Mobile Arena in Philadelphia. "Obviously, he was there from the moment I got drafted, and we had a good competitive battle. He pushed me to get better, so I'm definitely appreciative of that. And then, as far as tonight, two really great teams going at it, and there's nothing better than that."

Still, although there's heightened public interest in this match-up, the added emotions are nothing that he and the Celtics aren't used to.

"I've played a lot of playoff games, so I don't think it'll be that much different," Pritchard said. Three of those games came at the very arena where the Celtics held shootaround on Tuesday morning, just a few months ago, in the first round of the 2026 NBA Playoffs

Much of the discourse heading into Tuesday night will center on the dynamic between Brown and Jayson Tatum, whose relationship has been heavily scrutinized for years, especially in the last few weeks.

Paul George said he expects another big night from Tatum amid all the chaos.

"JT is locked in," George said. "He's as professional as they come at preparing for a game. He's been playing at an MVP level, and I think he's looking at it as another game on the schedule. Obviously, there's a lot of noise surrounding it that hopefully he's not lost in. But it's our job to support him, to help him do it, and make sure his focus is where it needs to be for us to come out here and have a chance to play against a really good team and continue to add to his MVP-performance resume this season."

Pritchard was asked at shootaround what people don't understand about NBA locker rooms, and how they differ from the locker rooms the general public is more familiar with, such as high school locker rooms.

"There's a lot of egos in it, and people need an ego to get to the NBA. And so it's definitely a balancing act," Pritchard said. "And in high school, like you said, it's all your buddies. Not to say that we're not all buddies, but everybody's got families. They're trying to make it. They got things to prove, so it's different. But in the day, I don't think there was ever beef or anything. I think it's working relationships. Some people are closer than others, but you find a way to win and keep going."

One storyline heading into Tuesday night is that Paul George will return to face the 76ers team he played for for two seasons. But there's not too much pressure on that return game for the 9-time All-Star.

"He said he's returned to four different teams before, so he's used to this," he said. "So it's nothing new to him."

George is averaging 20.4 points, 5.2 rebounds, 4.1 assists, and 2.3 steals, while shooting 42.5% from three on the season.

"He's been unbelievable," Pritchard said. "He's played great to start the year and shooting the ball unbelievable. His defense is impeccable, so it's been huge for us."

George said at the Celtics shootaround that he's thoroughly enjoyed his tenure.

"I've been kind of blown away," he said. "I think it exceeded my expectations since being in Boston. You never know how much culture is involved and how much culture changes your outlook on things, and the effect it has mentally and physically. Just being in an environment like the culture, or like Boston presents, and so I've been thrilled. I've been playing some of my best basketball late. Just been enjoying finding out with these guys here. It's a fun style of play. And again, I can't talk about just the thrill and the happiness and excitement and joy that it has brought to the end of it."

#2852. NFL news, live updates, trade deadline rumors: Vikings trade for QB Kirk Cousins; Colts don't trade Jonathan Taylor, send Josh Downs to Chiefs

Week 9 of the 2026 NFL season is in the books, and we've got the NFL trade deadline on deck, which is set for Tuesday afternoon at 4 p.m. ET. Buckle up!

The biggest news of the day — the Minnesota Vikings have traded for Kirk Cousins in a reported deal with the Las Vegas Raiders, sending a third-round pick to Vegas in exchange for the vet. The move reunites Cousins with the Vikings, for whom he last played in 2023 before suffering a torn Achilles, and gives Minnesota some much-needed support at quarterback following Monday night's abysmal loss under Carson Wentz.

The Jacksonville Jaguars kicked off the NFL trade news cycle for the week with a splash move to bolster the secondary, sending a 2027 second-round pick and a 2028 fifth-round pick to the Atlanta Falcons in exchange for Pro Bowl safety Jessie Bates III. They continued adding on defense throughout the day, also acquiring Davon Godchaux from the New Orleans Saints and former first-round pick Jordan Davis in a blockbuster deal with the Philadelphia Eagles.

The Indianapolis Colts were another team to watch ahead of the trade deadline, with star running back Jonathan Taylor at the crux of it all. But as was first reported by NFL insider Tom Pelissero, the Colts did not trade Taylor ahead of Tuesday's deadline. The Colts did, however, trade wide receiver Josh Downs, who gets a fresh start with the Kansas City Chiefs and quarterback Patrick Mahomes.

Follow along with Yahoo Sports for the latest NFL news, injury updates and more coming out of Week 9.

After a great deal of speculation was made that Jonathan Taylor could be moved ahead of the NFL trade deadline, the Indianapolis Colts are reportedly standing pat.

The team will not trade him ahead of the deadline, NFL insider Tom Pelissero reported Tuesday afternoon.

Pelissero reported that the Colts had received multiple offers for Taylor of a second-round pick-plus, but instead, the team will move forward with him as a foundational piece within the locker room as they look to rebuild.

The Dallas Cowboys have signed former Saints running back Kendre Miller to their practice squad, according to multiple reports.

Miller played sparingly in three-plus seasons as a backup for the Saints, tallying 656 rushing yards on 3.6 yards per carry in 27 appearances. The Saints released the former third-round pick on Saturday after signing Audric Estime off waivers from the Cleveland Browns.

With backup running back Tyler Goodson sidelined with a knee sprain, Miller adds a potential depth piece behind starter Javonte Williams.

Acquiring Kirk Cousins wasn't the only notable Vikings roster move on Tuesday.

Shortly after Tuesday's 4 p.m. ET NFL trade deadline, the Vikings announced that they have released wide receiver Odell Beckham Jr. Per NFL insider Tom Pelissero, Beckham will re-sign with Minnesota's practice squad.

The transaction clears a spot on the 53-man roster that can make room for Cousins.

Beckham joined the Vikings after being released by the Giants three games into his return to New York. Beckham played in just one game for Minneosta, Monday's disastrous loss to the Bills that ultimately prompted them to trade for Cousins on Tuesday. He did not catch a pass for the Vikings.

The Ravens and Falcons pulled off a buzzer-beater trade right at the deadline.

Baltimore sent G Andrew Vorhees and a 2028 6th-round pick to Atlanta for C Ryan Neuzil, per The Athletic's Jonathan Jones.

Vorhees was a backup guard for the Ravens, while Neuzil was the Falcons' starting center.

The Miami Dolphins placed quarterback Malik Willis on injured reserve on Tuesday, the team announced.

Willis has had a lingering groin injury that has been impacting his mobility. The quarterback had an MRI, and it showed that it is now a core muscle injury. Willis doesn't need surgery, but the amount of rest required to recover made it make sense for Miami to add him to IR.

With Willis sidelined for at least the next month, backup quarterback Kyle McCord is expected to take over as the starter this weekend against the Indianapolis Colts, per ESPN's Adam Schefter. Willis is not expected to miss the remainder of the season.

With just minutes left before the deadline, it looks like it will be a weak deadline for running backs. Pollard isn't the only significant name staying put, as the Indianapolis Colts are reportedly holding on to Jonathan Taylor as well.

Keenan Allen is reportedly headed back to California, this time to play for the San Francisco 49ers.

#2851. Rep. Jim Jordan, Sen. John Thune elected GOP leaders in Congress

Republican lawmakers on Tuesday picked Rep. Jim Jordan of Ohio and Sen. John Thune of South Dakota to serve as their respective House and Senate minority leaders in the next Congress.

Jordan, the House Judiciary Committee chairman, was elected in a 106-73 vote, sources told MS NOW on condition of anonymity to discuss the private meeting. He beat Rep. Steve Scalise, R-La., the current House majority leader.

A two-decade House veteran and committed supporter of President Donald Trump, Jordan will take on the leadership role just as Republicans relinquish power, following blowout Democratic victories in last week's midterm election.

House Speaker Mike Johnson, R-La., announced the day after Election Day that he would not run for minority leader in the 120th Congress.

Thune, in a statement, said he "appreciated the opportunity to have an open, honest, and transparent conversation with my colleagues today, and I am honored to have their continued trust and support to lead the Senate Republican Conference in the 120th Congress."

"I'm proud of what this Republican team has accomplished together, and we remain united in our commitment to advancing conservative priorities, working with President Trump and our colleagues in the House, and holding the line against Democrats' efforts to enact their far-left, liberal agenda," Thune said in the statement.

"We have important work ahead of us, and I look forward to serving alongside my colleagues on behalf of the American people," he said.

#2850. Kyle Shanahan’s 49ers have perfect recipe to shred Cowboys defense in Week 10 rivalry renewal

Kyle Shanahan's 49ers have perfect recipe to shred Cowboys defense in Week 10 rivalry renewal appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The San Francisco 49ers and Dallas Cowboys played in one of the greatest rivalries in NFL history. There have been three major generations to this rivalry that has gone beyond the local fans of both teams. This is a national rivalry that has been captured in the annals of NFL history

There was a time when the 49ers were an upstart team with a Hall of Famer named John Brodie at quarterback who tried to climb the ladder by getting the best of the Cowboys' Doomsday Defense while battling Roger Stauabach.

Shortly thereafter, Joe Montana and the Niners were able to get to the top of the NFL with a remarkable victory in the NFC Championship game that included a remarkable Montana to Dwight Clark touchdown.

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

The Cowboys would rebound through the efforts of triplets Troy Aikman, Emmitt Smith and Michael Irvin. The Cowboys would go on to win three Super Bowl titles in a four-year span.

That last Dallas run was 30 years ago. The 49ers have the current advantage as they are in first place in the NFC West with a chance to go deep into the NFC playoffs. The Cowboys are a one-step forward, two-step backwards type of team.

The streaking 49ers should have an excellent chance to go into AT&T Stadium and assert themselves against the struggling Cowboys.

The 49ers rebounded from their only loss of the season with a one-sided triumph over the Las Vegas Raiders, while the Cowboys had to go all-out to beat the Indianapolis Colts. That victory came after an inexplicable home loss to the Arizona Cardinals. Brian Schottenheimer's team has yet to win back-to-back games this season, and that lack of consistency has been a hallmark of this team for the last couple of years.

Certainly, the Cowboys could increase their profile with a win over the Niners, but the visitors have the focus and the skill to take care of business on the road.

Mandatory Credit: Brett Davis-Imagn Images

The San Francisco offense has a number of key options at this point in the season, and head coach Kyle Shanahan has excelled at leading the Niners to employ a diversified attack.

However, he knows that Brock Purdy is a sensational leader who is in contention for the Most Valuable Player award this season. Purdy has completed 163 of 236 passes for 1,980 yards with 17 touchdowns and just 5 interceptions. The 49ers offensive line has done an excellent job of protecting Purdy as he has only been sacked five times. Purdy has also demonstrated his running ability, as he has 39 carries for 201 yards and 2 TDs.

His top receiving weapon is tight end George Kittle, as the big-play weapon has 38 receptions for 483 yards and 5 TDs. Kittle is a game breaker because of his run after-the-catch ability. He has excellent speed once he hits full stride, and that's one of the reasons he is so dangerous.

Of course, the Niners would love to have Mike Evans in the lineup. He has been slowed by tendinitis and he has played just 6 of 8 games. He has 27-420-3 to this point, but he could be unstoppable if he is healthy for this game. The Niners acquired WR Keenan Allen from the Indianapolis Colts at the trade deadline, and he will be able to contribute if Evans can't play up to his standards.

Christian McCaffrey is the most versatile running back in the NFL. He remains dangerous as both a runner and a receiver. McCaffrey has rushed for 457 yards and is averaging 4.5 yards per carry and he has crashed into the end zone 9 times. He supplements his RB skills with his ability to get open as a receiver. He has caught 36-270-1, and he remains one of the most challenging players in the NFL.

The Cowboys certainly seem like they should be able to compete on even terms with their historical rival. They are loaded with individual talent, but when it comes to playing consistently, mistakes are often the result.

The Niners are going to have to contend with Dak Prescott, who has all the tools to light up the scoreboard. He has completed 218 of 336 passes for 2,381 yards with 16 touchdowns and just 5 interceptions. When Prescott is cooking, his main weapon is CeeDee Lamb, who is enjoying one of the best seasons of his career. He has been targeted an eye-catching 109 times through the Cowboys' first nine games and he has 70 receptions for 904 yards and 7 touchdowns.

No. 2 wide receiver George Pickens is capable of going over the top of the San Francisco defense and he has added 39-533-2. Jake Ferguson has not been a huge factor this season, but the tight end could play a key role in this game. He has caught 19-136-0 to this point.

Running back Javonte Williams has rushed for 629 yards and is averaging 4.1 yards per carry and he has pounded the ball into the end zone 9 times.

The Niners defense is capable of shutting down the Cowboys. San Francisco is allowing 19.5 points per game, and that's the No. 6 scoring defense in the league. They rank 3rd in overall defense, as they are giving up just 291.9 yards per game. Fred Warner leads the Niners with 77 tackles. He also has a remarkable 5 forced fumbles, 3 passes defensed and 1 fumble recovery.

If the Cowboys get their offense going, look for Warner to make something happen to take away the Dallas momentum.

#2849. Mississippi Senate: Scott Colom extends tight lead over Hyde-Smith as ballot deadlines hit

Democratic nominee Scott Colom added slightly to his narrow lead over incumbent Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith in the Mississippi Senate election as two key ballot deadlines hit Tuesday.

Colom, a local district attorney, as of 4:30 p.m. ET, was ahead of Hyde-Smith by 2,607 votes, out of 953,547 votes counted, with an estimated 97% of the votes in, according to the Associated Press. That's a margin of just 0.27 percentage points.

Independent candidate Ty Pinkins had 20,034 votes.

Tuesday is the deadline for resolving affidavit or provisional ballots, which are cast by voters whose eligibility is disputed or unclear on Election Day. It is also the deadline for the receipt of mail-in absentee ballots that are postmarked by Election Day, Nov. 3.

Mississippi is one of just two states whose Senate election has not been called. Alaska is the other.

Democrats so far have flipped seven Senate seats held by Republicans going into the election. That gives Democrats a new majority in the Senate of at least 54 seats in the 100-member chamber come January.

Alaska before Election Day was viewed as possibly key to the question of whether Republicans would retain their narrow majority in the Senate.

Mississippi, which has not elected a Democratic senator since the segregationist John Stennis in 1982, was on few political analysts' radar as a possible pickup for Democrats. If Colom wins his race, he would be the first Black senator from Mississippi since the post-Civil War Reconstruction Era.

In Alaska, Democratic nominee Mary Peltola is ahead by just 2,517 votes over incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan, with 218,641 total votes counted, a margin of 1.1 percentage points.

Peltola has 48.6% of the total vote. If neither she nor Sullivan gets more than 50% of the total vote, the race will go to a ranked-choice round, in which the two other candidates, Gerald Heikes and another candidate named Dan Sullivan, will be eliminated, and the second choices of voters who made them their first choice will be allocated between Peltola and the incumbent.

#2848. Tre Johnson is struggling from three. So are the Wizards

DETROIT — Tre Johnson is an elite shooter. It's why the Washington Wizards selected him No. 6 in the 2025 draft. But his jump shot hasn't clicked at the start of the 2026-27 season.

Johnson scored two points on 1-for-8 shooting in Monday's 122-111 loss to the Detroit Pistons just two days after shooting 2-for-15 in a 114-75 loss to the Oklahoma City Thunder. Johnson missed all 13 of his 3-point attempts in those contests.

He is shooting 35.1% from the field through 11 games. His 24.5% 3-point percentage is the second-worst in the NBA among players with at least 50 3-point attempts this season, trailing only Ace Bailey's 22.6%.

In Monday's loss, Johnson missed two wide-open triples — one in the far corner and another on the wing — in the first half. In the second half, he missed three more open looks to finish 0-for-6 from long range. His lone make was a 3-foot push shot that rimmed in.

Johnson is getting open looks. He's just missing them.

"[Johnson] had a great game in Atlanta the other night. This is what this guy does. I got great faith in him," Brian Keefe said. "He got some good looks tonight. He's gonna knock those down. We saw what he did last year with the percentage he shot. That'll turn. He puts in all the work, and I got great faith in him."

Johnson's struggles are concerning. But he's far from the only Wizards with an inefficient 3-ball. The Wizards made just nine of their 36 3-point attempts in Monday's loss. They're shooting 29% from three, which ranks 29th in the league.

Trae Young made just three of his 10 3-point attempts in Monday's loss. He's shooting 28.8% from three. Alex Sarr missed both of his outside jumpers to fall to 19.4% from three this season. Kyshawn George shot just 2-for-6 from long range.

Washington has a shooting problem. Keefe believes this slump will pass. But 11 games in, the Wizards' spacing concerns have been realized.

"We've got guys who can make them. I've got faith in our shooters. We're in a tough stretch shooting-wise, but these things will average out," Keefe said. " We're going to continue to do the right things, put ourselves in the position to make those shots, and I got faith in our guys."

AJ Dybantsa, who scored 21 points against Detroit but made just one of his four 3-point attempts, reiterated that sentiment. The rookie remained confident in the Wizards' ability to make open shots, especially from three.

"Keep shooting. We're NBA players. We're gonna knock shots down," Dybantsa said. "We might be in a shooting slump right now, but [we] have to keep shooting."

#2845. Nvidia sells off for second day in fallout from Jensen Huang’s affair with alleged spy

Nvidia shares sank 5.47% on Tuesday as investors continued to weigh the potential fallout from CEO Jensen Huang's affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

The embattled chip giant briefly dipped below $4 trillion in market cap during afternoon trading, but bounced back to close above that level. Nvidia last cleared the $4 trillion benchmark on July 9, 2025.

Shares closed more than 4% lower on Monday, the day after Huang told staff in an email that he had been "unfaithful" to his wife and had "fallen short" of his responsibilities as CEO.

He reportedly had an affair with Alice Huang, an executive at Megaspeed, an Nvidia customer. The New York Times on Oct. 29 reported that U.S. officials had concluded she was "a Chinese intelligence asset, funneling information to Beijing" and "possibly steering advanced Nvidia A.I. chips to China."

U.S. President Donald Trump said Monday that the FBI is investigating.

"I've been reading a little bit about it over a period of time, but not too long ago," Trump told reporters at the White House. "We'll look into it. I know the FBI is looking into it in terms of something else, and I think that he'll be in good shape."

Adding to the tech giant's woes was a class-action shareholder lawsuit filed against Nvidia and Huang in federal court in San Jose, California.

The Rosen Law Firm's suit alleges the defendants made false or misleading statements about his affair with Huang and Nvidia's relationship with Megaspeed, thereby presenting a material risk to the company's reputation and business prospects.

"Right now the Street and investor chatter is assigning roughly a 15% chance he is removed as CEO of Nvidia with a high probability (85%) he stays on as CEO after the Board investigation is completed," said Dan Ives, senior and managing director at Yorkville Ives & Co, in a Tuesday note.

"We would expect a Board investigation that could take 4 weeks to 6 weeks and will create a clear overhand on the stock until more news emerges from Nvidia."

Amid Nvidia's turmoil, some of its chip rivals are seeing a boost. Advanced Micro Devices and Broadcom built on a strong start to the week, with AMD gaining 1.3% and Broadcom adding nearly 4%. AMD gained 13% on Monday, while Broadcom added just over 10%.

— CNBC's Dan Mangan and Caleigh Keating contributed to this report.

#2844. 10-year Treasury yield races back to 5% as inflation data, Waller comments drive rate hike expectations

Treasury yields jumped on Tuesday after the U.S. consumer price index data for October came in hotter than expected. A mediocre auction of $42 billion in Treasury notes compounded the decline in bonds.

The yield on the 10-year Treasury note — the benchmark for mortgages, car loans and credit card rates — traded above 5% before paring gains. It was last up 13 basis points at 4.992%. The 30-year Treasury bond yield, more sensitive to geopolitical developments, rose more than 9 basis points to 5.352%. The 2-year Treasury note yield, which closely tracks short-term Fed rate expectations, surged more than 18 basis points to 4.736%.

The 10-year yield last traded above the 5% level earlier this month. In October, 10-year and 30-year yields hit 24-year highs.

One basis point equals 0.01% or 1/100th of 1%, and yields and prices move inversely to one another.

The consumer price index for October grew 0.4%, more than the 0.3% expected from economists polled by Dow Jones and down from the 0.6% growth in September.

Core CPI, which excludes volatile food and energy prices, gained 0.3%. Economists had expected a 0.2% gain on the month. The reading was in line with September's 0.3% rise.

The report came after Fed Governor Christopher Waller said he would support "more robust action" if inflation readings are higher than expected.

"In that case, I believe a 50 basis point hike in the policy rate should be considered at the December meeting," Waller said in London at a central bank conference hosted by the Bank of England. "Hiking only 25 basis points would put our credibility at risk, a risk I am not eager to take."

Lindsay Rosner, head of multi-sector fixed income investing at Goldman Sachs Asset Management, said the hot CPI numbers signal that Fed hikes could spill into 2027.

"The Fed could push for a more-aggressive tightening cycle with CPI continuing to print strong," she said. "A December hike is our firm base case despite wider AI jitters, and stubborn inflation means we cannot rule out further hikes in 2027."

A lackluster auction of 10-year Treasurys added further downward pressure on bond prices Tuesday. "Today's 10-year auction was a bit soft," BMO Capital economists wrote. "Before the auction, Treasuries were selling off on strong volumes, and 10-year notes went into the bidding deadline 12 bp cheaper on the day. Since the results, yields have ticked slightly higher in the follow-through."

"[D]ealers were left with 10% of the auction, the most since May," said Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners. "Inflation breakevens today are focused on the drop in oil prices while regular Treasuries today are reacting to a Fed that is definitely going to hike next month, as of now, with one another in January," he said.

Oil prices dipped further Tuesday, with Brent crude falling below $90 per barrel for the first time since August. Brent futures settled above that level, however, falling 1.17% to at $91.49 a barrel. This comes on the back of President Donald Trump's comments that the war with Iran could end "fairly soon" and downplaying the prospect of further strikes in the region.

Tuesday's CPI data, together with wholesale inflation numbers due Friday, may shape the Federal Reserve's future decisions on interest rates. Markets have largely priced in a quarter percentage point hike in December, according to the CME Group's FedWatch tool. Friday's stronger-than-expected U.S. employment data, more than double what economists had forecast, further strengthened those hawkish expectations.

#2843. 30 Fantasy Basketball Takes on Every NBA Team: The rookie takeover is changing rotations

Here are five fantasy trends grabbing my attention heading into Week 4 of the fantasy basketball season, plus some rapid-fire notes on every other team.

Heat: One of the bigger surprises has been Miami's Davion Mitchell, who's ranked inside the top 55 in both 9-cat and standard points leagues, outpacing his ADP of 118.7. He's averaging 35.1 minutes and 6.2 assists per game, making his playmaking a major part of his fantasy value. With six double-doubles through 10 appearances, Mitchell has been a quiet hero early in the season.

Warriors: F Yaxel Lendeborg is another draft-day bargain, ranking 54th in 9-cat and 37th in points leagues after carrying an ADP of 104. His usage ranks fourth, but he has the highest TS% among rookies playing at least 20 minutes a night. The efficiency at high-end minutes (32.3 per game) with a defined role led to some nice cross-category value across scoring, rebounding, facilitating and defensive production. I'm buying the breakout and hard-pressed to find a reason to sell. He's that good.

Clippers: LAC is hoarding one of the best fantasy values in Kris Dunn. He's sitting at 33rd in 9-cat, despite being drafted in the late rounds (ADP 119). The steals he's piling up (over 2 per contest) are what's driving most of his rise up the ranks. He's playing a ton of minutes while keeping his turnovers down and delivering a versatile 9-4-4 line with almost 3 steals a night. There's some sell-high appeal here, especially in 9-cat leagues. In 42% of Dunn trades, he's part of a package deal, so you'll likely have to include someone else to make a swap.

Timberwolves: Let's not gloss over Minnesota's Jaden McDaniels, who is crushing his ADP (ranked 46th in 9-cat after going 89th on draft day). Through 11 games, he's on pace for career-bests in minutes, points, rebounds, assists, 3s and usage per game. I think his defense will pick up once Ant-Man returns and he carries less responsibility on the offensive end.

Kings: Sacramento's Darius Acuff Jr. has a crazy high 32.3% usage rate, eighth in the NBA among qualified players. That's a massive offensive responsibility for a rookie, and the Kings' 1-9 start gives them little reason to limit his development. His scoring (25.9 ppg) and assists (7.4 apg) make him especially appealing in points leagues. Although, even with his warts in inefficiency and turnovers, he's in the top 80 in 9-cat, well above his preseason ADP.

Bucks: In Milwaukee, Brayden Burries has overtaken Ryan Rollins in the starting lineup. Burries is averaging 13 points, 8.3 rebounds and 3.5 assists across three starts. That rebounding from a guard is particularly appealing in category leagues, and the Bucks' 1-9 start should give Burries an opportunity to establish himself. He is one of my priority adds this week.

Hawks: Atlanta has made a similar move to Milwaukee, starting rookie Kingston Flemings over CJ McCollum. Flemings is gaining valuable playing time and experience, while McCollum's fantasy outlook remains stable, operating in a timeshare and sixth man role. The Hawks have a 124 Offensive Rating with Flemings running point (small sample but a good signal nonetheless). His shot volume and efficiency have improved over that three-game span, too — now, let's see if it'll hold and if he can generate more potential assists.

Thunder: Rookie Aday Mara offers immediate appeal as a shot-blocking specialist. The rim protection, plus rebounding, give him a useful 9-cat profile. Coming off the best performance of his young career over the weekend (18 points with 8 rebounds and 3 blocks), Mara will have to earn every chance to sustain a 20-25 minute role nightly.

Hornets: Charlotte is dealing with injuries to Kon Knueppel, Brandon Miller and Dennis Schröder, creating additional opportunities throughout the rotation. Grayson Allen is the priority pickup, averaging 15-3-3 and over 3 3s per game. He's worth adding, though some of that opportunity will disappear when Charlotte gets healthier.

Pacers: In Indiana, it's Andrew Nembhard who has taken on the creator role for Tyrese Haliburton. With Hali off the floor, Nembhard's assists went up from 5.2 to 8.5 per game. Given Hali's injury history, I'd hold Nembhard even if it feels like a sell-high moment.

Raptors: Toronto's Jamal Shead has shown impressive playmaking upside with Immanuel Quickley sidelined. After dishing out 16 assists on Saturday, he followed that up with a 20-point, 11-assist performance on Monday. Keep streaming him until IQ returns.

Pistons: For Detroit, fantasy managers should add Daniss Jenkins. He started in place of Paul Reed on Monday. I'm keeping Reed even if the demotion isn't matchup-based because he can still get plenty of rebounds and stocks until Jalen Duren returns. Though, that's been delayed at least another two weeks.

Magic: At 2-7, they need Paolo Banchero back because leaning more heavily on Franz Wagner without him isn't working out. Wagner's 28.9% season usage rate reflects his importance to Orlando's offense, while Desmond Bane continues to struggle, shooting 39% from the field across eight contests.

There's a difference between a player struggling to make shots and one whose early production might be difficult to sustain.

Bulls: Chicago has the worst Defensive Rating in the league at 123.6. Josh Giddey hasn't helped, as he's been rough on both ends. Sunday was different, though — he had a season-high 19 points, seven rebounds and a season-best 15 assists in 34 minutes in a 127-121 loss to Philly. I'll be honest: I'm panicking a bit, and I'm trying to use that breakout game as trade bait.

Knicks: Another player falling short of expectations is New York's Mikal Bridges. He's averaging 6 points, 3 rebounds, 2 assists and 1.4 stocks per game. I've seen Bridges on waivers in a couple of spots and I don't blame anyone. He won't miss games, but the production has been awful.

Grizzlies: Ty Jerome is on the other side of that conversation. He's averaging 26.7 points, 4.9 assists and 2.5 3s in just 30.4 minutes while shooting 50% from the field. That's excellent production, but I'm skeptical he maintains that scoring efficiency all season. With a usage rate around 30%, I'm not selling just to sell. But if someone values Jerome as a top-25 fantasy player, I'd entertain it.

Nets: In Brooklyn, it's been a guard carousel featuring Nolan Traore and Mikel Brown Jr. It's a tough week to hold either player with just two games on Wednesday and Saturday. I'd send them to waivers for a better streaming option.

Nuggets: It's been more of the same in Denver — Nikola Jokić and Jamal Murray are first-rounders, and then there's a significant drop-off with the supporting cast. Christian Braun is the only other Nuggets player inside the top-100 in 9-cat (74th).

Rockets: Alperen Şengün is pacing to a career-high in assists per game, currently sitting at 7.8 per game, the highest mark on the team and seventh in the NBA. It's early, but Fred VanVleet's leadership is key to their success and unlocking the upside of the starting unit — just look at Jabari Smith Jr.

Celtics: Boston initially caught some heat swapping a former Finals MVP for Paul George, but it's paying off. Offensively, George is fitting right in, but he's adding value on defense, averaging 2.3 swipes per contest.

Cavaliers: In Cleveland, Peyton Watson is worth monitoring for blocks. He's blocking 1.4 per game, tied for ninth in the league with Kevin Durant.

76ers: Joel Embiid, while solid this far, admitted to dealing with a plantar fascia injury. That won't age well.

Lakers: Walker Kessler ranks seventh in 9-cat against an ADP of 40. His rebounding, blocks and field-goal percentage are driving his value, even with limited offensive usage.

Mavericks: Cooper Flagg looks every bit the part of a first-round fantasy talent. He ranks sixth in 9-cat and points leagues, which is pretty impressive for the second-year phenom.

Spurs: Wemby just missed a triple-double on Monday, posting 40 points and 11 rebounds with 9 blocks. Wemby has put up an incredible 48 blocks through the Spurs' first 11 games — landing him at No. 2 all-time since blocks began being tracked in 1983–84.

Pelicans: Zion Williamson is 27th in 9-cat against an ADP of 69. His efficiency and defensive contributions have helped offset his familiar category weaknesses. But also, sell high!

Trail Blazers: Damian Lillard is outside the top-100 in 9-cat (109th) and averaging 30.24 fantasy points per game in just under 30 minutes a night. Good buy-low spot as he's shooting 41% from the field through nine games.

Jazz: Keyonte George's ankle injury could create opportunities for Isaiah Collier. It could also provide a big boost to No. 2 overall pick Darryn Peterson's fantasy profile.

Wizards: Trae Young is pacing to career lows in field-goal (39.3%) and 3-point (28.8%) percentages. At least the assists are up to 8 per game.

#2842. Where the Pirates Stand With Brandon Lowe’s Free Agency

The key narrative surrounding the Pittsburgh Pirates since the offseason began has been Brandon Lowe's free agency. Re-signing the 32-year-old is unequivocally the team's biggest priority this offseason.

The All-Star second baseman was arguably the team's best all-around player in 2026 and one of the key reasons for their 11-win improvement from the year before. However, after just one year in Pittsburgh, there's a chance Lowe could move on to a new city.

That said, Pirates general manager Ben Cherington said on Tuesday at the GM Meeting in Las Vegas that the team has had conversations with Lowe and his agent, and they've been "good."

Cherington also reiterated that he hopes Lowe ends up back with the Pirates next year, but the sides are taking it one step at a time.

As far as any other details are concerned, that's where Lowe's free agency with the Pirates stands right now.

It may not be the news many were hoping for, but it was available just a few days ago: Lowe and the Pirates are talking, but no new deal appears imminent based on the executives' comments.

Whether a deal actually gets done remains to be seen.

May 9, 2026; San Francisco, California, USA; Pittsburgh Pirates infielder Brandon Lowe (5) hits a two RBI double against the San Francisco Giants during the ninth inning at Oracle Park. Mandatory Credit: Robert Edwards-Imagn Images

Another important piece to Lowe's free agency is that the Pirates did extend the qualifying offer to him about a week ago. That represents a one-year deal worth $23.15 million.

If anything, Pittsburgh offering that contract to the free agent at least signals that the front office is serious about bringing him back. (The extra draft pick if Lowe signs elsewhere doesn't hurt, either.)

Lowe has until November 17 to either accept or decline the qualifying offer from the Pirates. Even if it is declined, that wouldn't rule out a return to Pittsburgh.

Just as the Pirates are actively trying to re-sign Lowe, other teams across baseball are getting into the mix, too, and the competition for the prized free agent appears to be heating up.

Earlier in the week, it was reported that several American League East teams, namely the Boston Red Sox, New York Yankees, and Toronto Blue Jays, had some level of interest in Lowe. All three teams need a second baseman, and Lowe is the best on the market.

Where Lowe ends up signing this offseason is still anyone's guess.

Cherington confirmed he's had talks with Lowe's agent about staying in Pittsburgh, but that doesn't guarantee he's still a Pirate on Opening Day.

The NFL trade deadline went down at 4 p.m. ET on Tuesday. Here's a breakdown of the trades and grades. Yahoo Sports' Eric Edholm assessed the NFC teams and Frank Schwab tackled the AFC. Trade activity is noted from late August:

The Cowboys' big move came on Oct. 1 when they traded for CB Joey Porter Jr. for multiple picks. Looking back now, the move looks good from a value standpoint, as Jessie Bates III ended up being traded for more, and Porter has played pretty well for Dallas. The problem is that Porter has played in only four of Dallas' six games, and the Cowboys are still treading water. Team owner Jerry Jones hinted that the Cowboys were still big-game hunting in the trade market, but nothing else emerged.

The Giants have had a fascinating pre-deadline period, acquiring J.J. McCarthy for nickels on the dollar and potentially changing the team's landscape at the position. Jaxson Dart presumably remains the 2027 starter, but a strong finish from McCarthy could set up some interesting optics next offseason. They were sellers before the draft, trading Dexter Lawrence to Cincinnati, but the Giants were shopping right up until the deadline as NFC East leaders. They reportedly went in hard on Jeffery Simmons and made the Colts a strong offer on Jonathan Taylor, but nothing materialized.

It has been a surprising turn of events with the Eagles — often among the more aggressive teams around deadline time — turning into sellers. Philadelphia dealt A.J. Brown this offseason but also traded for multiple players, so that felt like a separate situation. The in-season loss of Jalen Hurts has precipitated some of this movement, but the Eagles were already in sell mode with the Cam Jurgens trade the week prior to Hurts being diagnosed with blood clots. Trading Davis brought back some potentially great returns. It's not how we imagined it going, but the Eagles were smart to pivot, especially Hurts' murky future.

The Commanders were reportedly open to the idea of trading DT Daron Payne, whose contract expires after this season, but he suffered a broken foot, thus killing off any deal. The same might have been true for Javon Kinlaw, who is also on IR. The team's salary

cap situation is fine, but the Commanders are underwater at 2-5-1 and could use the draft-pick help. They've made only 11 selections combined the past two drafts, with a roster that needs an influx of young talent.

It's possible we look back at the Simmons trade as the most impactful of the 2026 cycle. In Simmons' first game as a Bear, he took over against the Seahawks, giving the team and its fans hope for another Super Bowl run. Two first-round picks is potentially a significant cost for a defensive tackle, but if the Bears finish closer to the top of the standings, those picks might be worth about the same as the No.10 overall selection the Bengals paid for Dexter Lawrence. The Simmons trade was aggressive and could help put the Bears over the top. What's not to like?

2028 sixth-round draft pick (to Jets, part of Andre Cisco deal on Nov. 9)

That likely will have to count Green Bay's big "acquisition" this season, although they did reportedly kick the tires on Jonathan Taylor amid Josh Jacobs' once-uncertain status. Do the Packers have enough to make a big run? We'll find out.

The Vikings have a world-class defense, and it would be criminal not to give that unit a fighting chance to compete for a Super Bowl. There are still questions on offense that weren't addressed, but getting Henley was still a plus.

#2840. Here's the inflation breakdown for October 2026 — in one chart

Inflation throttled back in October from its blistering pace in September amid a more moderate rise in gasoline prices, but remained high as categories like food and housing put upward pressure on consumer prices.

The consumer price index, an inflation barometer, rose 0.4% on a monthly basis in October, after adjusting for seasonal factors, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported Tuesday. That's down from 0.6% in September but higher than the 0.3% that economists expected.

"It's uncomfortably high," said Mark Zandi, chief economist at Moody's. "The Fed's not going to be happy about it. It certainly makes it more likely we get another rate increase in December."

The Federal Reserve uses inflation data to guide its interest rate policy. The U.S. central bank raised rates in September amid a recent pickup in inflation, which economists largely attribute to the war in Iran. It held rates steady at its last meeting in October.

Economists generally assess a measure known as "core" CPI — which strips out volatile food and energy prices — to examine underlying inflation dynamics in the U.S. economy.

That core metric rose 0.3% in October, as it also did in September and August. A consistent monthly reading around 0.2% would be more in line with the Fed's 2% annual inflation target, Zandi said.

Inflation has been above the Fed's target for more than five years.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics didn't report an annual inflation figure in the latest CPI report because of the lengthy federal government shutdown that happened a year ago. That shutdown, which ran from Oct. 1 to Nov. 12, disrupted the agency's typical data collection, and it didn't release an inflation report for October 2025.

"Investors do not need year-over-year figures to see that this was a hot inflation report and that prices are not on a sustainable path back toward the Fed's 2% target," Jason Pride, chief of investment strategy and research at Glenmede, a wealth management firm, wrote in an e-mail Tuesday.

"It would have taken exceptional evidence of disinflation to dissuade the Fed from raising rates in December, and this report delivered the opposite," he wrote.

Economists pointed to four big reasons for the hotter-than-expected CPI reading.

Perhaps the largest contributor to the outsized inflation reading in Tuesday's CPI report was housing, Zandi said.

Housing is the largest component of CPI, and movements in the category strongly influence the index's month-to-month reading, he said.

The "shelter" CPI index rose 0.3% in October, accounting for over 30% of the CPI increase, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics.

Zandi said he doesn't expect that dynamic to persist, however, because it's not consistent with the trajectory of market rents in the U.S.

"It does feel like we have a high level of monthly volatility" in the CPI data, he said. "It's all over the map month to month."

But economists said broader factors are putting upward pressure on prices, too.

"Core inflation today reflects three outsized shocks: tariffs, energy prices, and AI-related demand," Josh Hirt, senior U.S. economist at Vanguard Group, an asset manager, wrote in an e-mail Tuesday.

The Iran war has raised oil prices, pushing up prices for fuels like gasoline, diesel and jet fuel, which are refined from crude oil, economists said.

Gasoline prices rose 1% in October, a significant pullback after a 9.1% monthly increase in September, CPI data showed.

While diesel prices have retreated from recent record highs, they remain elevated and could spill more broadly into the U.S. economy in the coming months, economists said.

"Diesel prices have a really slow pass-through to all the areas of CPI, given it affects transportation costs," said Thomas Ryan, a senior North America economist at Capital Economics.

"These things take time," Ryan said. "It'll be an inflationary impetus for at least the next three to six months, I suspect."

There was some evidence of that broader percolation of diesel prices in October, such as in food prices, Zandi said. Higher diesel prices add to the cost of transporting food to the grocery store shelves, he said.

Food prices rose 0.5% in October after an unchanged reading in September, according to CPI data.

The run-up in jet fuel costs has also led airlines to raise their ticket prices, economists said. Airfares climbed 0.4% in October, after monthly increases of 1.2%, 2.7% and 3.8% in the preceding months, respectively, according to the CPI report.

Meanwhile, the inflationary impact of tariffs imposed by the Trump administration should fade by the end of the year, assuming the White House doesn't renew its push for more import taxes, Zandi said.

Certain categories of physical goods, like toys, furniture, and appliances, saw elevated inflation rates in October, likely because businesses have passed on tariff costs to consumers, he said.

The buildout of data centers that power artificial intelligence has driven up prices for electricity and consumer electronics, economists said.

Those data centers have led to a shortage of technology equipment, such as memory chips used in household electronic products like iPhones, Ryan said.

#2839. Binance breach puts custody at center of crypto infrastructure trade

The $6.3 billion Binance hack could accelerate a shift in how investors value cryptocurrency companies, putting a greater premium on custody providers and the infrastructure needed to securely hold and protect digital assets.

Much of crypto's evolution this year has involved companies moving past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, to focus instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance. Last Thursday's breach adds a new dimension, bringing cybersecurity, custody, compliance and balance-sheet resilience into sharper focus.

Importantly, the Binance breach was not an indictment of crypto itself, or a case of leadership-driven fraud, as with FTX in late 2022. Instead, Binance's leadership moved quickly to contain the fallout, protect customers and commit the exchange's own resources to cover losses.

On Tuesday, Binance CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that the company has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Management's response, alongside efforts elsewhere in the industry to boost confidence, helped prevent a security breach from escalating into a broader crisis of confidence. For example, Tether, Circle, Ripple and others moved quickly to freeze or restrict access to about $3 billion in stolen assets; Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

The hack could even help shape the next phase of the crypto infrastructure trade. The new winners may not just be the businesses with the most users, volume or assets under custody, but the ones that can also demonstrate they're able to protect those assets under extreme duress.

"Which company has the resources to invest in cybersecurity and protect customer funds? For a smaller company, it could be much harder because once these crypto companies become infrastructure plays, providing the well for tokenization … they need to have the compliance and control in place so that a similar hacking will not happen or can be minimized," Owen Lau, senior analyst at ClearStreet, told CNBC. "Larger companies have more resources that could give them some advantage."

While institutional investors have increasingly embraced digital assets, broader adoption depends on confidence that those assets can be securely held, accessed and transferred. The Binance breach highlights the importance of that infrastructure, and could increase demand for specialized custodians and platforms with established asset-protection capabilities.

Coinbase has operated a dedicated institutional custody business since 2018. Circle entered the custody business this year, after the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency granted it approval to operate as a trust bank. In the past few years, Ripple and Kraken have also launched custody businesses.

Still, smaller players compete in the space as well. Small cap crypto exchange stock Gemini Space Station has offered custody services since 2019. BitGo Holdings, founded in 2013, is one of the earliest players in institutional crypto custody and infrastructure, with a reputation for security and serving major institutions.

The shift could also drive consolidation as companies look to buy capabilities, deepen their infrastructure offerings and achieve the scale needed to compete, Lau said.

The more consequential structural shift may be the separation of custody from execution, according to Morgan Stanley.

"Crypto exchanges have historically combined trading, custody, and settlement within vertically integrated platforms, concentrating operational risk. Repeated security breaches could encourage investors to move assets toward independent custody while continuing to trade on CEXs," the firm's analyst Felix Stratmann said in a note Monday," referring to centralized cryptocurrency exchanges.

"This would preserve the role of centralized exchanges as liquidity and execution venues, while potentially benefiting regulated custodians, including banks, as institutional investors place greater emphasis on asset segregation, operational resilience, and investor protection," Stratmann said.

#2838. CoreWeave beats on revenue and lifts guidance, easing AI slowdown concerns

CoreWeave reported better-than-expected quarterly revenue and lifted its 2026 guidance on Tuesday, showing that demand for artificial intelligence infrastructure remains strong despite concerns of a market slowdown. The stock advanced in extended trading.

The earnings announcement lands at a particularly volatile moment for CoreWeave and its neocloud peers, as the market sours on the AI trade and as Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, the central figure in the global buildout, faces a scandal tied to an affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

Here's how the company performed relative to LSEG consensus:

CoreWeave's revenue soared 168% from about $1.36 billion a year earlier, according to a statement. The company recorded a net loss of $709 million, or $1.27 per share, compared to a net loss of $110 million a year earlier. Adjusted earnings exclude stock-based compensation.

Executives pushed up their forecast for 2026. They now see $1.275 billion to $1.375 billion in adjusted operating income on $13 billion to $13.3 billion in revenue. Analysts surveyed by Visible Alpha were looking for $1.07 billion in adjusted operating income, and analysts polled by LSEG had expected $12.86 billion in 2026 revenue.

In August, CoreWeave's 2026 guidance included $960 million to $1.15 billion in adjusted operating income and $12.4 billion to $13.2 billion in revenue.

CoreWeave has lost over 40% of its value in the past month, as investors rotate out of companies most closely tied to AI infrastructure. Rising interest rates have pushed lenders out of the market, raising concerns about ongoing access to capital. Some CoreWeave bond yields recently topped 16%.

"The business can execute well and still face a tougher financing environment," Joe Tigay, portfolio manager at Equity Armor Investments, wrote in an email. "Strong demand is encouraging; the cost and availability of capital remain critical."

CoreWeave will get most the capital it needs for 2027 through clients pre-payments and financing from hardware partners, and the company will raise "a limited amount" as well, CEO Mike Intrator told analysts on a conference call.

The company plans to raise new debt tied to a set of contracts in the weeks to come, said Nitin Agrawal, its finance chief.

"At current yields, we do not intend to issue additional parent-level unsecured debt through 2027," he said. "By contrast, the asset-level secured debt market has remained stable, with our loans continuing to trade around par value." In all, he said he projects under $5 billion in parent-level debt financing through 2027.

Fellow neocloud Nebius, whose stock is down by a similar amount, is scheduled to report earnings on Thursday. Nebius shares got a small boost from CoreWeave's results.

Both companies and several other providers of cloud-based graphics processing units are particularly reliant on Nvidia, which provides GPUs as well as necessary capital to the whole ecosystem. Nvidia shares have dropped nearly 10% to start the week as the Huang affair raises pressure on the company in Washington, D.C. Nvidia's board said on Tuesday that it hired Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to investigate Huang and determine whether national security or company secrets were compromised.

CoreWeave investors are also looking toward next week, when key customer Anthropic is scheduled to debut on the Nasdaq. In its updated IPO prospectus on Tuesday, Anthropic indicated that it could be valued at around $1.4 trillion after its share sale. Anthropic has signed deals for the construction of entire data centers that will meet its needs. "We see that we're going to have long-term relationships with the AI labs," said Intrator, a CoreWeave co-founder.

During the quarter, CoreWeave said it would establish its first data centers in Asia and announced the Forge service for running and evaluating AI models.

Management still anticipates 1.85 gigawatts in active power by the end of 2026. CoreWeave is targeting $37 billion to $39 billion in full-year capital expenditures, which would imply 155% growth at the middle of the range. Analysts polled by Visible Alpha were looking for $37.69 billion.

CoreWeave owed almost $47 billion in debt at the end of the third quarter, according to the statement. Like its bigger cloud rivals such as Amazon and Oracle, CoreWeave is rushing to bolster computing capacity for clients.

But last week, voters in the U.S., where CoreWeave derives most of its revenue, rejected dozens of initiatives on data center development. In Texas, Democrat Gina Hinojosa defeated the incumbent, Republican Greg Abbott, after she said Abbott should try to end 20-year tax breaks on large data centers.

"So to begin with, we do not anticipate any impact from the seismic regulatory shift that's occurred," Intrator said.

Almost all of CoreWeave's power is online or is permitted and under construction, he said.

"If data center construction across the sector were to slow, installed compute becomes scarcer, and the knock-on effect is that GPU pricing may stay higher for longer," Intrator said.

He said the company does not expect risks to deliveries in 2027 based on construction at permitted locations.

"We could sell all of the capacity that we have to current buyers that are in the queue. We are choosing not to," Intrator said. We're choosing to be patient. We're choosing to bring the infrastructure to market when it is further along, because we believe that that will result in better economic output for the outstanding infrastructure that we have."

Meanwhile, demand remains robust, with the company now sitting on a $132.5 billion revenue backlog, up from $104 billion as of June.

"Should we choose to focus only on our committed backlog, we would expect capex for 2027 to remain in line with our 2026 capex investment, while revenue and EBITDA grow significantly," Agrawal said.

#2836. Steelers’ 6-2 start offers encouraging history, but also a warning

This article originally appeared on SteelersNOW.com.

The Pittsburgh Steelers exit their bye week on a roll, riding a four-game winning streak and sitting at 6-2. As Pittsburgh prepares for the second half of its season, an interesting question emerges: How have previous Steelers teams with the same record finished?

The good news for Pittsburgh is that history strongly favors another postseason appearance. Of the previous 16 Steelers teams to start 6-2, 15 went on to reach the playoffs, with the 2009 team being the lone exception.

However, the numbers tell a different story when it comes to postseason success.

While reaching the playoffs is always an accomplishment, the bigger goal for Pittsburgh is overcoming a hurdle it has struggled to clear in recent years: winning a playoff game. Of those 15 teams that reached the postseason, seven lost their opening playoff contest.

The most recent example came in 2024, when Pittsburgh not only opened the season 6-2 but improved to 8-2 through its first 10 games. Unfortunately, the Steelers struggled down the stretch, going 2-5 over their final seven games to finish 10-7 before suffering a 28-14 loss to the Baltimore Ravens in the Wild Card Round.

#2834. Singapore wants a bigger slice of the global gold market. Is it too late to the party?

Singapore is stepping up efforts to become a major player in the global gold market, betting that a new central bank vaulting service and tax incentives will help it attract more bullion flows.

The Monetary Authority of Singapore on Tuesday launched a gold vaulting service for foreign central banks and sovereign entities, with an initial capacity of 500 metric tons.

The city-state also plans to introduce tax exemptions for certain wholesale gold-related services such as vaulting, refining, recasting, testing and broking. In June, Singapore unveiled plans for a physical gold clearing system to facilitate the settlement of bullion transactions in the city-state, with operations expected to begin in the first quarter of 2027.

The initiatives mark the country's latest attempt to establish itself as a key bullion hub alongside established centers such as London and Switzerland, as more gold trading activity shifts toward Asia.

But breaking into a market dominated by established trading hubs may prove an uphill battle. For instance, London vaults held about 9,850 metric tons of gold at the end of October, including commercial and central bank storage.

The push from Singapore also comes as rival Hong Kong is also expanding its gold storage and clearing infrastructure, leveraging its proximity to China, the world's largest gold market.

"Singapore is very late to the party as building up a physical hub requires volume when it comes to trade backed structures," said Juerg Kiener, chief investment officer at Swiss Asia Capital.

"The largest gold market is China and its window to the rest of the world is Hong Kong," he told CNBC, adding that Singapore is not well-positioned to compete with Hong Kong in gold-backed trade.

Kiener said Singapore could instead carve out a niche in physical gold storage for financial institutions, although commercial vaulting services are already available in the city-state, including at its high-security freeport near Changi Airport.

Unlike commercial vaults, the new facility will be operated by Singapore's central bank, offering foreign monetary authorities an alternative sovereign custodian for their gold.

"It's not something that we see happening all that often in the gold market or in the central bank community," said Shaokai Fan, global head of central banks at the World Gold Council.

The move comes as central banks increasingly reassess where they store their bullion amid geopolitical tensions, said Fan. Over the past 12 months, several countries have diversified gold storage, with many planning to do so, according to a World Gold Council report published in September.

Singapore's political stability, strong legal framework and extensive air connectivity could make it an attractive alternative to traditional storage centers, he added.

Nikolas Kavalis, managing director at Metals Focus, said the sovereign vaulting service represents an important addition to Singapore's existing gold infrastructure, but cautioned that storage alone would not be sufficient to establish a fully functioning bullion market.

The bigger challenge is building sufficient liquidity and trading activity to compete with London, which remains the dominant global over-the-counter gold market.

Both Singapore and Hong Kong are still developing their local clearing infrastructure, Kavalis said, meaning neither has yet established the kind of deep trading ecosystem that underpins London's dominance.

Attracting central bank gold could help Singapore build that liquidity, particularly if bullion stored locally can also be mobilized for transactions rather than simply held in custody.

Singapore's efforts come as gold flows have already undergone a significant transformation over the past decade.

Historically, much of the world's bullion moved through London and Switzerland, where large gold bars were traded or refined before being shipped to Asian markets.

But the expansion of Asian refineries and growing regional demand have increasingly allowed gold to move directly between Asian markets, reducing their dependence on European trading centers, Kavalis said.

The development of more sophisticated gold hubs in Asia could accelerate that shift.

Still, Hong Kong might have an edge in serving central banks and institutions whose financial activities are closely tied to China, while Singapore could appeal to those already active in its banking and financial markets, Kavalis said.

Fan sees room for both centers to grow. "We see the two centers complementing each other and not really competing with each other," he said.

Singapore's Deputy Prime Minister Gan Kim Yong similarly dismissed suggestions that the two financial hubs were looking at securing a larger share of the same pie. "It's not a zero-sum game," Gan said Tuesday, adding that Singapore's vaulting service would provide central banks with another option for diversifying where they hold their reserves.

Whether Singapore can translate its reputation as a trusted financial center into meaningful bullion flows remains to be seen. "It's a bit chicken and egg because to attract volumes you want liquidity, but to get liquidity you need volumes," Fan said.

#2833. Democratic sweep puts crypto on defensive in Washington

Democrats' sweep of Congress likely resets the political calculus for crypto, ushering in a more adversarial approach in Washington and pushing industry-friendly legislation like the Clarity Act to the back burner.

With crypto critics like Sherrod Brown, D-Ohio, returning to the Senate and Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., retaining significant influence over financial policy, analysts say the new Congress is likely to focus on scrutinizing President Donald Trump's personal crypto dealings over supportive industry legislation.

For years, crypto fought an uphill battle in Washington, asking for clear operating rules, but often landing on the receiving end of regulation by enforcement. That shifted after Trump's 2024 election victory, giving the industry a reprieve and seat at the policy table.

"We see Sherrod Brown winning in Ohio as damaging the crypto industry's political clout as the industry worked to defeat him," Jaret Seiberg, TD Cowen's Washington analyst, said in a note Wednesday. "We did not think Clarity could advance over the next two years. This makes us more confident in our prediction."

The crypto industry spent more than $200 million to sway the midterms, hoping to get leaders elected that could push a comprehensive crypto market structure bill over the finish line after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate.

Seiberg emphasized that Democrats "will investigate Trump's crypto entanglements including World Liberty Financial" and said "the resulting political environment may make it impossible to advance a version of Clarity that the crypto sector could back."

Mike Townsend, Charles Schwab's managing director of legislative and regulatory affairs, said he expects "Democrats to push next year for a version of the bill that is much tougher on cryptocurrency companies."

The stakes extend beyond market structure legislation via Clarity. Stablecoin innovation; decentralized finance, or DeFi, development; banking access; tokenization; and sensible tax policy are priorities the industry sees as important to ensuring the U.S. remains competitive in the global crypto economy.

The industry will need continued bipartisan engagement to ensure these priorities aren't sidelined by changing political dynamics. And Democrats have historically been less supportive of crypto than Republicans, though that divide has narrowed as more Democrats embrace the industry.

The implications of Democrats' big win aren't all bad. Crypto has reached a level of financial integration that could be difficult to reverse, with its future increasingly centered on establishing crypto as financial infrastructure. Even with the Clarity Act potentially stalled until 2028, key financial regulators can help advance the industry through rule-making.

Coinbase's vice president of U.S. policy, Kara Calvert, points out that the election results point to a historic shift in the makeup of the incoming Congress, with a record number of pro-crypto voices expected to join its ranks.

"With last night's results, the 120th Congress is on track to have more new pro-crypto members than we've ever seen in any freshman class," she said. "Crypto is 100% bipartisan, and that's why the future of finance does not rest on either political party or a single election night. Democrats and Republicans alike know that clear rules protect consumers and keep innovation in the U.S."

As of Thursday morning, 19 candidates characterized as pro crypto by the advocacy nonprofit Stand With Crypto have been elected to the Senate versus eight Senate candidates it characterizes as anti crypto. In the House, 228 pro-crypto candidates have been elected versus 106 the group deems anti crypto.

Several races have not been called, but Fairshake — the super PAC launched in 2023 to support pro-crypto candidates regardless of party — has so far won 29 of the 34 races it spent on, per Reuters, including three of the six House candidates it prioritized by spending a baseline of $1 million on their campaigns.

Dante Disparte, chief strategy officer and head of global policy and operations at Circle, emphasized Circle's bipartisan approach and readiness for new Senate and House leadership, but said it "may take longer to get Clarity tabled again."

That bipartisan support may offer a blueprint for what comes next, but even if crypto legislation stalls, progress can continue, said Joseph Chalom, CEO of ether treasury company Sharplink.

"We expect the Clarity Act to remain on hold through 2028. Even so, there is a clear path forward for stablecoins … and for the tokenization of public equities under the SEC's five-year innovation exemption," he said. "Technological progress is rarely rolled back. Once the benefits of faster settlement, lower costs and markets that never close become clear to investors and institutions, this shift will be very difficult to reverse, regardless of who wins the White House in 2028."

Near-term, investors should expect more volatility as the market tries to figure out how crypto regulation may shift under a Democratic Congress.

The impact of the blue wave "should vary considerably across assets," said James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares. "We would expect U.S. crypto equities to be more sensitive than bitcoin, particularly businesses reliant on new regulatory permissions. Established, compliant operators could even benefit from greater scrutiny of competitors. So miners who already have data centers set up are likely to remain in high demand by AI hyperscalers."

— CNBC's Talia Kaplan contributed reporting.

#2831. White House vows to press ahead with tariffs despite midterm backlash over rising prices

One week after voters punished Republicans over rising prices in the midterm elections, President Donald Trump's White House signaled it would stay the course on tariffs, even as his former top economic advisor warned that Americans are footing the bill.

Jim Goyer, Trump's director of White House Office of Public Liaison, defended tariffs as a tool to rebuild U.S. manufacturing, secure supply chains and protect national security during an address Tuesday at the B20 summit, a gathering of global business leaders in Washington hosted by the U.S. Chamber of Commerce.

"And we are just getting started," Goyer said. "Our ambition is to continue this pace for the remainder of the president's term in office."

But Gary Cohn, who served as Trump's chief economic advisor during his first administration, offered a sharply different assessment during an onstage interview later Tuesday.

"Tariffs are a consumption tax on the U.S. consumer," Cohn said, arguing that the duties are raising prices and forcing Americans to reconsider purchases.

The divide exposed a growing dilemma for Trump. His trade agenda is reshaping global commerce – and driving up costs for voters. Companies are adapting to the chaos, but the old trading system isn't coming back.

"President Trump has disrupted that system in a way that we're not likely to return to," Kevin Brady, a former Republican congressman who helped lead the B20 summit, said during a discussion with U.S. Chamber CEO Suzanne Clark.

The political backlash was evident in the Nov. 3 election. Roughly two-thirds of voters said Trump's policies contributed to inflation, according to the CNBC Voter Poll. Nearly nine in 10 said the cost of living was a major or crucial factor in their vote.

A separate Reuters/Ipsos poll released Tuesday found 62% of Americans supported repealing Trump's tariffs, while just 21% approved of his handling of the cost of living.

"Last week, American voters sent a clear message about the cost of living and their economic security," Clark said in her keynote address.

The pressure intensified Tuesday as new government data showed consumer prices rose 0.4% in October, exceeding economists' expectations. Annual inflation reached 3.7% in September, the latest available year-over-year figure and well above the Federal Reserve's 2% target.

Tariffs are not the only factor driving higher prices. The war with Iran has pushed energy costs sharply higher, while housing, healthcare and other household expenses continue to strain consumers.

But research has found Trump's import duties have added to those pressures. A Federal Reserve Bank of New York study estimated that about 26% of tariff increases were passed through to consumer prices, with some effects taking nine to 12 months to work through supply chains.

Cohn, now vice chairman of IBM, argued that while tariffs generate revenue for the U.S., those gains come at consumers' expense.

"You're looking at one side of the equation. You're looking at the credit side of the balance sheet," Cohn said, describing how he would respond to Trump's defense of tariff revenue. "There's the debit side of the balance sheet, which is every hardworking American who happened to pay the tariff through some product that they bought."

Still, Cohn saw room for a bipartisan trade deal. He suggested Trump and the incoming Democratic Congress could make protections against China and unfair trade practices permanent while rolling back tariffs on Canada, leaving less for future presidents to undo.

For businesses, the shift is already underway. UPS CEO Carol Tomé said customers are moving production and sourcing closer to home as global supply chains become more regional.

"Supply chains are diversifying. Supply chains are regionalizing," Tomé said during a panel discussion moderated by CNBC's Morgan Brennan.

Separately, UPS is opening a new $80 million-plus air hub in the Philippines in October to expand its Asian network and improve connections across more than 1,100 global trade lanes.

Other executives, like Jim Fitterling, executive chairman of chemicals giant Dow, defended Trump's tariffs despite their costs. Fitterling acknowledged they can drive up prices but argued they are necessary to combat unfair competition and protect U.S. manufacturing.

Democrats plan to challenge Trump's tariffs when they take control of Congress in January, but his veto power and broad trade authority could make them difficult to overturn.

#2830. 2027 South Side Sox Top Prospect No. 90: Carson Jacobs

Carson JacobsRight-Handed Relief Pitcher6´9´´215 poundsAge: 262025 South Side Sox Top 100 Prospect Ranking 712026 South Side Sox Top Prospect Ranking 862026 High Level Birmingham (AA)Age relative to high level -0.6 yearsSSS rank among all right-handed relievers in the system 15Overall 2026 season stats 2-2 ⚾️ 32 games (7 finishes) ⚾️ 43 2/3 IP ⚾️ 3.71.ERA ⚾️ 62 K ⚾️ 43 BB ⚾️ 1.695 WHIP ⚾️ 0.7 WAR

If you're jumping center against 6´9´´behemoth Carson Jacobs, you know what you're in for. Facing him in the batter's box, against a reach that releases the ball close enough to tickle your chin, is another matter entirely.

Jacobs has used his height to great advantage in his year-and-change in the White Sox system. The White Sox ignored his stats at the baseball hotbed of North Dakota State University in 2023 (2-5, 34 innings, 6.62 ERA) and snapped him up immediately after the draft as a UDFA. With no inhibitions, the club put the tall righty right to work, and its confidence paid off handsomely, as Jacobs busted out 6 1⁄3 innings while allowing just one earned run.

Despite that small sample size greatness, it's only fair the White Sox packed Jacobs back up for a repeat ACL tour to start 2024 — and it was only fair Jacobs murdered ACL batters once more (3.22 ERA in 22 1⁄3 innings, plus his first pro save). From there, Jacobs did time in both Kannapolis and Winston-Salem, ending the year with a sparkling 2.37 ERA over 31 games. With 19 of those games being finishes, it's clear the White Sox want to slot Jacobs in as a setup/closer short man.

While Jacobs had been killing it through his first two summers with the Sox, 2025 turned out to be another matter entirely. The righty's peripherals held steady, except for one key spot: Hitters caught up to him, with a dramatic increase in hits per nine (5.7 H/9 in 2024, up to 8.4). That's a worrying sign, as Jacobs was age-appropriate for High-A, where he pitched all but two of his games.

One bright spot for the reliever is that the White Sox thought highly enough of him in spite of a rough 2025 to send him on an audition to the Arizona Fall League. There, Jacobs saw action in eight games to a 4.50 ERA — and he was lucky to get away with just that, having walked 11 batters for a 16.5 BB/9.

Jacobs tasted Double-A for two games in 2025, and jumped right back to Double-A for 2026. And, back with the Barons, he was used somewhat sparingly. He never spent time on the IL, at least officially, but didn't even make his season debut until the end of April. And in spite of relieving all year, his rest between appearances often more closely resembled a rotation arm, sitting for upwards of a week between games.

When he was on the mound, Jacobs was stingier with contact, but offset that with a wild streak that raised his overall WHIP. Bottom line, though, Jacobs improved his ERA, with run prevention that should see him get time in Charlotte in 2027, if not when camp breaks then soon after.
