#2828. CNBC's experts on whether the AI trade is ending and what investors should do next

Amid a powerful rout in tech stocks, investors told CNBC on Thursday that something may be fundamentally breaking in the AI trade following the big success of Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections and the threat they try to slow the data center buildout.

Democrats are more aggressively anti-data center than Republicans, and Wall Street is quickly redoing its math what any restrictions they implement could mean for the related companies' future growth.

"Right now, investors are questioning that $1.2 trillion of capex that we forecast for next year," Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners, told CNBC. "Well, maybe that's not going to happen."

Boockvar believes this could be the start of the end of the AI trade as we know it.

Whether it's completely over or not, investors need to be reassessing how they're planning to play the AI trade going forward, the analysts said.

The Nasdaq Composite dropped 5% on Thursday and fell into correction territory, off by more than 11% from its record high set last month. The iShares Semiconductor ETF dropped 11% on Thursday, its biggest decline since during the Covid breakout in March 2020. The Global X Artificial Intelligence & Technology ETF (AIQ) is down 6% on the day and down 15% over the past month.

"There has been a huge change in the outlook for AI infrastructure buildout," Eric Johnston, head of equities at Cantor Fitzgerald, told CNBC. "The entire economy and the consumer and earnings growth are all levered to that buildout."

The selling Thursday spilled over to non-AI stocks with the Dow Jones Industrial average down more than 1,600 points.

Josh Brown pointed to the 4.8% yield for the 10-year Treasury yield, saying a compelling risk-free rate is causing investors to reconsider taking the risk in these volatile AI stocks.

"You don't need to be at 80% U.S. stocks and have 60% of that exposure be tech," Brown said.

Many investors believe the AI trade isn't over completely, investors just need to get more selective.

"The AI buildout just got harder," said Rob Sechan, Newedge Capital Group Founder & Managing Partner:

"Nobody voted to make compute less scarce," Sechan said, citing Microsoft's Azure cloud business growing 48% year over year in its most recent third quarter. That level of growth suggests its capacity constrained, not demand constrained, he said.

"When new capacity gets harder to build, existing capacity becomes more valuable," Sechan said.

Richard Clode, portfolio manager at Janus Henderson, noted a few bright spots in tech on Thursday despite the highly correlated downward movements in the big tech indices.

"Motorola Solutions had some good results," he said. "We're seeing a little bit of rotation into some of the … internet names...Sony reported some good results, and again they were up strongly overnight."

"So there are some brief pockets of respite from this, but obviously pretty painful across the board today," he said.

Some experts believe the selling was overdone and investors could start nibbling.

This "indiscriminate selling" is a "buying opportunity," said Larry Adam, Raymond James chief investment officer. If investors "take a longer term perspective, which is what we do, I think the valuations at today's levels will tend to prove to be a good buying opportunity," he said, urging investors to look at the fundamentals.

Victoria Greene, chief investment officer of G Squared Private Wealth, said she saw some strong supports around the S&P 500's 200-day moving average and was on the lookout for a market bounce following Thursday's rout.

"Typically, the average return is plus about 180 basis points the next day," she said. "The question we have to ask ourselves: Is this a pullback in an uptrend, which is what I believe? Or is this the end-all, be-all [and] the wheels are coming off and everybody just needs to buy T-bills? I don't think we're there yet."

#2827. Lakers ‘registered interest’ in Nique Clifford before trade to Thunder

On Sunday afternoon, the Sacramento Kings agreed to deal second-year wing Nique Clifford to the Oklahoma City Thunder in exchange for two second-round picks. Clifford held a fairly sizable role during his rookie season, when he averaged 8.6 points, 3.8 rebounds, 2.4 assists and 0.9 steals in 25.1 minutes per game.

But he'd fallen out of Sacramento's rotation to begin 2026-27 and played just 50 minutes in eight games prior to the trade. With the Thunder ravaged by early season injuries, they opted to tap into their surplus of picks and add a reinforcement to their wing room with the hopes he might break out in a more stable organization.

Oklahoma City, however, was not the lone playoff hopeful eyeing Clifford. Marc Stein and Jake Fischer reported that a trio of other suitors, including the Los Angeles Lakers, gauged the market for him. Ultimately, though, the Thunder emerged victorious in the Nique Clifford Sweepstakes.

"The Pacers, Nuggets and Lakers, sources say, were among the teams that registered trade interest in the 24-year-old," according to The Stein Line Substack, "but Oklahoma City had multiple advantages when it came to getting a deal done."

Among the "multiple advantages" referenced there is presumably Oklahoma City's wealth of picks. Between 2027 and 2033, it owns 15 second-round picks, one of which is Los Angeles' 2032 second-rounder. For comparison, the Lakers own just three over that same span.

Plus, the Lakers are also already fairly well-stocked with youthful wings worth taking a swing on this season. Adou Thiero (22 years old), Cameron Carr (22) and Ziaire Williams (25) are all 25 or younger and have garnered varying levels of rotation minutes to open the year. Given the Lakers' depleted well of draft capital, it probably didn't make much sense to fork over two of their remaining three second-round picks through 2033 for a dude who *might* crack the rotation on certain nights.

If they're going to move some of those picks, it should be for someone they know can be a difference-maker and mainstay in the rotation. Clifford, while still pretty young and far from a finished product in the league, certainly does not fit that billing.

#2824. Anthropic opens Australian retail IPO offering as AI scrutiny intensifies

Anthropic, the maker of AI chatbot Claude, opened its Australian retail initial public offering on Wednesday, giving individual investors access to shares ahead of the company's planned Nasdaq listing.

The offering opened at 10 a.m. Australian Eastern Daylight Time on Wednesday and is expected to close at 5 p.m. on Nov. 17, according to the timetable published by participating broker Stake.

Anthropic is seeking to raise as much as $64.4 billion in its IPO, with a separate $26 billion private placement, bringing a combined total to as much as $90.4 billion. Shares are expected to be priced between $70 to $78 each.

The Australian retail offering is expected to consist of newly issued Class A common shares under a prospectus to be lodged with the Australian Securities and Investments Commission, Anthropic said on its website.

Participating brokers include Commonwealth Bank of Australia's CommSec unit as lead broker, along with Bell Potter, LGT Wealth, Macquarie Group, Morgans, Morgan Stanley Wealth Management, Sharesies and Stake.

Anthropic said the prospectus would be available on its IPO website once the Australian retail offering opens. Investors will need to review the document and apply through participating brokers under the terms of the offer.

Australia is one of the small numbers of markets outside the U.S. where retail investors will be able to buy the company's shares directly, alongside Japan, Canada and parts of Europe.

The San Francisco-based AI startup has applied to list its Class A common stock on the Nasdaq Global Select Market under the ticker symbol "ANT." Anthropic said it does not plan to seek a listing on the Australian Securities Exchange.

The offering comes as Australia moves to tighten oversight of AI companies after an OpenAI agent gained unauthorized access to the medical statistics website of Medicare,  the country's universal health insurance program.

Prime Minister Anthony Albanese said Tuesday that the government would introduce mandatory reporting for certain AI-related incidents and establish an AI safety regulator with powers to enforce penalties.

"The law needs to get ahead of, and in some cases, make sure that it catches up with what is a very fast-changing world," Albanese told Reuters.

The government is also considering changes to criminal law for cases involving serious misconduct.

The country has also ruled out a copyright-law exemption that would allow AI companies to train models on Australian content without licensing it.

Open AI and Anthropic told Australian parliament last month that they would support mandatory reporting of data breaches involving their AI agents, acknowledging that disclosure is currently voluntary.
